# Phazite design

*Status: draft for review, October 2026.* This document describes the product Phazite is aiming for, and why. The plan for building it is in [roadmap.md](roadmap.md). **Decided** marks choices the project owner has made; **Open** marks questions that still need a decision ([section 25](#25-open-questions)). The research behind the design is summarized in [appendix A](#appendix-a-research-summary), with sources in [appendix B](#appendix-b-sources). How the code is structured is in [section 24](#24-code-structure).

## 1. Vision and scope

Phazite is an open-source (AGPL-3.0-or-later) digital audio workstation with its own song model and playback engine, built on JUCE, for Windows first and then Linux and macOS. It aims to be:

- **Quick to start:** sound within seconds of opening it. Song outlines and chord progressions show newcomers what to do next.
- **Deep when needed:** professional routing, automation, modulation and mixing are one click away, never hidden behind a mode.
- **Fast and lean:** low CPU and memory use, in both the audio engine and the UI.
- **Smart in context:** AI and generative features that are useful *because* they run inside a project. They know its key, chords, sections and sounds.
- **Local and open:** no account, no cloud requirement, and projects that stay on your machine.

In one line: **FL Studio's speed without FL Studio's split.** Phazite keeps the playlist, piano roll and mixer workflow that electronic producers love, and drops the channel rack and unlinked mixer that they complain about most ([appendix A](#appendix-a-research-summary)).

**v1 scope (decided):**
- the playlist, piano roll and mixer;
- built-in instruments and effects for electronic music;
- automation and modulation;
- song outlines and chord progressions;
- VST®3 hosting on Windows.

Chord suggestions and the drum sound generator, first planned for v1, moved after it (the owner, 2026-11-04). Versions of a project, as git's commits and branches, with DAWproject import and export, were built ahead of v1 ([versions.md](versions.md), 2026-11-04 and 2026-11-05). [Section 25](#25-open-questions) and the [roadmap](roadmap.md#after-v1) list what waits until after v1.

## 2. Who Phazite is for

| | Who | What they need |
|---|---|---|
| **Primary** | Electronic and beat producers coming from FL Studio or Ableton Live, mostly on Windows | Fast clip editing by hotkey, a great piano roll, free routing, automation on everything, many third-party plug-ins (such as Serum and OTT) |
| **Secondary** | Newcomers making their first tracks | Sound immediately, guidance on song structure and chords, plain language, nothing hidden |
| **Later** | Recording musicians and mix engineers | Audio recording, takes and comping, deeper metering, project interchange |

Why this audience:
- **Professionals:** surveys of professionals put Pro Tools first, with about 37% of music and 64% of post-production picks in Production Expert's 2025 survey. FL Studio gets about 1% there, but that audience is mostly engineers ([B1](#appendix-b-sources), [B2](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **Electronic producers:** an academic survey found that Live and FL Studio lead among amateur electronic producers, and Live and Pro Tools among professionals ([B3](#appendix-b-sources)). FL Studio users are the youngest and least formally trained, and 83% use Windows.

"Professional" for Phazite's audience therefore means routing, automation, mixing and plug-in depth, not film post-production.

## 3. Design principles

1. **One track, one channel (decided).** Every track is a playlist row, a device chain and a mixer channel, created together with the same name and color. There is no channel rack. Free routing stays available through sends, buses and sidechains. *Why:* FL Studio's split between channel rack, patterns, playlist and mixer is its most repeated complaint, and Image-Line itself is moving to a 1:1 "Track Mode" ([B4](#appendix-b-sources), [B5](#appendix-b-sources)).
2. **One window.** Panels dock around the playlist, any panel can move to a second monitor, and layouts are saved. No pile of floating windows.
3. **No skill-level modes.** There is no beginner/pro switch. Depth appears in place: in the inspector, in context menus, in the command palette. *Why:* Apple removed Logic's "Complete Features" switch in Logic 12 after years of confusion ([B6](#appendix-b-sources), [B7](#appendix-b-sources)).
4. **Every action is a command.** Each action has a name, a description and a default shortcut. It can be searched, rebound, chained into macros, and later called by scripts or an assistant.
5. **Opinionated defaults.** What Phazite offers sounds good and looks finished: its devices' defaults, its presets and its example songs, which make sound at once. Customizing is optional, never required. *Why:* REAPER's blank first screen and Waveform's "eccentric" one are the most common complaints about each ([B8](#appendix-b-sources), [B9](#appendix-b-sources)). A new project itself starts empty (the owner's choice, 2026-11-01), with the example songs and the lessons a click away ([section 13](#13-getting-started-and-song-outlines)).
6. **Help where you are.** A hint bar says what the mouse and modifier keys will do under the pointer, and hovering any control explains it.
7. **Nothing invisible.** Routing is drawn, linked clips are marked, automation and modulation show on the controls they move, and each track shows its CPU use.
8. **Plain names.** Track, clip, folder, section, device, send. No private vocabulary.
9. **Stable by default.** Autosave and crash recovery from the first release; a crashing plug-in should never cost the user their work ([section 19](#19-plug-in-hosting-and-stability)).
10. **Fast by default.** Strict real-time rules on the audio thread, a UI that does nothing when nothing changes, quick startup ([section 20](#20-performance)).
11. **Local, private, licence-clean.** No account, no personal data in project files, and only AGPL-compatible code, models and data ([section 22](#22-privacy-licences-and-trademarks)).
12. **Accessible.** Everything works from the keyboard, screen readers work on Windows and macOS, and color is never the only signal ([section 21](#21-accessibility)).

## 4. The window

```
+-----------------------------------------------------------------------+
| File Edit Add View  My Song *  [<-][->][ Search commands ] [|][_][|][#] [-][o][x] |
+-----------------------------------------------------------------------+
| Play  Rec   140 BPM   4/4                   master level   keyboard   |
+----------+--------------------------------------------+---------------+
| Browser  | [Playlist] [Mixer]      (the whole song)   | Inspector     |
| (Ctrl+F) | + Track  Intro    Build    Drop    (bars)  | (properties   |
| folders  | v Drums  (folder = bus)                    |  of what is   |
| tags     |     Kick    [] [] [] [] [] [] [] []        |  selected)    |
| plug-ins | v Synths                                   |               |
| samples  |     Lead    [riff ] [riff*] [riff*] *alias |               |
| presets  |       `- Cutoff lane   /\/\/\_/\           |               |
|          +--------------------------------------------+               |
|          | [Piano roll] [Instrument] [Effects]        |               |
+----------+--------------------------------------------+---------------+
| Saved | hint: what the mouse and keys do here | 48 kHz . 10 ms | CPU 12% |
+-----------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

**The whole song in the centre, the selection below (the owner's decision, 2026-10-17).** The owner found that the editor dock mixed one clip's editor with every track's mixer, that the inspector showed only a copy of a mixer channel, and that effects needed a place to be edited in line, as notes are in the piano roll. Seven DAWs' layouts (Appendix A) showed that the bottom of the window is for the selection in all of them; four also let the mixer take that place (Bitwig, Studio Pro, Logic Pro, Cubase), while Ableton Live keeps it for the selection and gives the mixer a main view of its own, as Bitwig's Mix view does with its Device panel below; an in-line view of the selected track's devices is the clearest trend (Ableton's Device View and Bitwig's Device panel for years, Studio Pro 8's Channel Overview and Reason 14's Track Panel since 2025-26); and side inspectors show properties of the selection.
- **Title bar (2026-10-21, as the owner asked: "integrating with the top action bar smoothly like most apps", with VS Code's command search in the middle and Undo and Redo to its left; decided by Claude for the owner to review, `src/ui/shell/TitleBar.h`):** Phazite's logo (since 2026-11-06, where VS Code shows its own: [design language](#design-language)), the menus and the project's name (a dot after it while it has unsaved changes) on the left; Undo and Redo, which name the step they take, beside the command search (Ctrl+K) in the middle of the window; on the right, a switch for the browser, the editor dock and the inspector, lit while each shows, as VS Code's layout controls, and a Layout menu (the layouts, each panel, Maximize the Panel in Use, Full Screen); then the window's own minimize, maximize and close buttons. Its empty parts and the project's name drag the window, a double-click on them maximizes it, and Windows 11's snap layouts open from the maximize button; a restored window resizes from a band along its sides, which the panels keep out of so that their scroll bars there can be dragged. In a narrow window the menus fold into one button, then the name goes, then the search narrows. F10 opens the menus. In full screen the bar stays, without the window's buttons. **Platforms:** on Windows it is the window's title bar (JUCE tells Windows which parts are the caption and which the buttons); on Linux the system's title bar stays above it, as nothing in it needs the window's frame, and a switch to use Phazite's own there, as on Windows, can come later (JUCE removes the decorations and moves the window through the window manager); on macOS the system's title bar and its traffic lights stay, with the menus in the menu bar at the top of the screen, and a small piece of native code (a full-size content view under a transparent title bar) can put the bar beside the traffic lights later.
- **Transport bar:** play, loop and record, tempo, time signature, the master level and volume, and the computer keyboard, in a row right under the title bar's controls, as much room under it as shows above it (2026-11-07, the owner: it "looks like it has more padding on the top than the bottom"). A key is a track's, so the piano roll shows it, not the transport bar (2026-10-13).
  - **What a tempo change stretches (2026-10-28, as the owner asked: "make it obvious to the user when they change tempo, what is going to be stretched", with samples, patterns, both or neither; `src/ui/shell/TempoStretchPanel.h`):** as soon as the tempo field is dragged, scrolled or typed in, a panel under it says how many of the song's samples follow the tempo and whether its patterns do, with a switch for each (the commands Samples Follow Tempo and Patterns Follow Tempo); it goes a few seconds after the tempo stops changing. In the playlist, a sample that follows the tempo shows a metronome at the end of its name, as FL Studio marks a stretched clip. A stretched sample plays faster or slower with the tempo, from Phazite's render; patterns follow by default, their notes on their beats; whatever isn't stretched keeps its speed, and every clip still starts on the same beat. No DAW found asks at each tempo change: each keeps the choice in settings, per clip (Ableton Live's Warp, Logic Pro's Flex & Follow, Cubase's Musical Mode, Waveform's Auto-Tempo), per track (Studio One's Don't Follow, Follow and Timestretch, Cubase's musical or linear time base) or per project with overrides (REAPER's timebase) ([B76](#appendix-b-sources), [B106](#appendix-b-sources)-[B110](#appendix-b-sources)). So Phazite keeps them as settings too, Samples Follow Tempo switching every sample's own Follow tempo at once, and shows them where the tempo changes rather than in a dialog that would stop each drag.
  - **Tempo changes along the song (2026-11-08, the owner: "i want you to implement all of the reccomended system for the tempo work", after a survey of how the popular DAWs do it; `src/ui/playlist/TempoLane.h`, `src/model/transport/TempoModel.h`):** the tempo field shows the tempo where the playhead is, and changing it changes the tempo in effect there, so a song of one tempo works as it always did and there is no override mode. A tempo change is added at the playhead (Add > Add Tempo Change, `transport.addTempoChange`) or where the time bar is right-clicked (Add Tempo Change Here); from then on, a tempo lane shows under the time bar, over the chord lane, until Remove Tempo Changes leaves the first tempo all the way through, as other DAWs show their tempo tracks only once the tempo changes. The lane draws the tempo as the engine plays it, with a point where each tempo starts and its value beside it, and takes the automation lanes' gestures: a click adds a tempo change at the tempo clicked, a press on the line pulls one from it, a point drags on the grid (Alt places it freely, Shift moves it in tenths), the first up and down only; right-click a line to make it ramp or hold, the handle in a ramp's middle bends it, and a double-click deletes a point or straightens a ramp. Each tempo holds until the next and jumps to it, as tempo changes start, or ramps to it, straight or bent as an automation lane's line bends (`TempoChange::curve`, `TempoValues.h`; `engine/core/TempoMap.h`, which plays a ramp exactly and a bent one in straight pieces a sixteenth of a beat long). Samples that follow the tempo stretch along its changes, ramps included, each beat of their audio on a beat of the song, from a render whose stretch follows a time map (Rubber Band's R3 in its real-time mode, its stretch set at each of its hops from where its output has come to, so that it lands on the map: within half a millisecond of each beat through a ritardando, and as close as at one tempo through a ramp that doubles the stretch); those that keep their speed keep it. Patterns keep their beats along the changes: Patterns Follow Tempo, off, keeps their speed only while the song has one tempo, as a note has no time of its own between two tempos. A MIDI file whose tempo changes brings them into a song with neither clips nor tempo changes yet, and a DAWproject carries them both ways, ramps as straight lines and bent ones as straight pieces along them. With nothing selected, the inspector shows how far the tempo goes ("50 to 260 BPM"). Tempo detection from audio and tap tempo wait until after v1.
- **Browser (left):** instruments, effects, plug-ins, presets, samples, generated sounds and projects ([section 11](#11-the-browser)).
- **The Project view (left; 2026-11-06, the owner: "add the Project view to the left wing with it's own icon where you suggested", open question 22; `src/ui/project/ProjectView.h`):** what the song holds, as Bitwig's Project panel shows it ([B135](#appendix-b-sources)), its icon (Lucide's "clipboard-list") between the browser's and source control's on the activity bar, in three panes that fold, as VS Code's side bar lays out a view of panes:
  - **Notes,** kept in the song and saved with it: typed in place, each burst of typing one undo step, "Change Notes" (it ends at a pause of a second and a half, or when the keys or the mouse go elsewhere). Its icon gives the view the keys, not the notes, so that the keys' shortcuts work on until the notes are clicked;
  - **Audio Files:** each audio file the song plays (its audio clips' and its devices' sound files: a drum sampler's pads, a wavetable synth's tables), with the tracks that play it, the header counting them and saying how many are missing. A missing one shows in the warning colour, so said, with its Locate... (a magnifier, under the pointer, or a double-click): the file chosen, copied into the project's Audio folder as an import copies it, takes its place everywhere the song plays it, as one undo step, "Locate Audio File". Under the list, while the project's folder has audio files nothing plays (no version either), how many, with **Clean Up...**, which runs Clean Up Unused Audio: one way to do it, now where its files show;
  - **Plug-ins:** each plug-in the song holds, on its track or the master, the header saying how many crashed, aren't installed or are off. A crashed one has its Reload (an arrow, under the pointer); a click on one selects it and shows it in the Devices view.
  It reads the song again once a turn after it changes, and every five seconds while it shows, for files that came or went on the disk; it repaints only when what it shows changed.
- **Source Control (left; built 2026-11-04 and 2026-11-05, [versions.md](versions.md); `src/ui/versions/VersionsView.h`):** the project's versions as git's commits and branches, in git's words as VS Code's Source Control view says them (the owner, 2026-11-05), its icon (Lucide's "git-branch") the third on the activity bar: the message and Commit, the staged changes and the changes since the last commit, by track, the graph of every branch's commits, branches made, switched in place and merged, a commit restored, and commits' bounces heard in place of the song; DAWproject export and import beside them.
- **Centre: the whole song,** in one of two views, chosen by the tabs at its top, so that no key needs remembering (the owner asked for as few "magic key presses" as possible; F5 and F9 do the same, as in FL Studio):
  - **Playlist:** the time bar (the song's sections over the bar numbers, with the button that adds a track beside it), then the chord lane while the song has chords ([section 14](#14-chords)), then the tracks, with the sections drawn faintly behind them ([section 6](#6-the-playlist));
  - **Mixer:** every channel side by side ([section 8](#8-the-mixer)).
- **Editor dock (bottom): the selection, in detail,** in tabs:
  - **The selected clip's editor** (F7), its tab named for what it shows: the **piano roll** for MIDI clips, and the **audio clip editor** for audio clips (2026-10-17, [section 7](#the-audio-clip-editor)). It follows the selection, as Studio Pro's Edit view opens the Audio Editor for audio events and the Note Editor for instrument parts ([B81](#appendix-b-sources)): MIDI clips selected show in the piano roll, audio clips in the audio clip editor, and a selected track in the editor for what it holds; double-clicking a clip shows it here;
  - **Instrument** (F6) and **Effects** (Shift+F6): the selected track's instrument apart, and its effects one under another down a column, as FL Studio's mixer stacks its slots, the one clicked showing beside them in the rest of the dock ([section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets); since 2026-10-21, as the owner asked: the instrument looked like another effect, and a long chain ran off sideways; stacked since 2026-11-06).
  Automation has no tab: it is edited in its lanes, under its tracks in the playlist (2026-10-17, [section 10](#10-automation-and-modulation)).
- **Views move between the two (2026-11-01, as the owner asked: "the user should be able to drag the mixer tab down into the bottom panel, or the piano roll up, etc."; `src/ui/shell/Panels.h`):** each of the four views, the playlist, the mixer, the clip editor and the devices (whose Instrument and Effects tabs are pages of one view, so they move together), can be in either area: its tab dragged onto the other area, which tints and shows a line where the tab will go, as VS Code's views move between its side bars and its panel, or moved there from the tab's right-click menu; dragged along its own area, a tab changes places with the others. The settings keep where each view is, and **View > Reset View Places** (also in a tab's menu) puts them back. The two areas go by where they are, the **Upper Panel** and the **Lower Panel**, in their switches and commands; one left without views hides, and the other fills the centre. **Side by side (2026-11-03, as the owner asked: "drag a window ... to one side of the window space and have it tile next to the other window horizontally ... the max would be a 2x2 grid in the center"):** each panel is a row that holds one area or two side by side, so the centre holds at most four, two by two, between the browser and the inspector. A tab dragged onto an area's left or right quarter goes into an area of its own beside it (the half it would take tinted and outlined), while its row has one area; onto the middle or the tabs, among the area's tabs, as before. The line between two areas drags to share the row out (each at least 240 pixels), and the settings keep where the line is. A row's buttons sit in its right area's corner. **A row left without views can be filled again (2026-11-03, the owner: it was then out of reach, and its switch did nothing):** a tab dragged onto an area's edge toward it, its lower quarter in the upper row or its upper quarter in the lower one, goes there; and the row's switch (Show the Lower Panel, say) brings its own views back to it, as they start, the clip editor and the devices for the lower one. F5, F6, F7 and F9 show a view wherever it is; in the lower panel, F6, F7 and F9 hide the panel when it shows what they show, as F7 always did there. A tab drags only within the main window: the lower panel in its own window moves its views from their menus. No DAW checked lets a view move between its main area and its lower one (Cubase's Lower Zone has fixed tabs, Bitwig's panels are fixed in each view, Ableton stacks Clip View and Device View at the bottom); the pattern is VS Code's and JetBrains' ([B121](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **Inspector (right): the properties of what is selected,** as Bitwig's, Studio Pro's, Logic Pro's and Cubase's inspectors show them, laid out to use its width: a track's name, color, volume, pan, where its sound goes and its sends; a clip's name, place, length, mute, fades and whether it is an alias; a section's name, color, place, length, energy and tip; and, with nothing selected, the song's tempo, time signature and length. A track's also shows its instrument, its effects and its automation mode, each a click from the Devices view or its lanes. It isn't a copy of a mixer channel, which the mixer already shows. **What is clicked, changed there (2026-11-02, the owner: "could it show more contextual information for whatever you click on?"):** notes selected in the piano roll show their start, length, pitch, velocity and mute; automation points, their parameter, position, value and bend; clips, their start and length, and audio clips their gain and pitch, as fields to drag, scroll, step or type in. Several notes, clips or points show together, as Logic Pro's region inspector and Bitwig's note inspector (which reviewers praise) edit several: a value shows the range of theirs while they differ ("64 to 100"); dragged, scrolled or stepped, each changes by as much (notes and clips moved and transposed together as far as all can go, so a chord keeps its shape); typed, each length, velocity, gain and value becomes it, and the earliest start and the lowest pitch go there, the others with them. No key combinations: Logic Pro's Option-Shift for one value for all is typing here. A drag is one undo step. Users' complaints about inspectors are long scrolling sections (Cubase) and controls too small to find (Logic Pro), so each page shows only what the selection has, a row each. A sound in the browser isn't in the song, and no DAW's inspector shows one: the browser shows it under its tree ([section 11](#11-the-browser)).
- **Status bar (bottom edge, 2026-10-21, as VS Code's: the owner asked for "small infos" there, beside the hints; `src/ui/shell/StatusBar.h`):** on the left, whether the project is saved ("Saved", "Unsaved changes", "Not saved yet"; a click saves), what is selected ("Bass", "3 clips", "16 notes", "Bars 5 to 8") and where what is played on a MIDI keyboard or the computer keyboard goes ("MIDI → Bass"; a click opens the MIDI settings); in the middle, the hint bar: what clicking, dragging and the modifier keys do under the pointer, the guide's next step, and alerts such as no audio output; on the right, while clips are being stretched again in the background, "Stretching 2 clips" with a bar of how far that has come (2026-10-29, as the owner asked: the clips are silent until then); the song's delay compensation while it has some ("Delay comp. 5.3 ms", naming the devices that look ahead; a click opens the audio settings); the audio output's sample rate and delay ("48 kHz · 10 ms", Bluetooth's in the warning colour; a click opens the audio settings) and how busy the engine is (CPU, moved from the top bar, in the warning colour near its limit). Each says more under the pointer. In a narrow window the items leave the hints room, what is selected going first (2026-10-29, decided by Claude for the owner to review).

**Panels and layouts:**
- Panels can be resized, hidden, maximized and detached to another monitor (2026-10-21, as the owner asked: "options to hide and also an option to fullscreen only that panel", for the browser, the playlist, the inspector and the editor dock; decided by Claude for the owner to review, from VS Code's panels and Ableton Live's View menu, `src/ui/shell/PanelLayout.h`):
  - **the lower panel's top right corner** has Maximize, its own window and Hide, an X as VS Code's panel's (2026-11-05, the owner: the side panels and the upper panel need none of these buttons, and one X reads the same everywhere where a hide icon turned for each panel didn't), which are commands with names that say what they do now ("Hide the Lower Panel", "Show the Lower Panel"), in the View menu too; **the inspector has the same X** at the right end of its title, which stops short of it (2026-11-07, the owner: "the right wing panel should also have an "x" to hide it, like the bottom panel has"), and none while it is in a window of its own, whose close button puts it back; every panel's commands stay in the View menu, the Layout menu and the command search, and the left panel hides by its activity bar icon or title bar switch, the inspector by its switch too;
  - **a divider dragged far enough closes its panel (2026-11-05, the owner: "like vs code"):** past half the least the panel shrinks to, as VS Code's sashes snap its side bar and panel shut; dragged back before the drag ends, the panel opens again, and closed, it opens later at the size it had before that drag. The divider between the upper and the lower panel closes either, the other then filling the centre. **A double-click on a divider** puts the panel beside it back at the size it starts at (the side panel's width, the lower panel's share of the height, or half each for two areas side by side), as a double-click on VS Code's sashes does;
  - **maximized,** a panel fills the window between the transport bar and the status bar, and one at a time; its first button, its command again or **Maximize the Panel in Use** (Shift+F11; the panel with the keyboard focus) brings back the others as they were. Asking to see another panel (its key, F5, F6, F7 or F9, a double-click on a clip, Ctrl+F) restores them first. A panel in a window of its own isn't maximized;
  - **the centre always shows the playlist and mixer, the editor dock or both:** hiding the one that shows shows the other, and the playlist stays while the editor dock is in a window of its own;
  - **sizes are kept:** a panel hidden and shown again, or the others brought back from a maximized one, come back at the width or height their dividers were dragged to, and the window opens with its panels as they were left (the settings keep them; a detached panel comes back docked);
  - **nothing plays out of sight:** a sound the browser previews stops as the browser hides or another panel fills the window;
  - **Full Screen** (F11) fills the whole screen with the window, over the taskbar.
  Keys, in Phazite's keymap: Ctrl+Alt+B the browser (Ableton Live's), Ctrl+Alt+L the editor dock and Ctrl+Alt+I the inspector (VS Code's Ctrl+B and Ctrl+J are Duplicate and Consolidate in DAWs), Shift+F11 and F11; in FL Studio's, F12 maximizes the playlist and mixer, as its F12 closes every window but the playlist.
- A layout saves the arrangement of panels. Three ship by default, and they are layouts, not modes:
  - *Beat:* playlist and piano roll;
  - *Mix:* the mixer, the selected track's devices and the inspector;
  - *Full.*
- Function keys follow FL Studio where that makes sense: F5 playlist, F6 the instrument (Shift+F6 the effects), F7 piano roll, F8 plug-in picker, F9 mixer. The browser shows and hides with Ctrl+Alt+B (Ableton Live's key; Alt+F8 in FL Studio's keymap).

### Design language

Phazite's look should be as clean and consistent as its workflow. Its design language has five parts. Typography is decided; [roadmap](roadmap.md) step 0.6 defines the rest as reusable design tokens (named colors, sizes and spacings that every component uses).

**Typography (decided): Inter**, an open-source typeface designed for screens ([B35](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **Why bundle a font:** text looks the same and takes the same space on Windows, Linux and macOS, so labels fit everywhere. The DAWs that could be checked do this:
  - Ableton ships its own "AbletonSans" fonts with Live ([B36](#appendix-b-sources));
  - REAPER's default theme switched to Inter "as default font on all platforms" in version 7.70 ([B37](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **Licence:** Inter uses the SIL Open Font License 1.1.
  - The OFL explicitly allows bundling with any software, and the software doesn't have to adopt the font's licence ([B38](#appendix-b-sources)).
  - The GNU project lists it as a free license for fonts ([B39](#appendix-b-sources)).
  - Phazite ships the font files next to the app rather than compiling them in, together with their licence text and copyright notice.
- **Numbers:** tempo, time, levels and meter readouts use Inter's fixed-width (tabular) digits, so values don't jitter as they change. JUCE can switch this OpenType feature on (`FontOptions::withFeatureEnabled`).
- **Scale:** a small set of sizes and three weights (regular, medium and semibold), scaled with the UI.
- **Fallback:** if the font files are missing, Phazite uses the system font.

**The rest of the design language:**
- **Color:**
  - semantic tokens (background, surface, text, accent, warning) instead of hard-coded colors;
  - a dark theme first, then light and high-contrast themes;
  - a track-color palette that stays distinguishable for color-blind users;
  - what pops up over the window (menus, tooltips and the lists that drop down: the command search, the pickers) on frosted glass, as VS Code's is (2026-11-05, as the owner asked): what is behind blurred as CSS's `blur(12px)` blurs, under the popup's colour at 80 % (`Popup::frostTint`). For that, they open inside the main window, as VS Code's open inside its own, and reach no further than its edges. Solid in the high-contrast theme, where the system is set to reduce transparency, and in Phazite's other windows (`src/ui/theme/Popups.h`). An editor in a call-out (a modulator's) stays opaque.
- **VS Code's look (decided 2026-11-05, as the owner asked: "audit how their ui looks ... and then i want it all implemented"):** the dark and light themes are VS Code's 2026 Dark and 2026 Light, colour for colour, and the window is laid out as its Modern UI lays out its own: each panel a card with rounded corners, 4 pixels apart on the window's frame, the activity bar joined to the left panel, VS Code's title bar, status bar, activity bar, tabs, buttons, fields, scroll bars, lists, menus, command search and tooltips, and its text sizes (13 px body, 12 px labels). VS Code's source is under the MIT licence, so its values may be copied with its notice, which ships next to the app; its name, its logo, its icons (CC BY 4.0) and Windows' Segoe UI aren't taken: Phazite keeps Inter and Lucide. The high-contrast theme stays Phazite's own. What was taken, from where, and where Phazite differs (its frosted glass is taken as a popup opens, not on every frame): [vscode-look.md](vscode-look.md).
- **Spacing and sizing:** a 4-pixel grid, standard control heights and consistent padding.
- **One on/off switch (2026-11-06, the owner: "all ON switches that we have right now should be unified to the same green toggle item that is used in the track lanes"):** whatever switches on and off has the track's switch: a round light, filled in the playing colour (green) when on, a hollow ring when off (`ui::PowerSwitch`, `src/ui/components/PowerSwitch.h`, drawn as a track's `TrackSwitch` is). An effect's in the Devices view and in the mixer's slots, a modulator's in its editor, an EQ band's, and a part of an instrument at its title's right end (the synth's filter, filter envelope, LFO and unison, the wavetable's oscillators and filter), which dims the part while it is off. A choice of modes (the multiband's band modes, the compressor's characters) stays a row of buttons.
- **Scroll bars over what they scroll (2026-11-06, the owner: "make the vertical scroll bar only take up the space it needs, and also be semi transparent (with semitransparent track) and thinner"):** the playlist's and the piano roll's rows' scroll bars lie over the right edge of what they scroll, as macOS's overlay scroll bars lie over what they scroll; they show only while there is more than fits. Their time lines scroll by their overviews, the miniatures along their tops ([section 6](#6-the-playlist)). **Shown while they are wanted (2026-11-07, the owner: they should "fade away when the cursor is not hovering on the playlist for a while", be "a bit wider, and slightly more opaque, since its a little hard to see and drag"):** 10 pixels wide (6 until then), a thumb at 85 % of its colour (60 %) over a track of the text's colour at 8 % (6 %), shown while the pointer is over their editor (scrolling there with the wheel, or dragging the bar), and faded out 1.5 seconds after it leaves, as macOS's and Windows 11's overlay scroll bars hide when not in use; an editor scrolling itself doesn't show it, so a project that opens doesn't flash it; faded out, one still takes clicks where it is, and the pointer there shows it again. The overview's thumb shows the part in view more strongly too: its wash at 20 % of the text's colour (12 %), 30 % under the pointer (20 %), inside an edge at 55 % (30 %).
- **Icons (decided in step 0.6): [Lucide](https://lucide.dev)** ([B42](#appendix-b-sources)), line icons under the ISC licence (those taken from Feather: MIT), drawn in the theme's colors, plus custom icons drawn to match. Phazite copies in only the icons it uses (`resources/icons/lucide/`).
- **Logo and startup sound (2026-11-06, as the owner asked: a logo "top left like VS Code, and used in the app icon", and a sound when Phazite opens, "a blip with reverb"; drawn and made by Claude for the owner to review):**
  - the logo is Phazite's own drawing (`resources/logo/phazite.svg`): a hexagonal frame shaded as three faces of a cube, a pale cyan top, a sky-blue left and a steel-blue right, around a smaller cube in darker, blue-tinted faces lit from the same side, after a picture the owner chose; the hexagon suits a name that sounds like a mineral. It keeps its own colours in every theme. It is the app's icon (JUCE makes Windows' icon file from it as CMake configures; the plug-ins' helper has it too, so that Task Manager shows the helper as Phazite's), the window's icon on Linux, and at the title bar's left end where VS Code shows its own: 16 pixels in a 35-pixel slot, faint with the bar while another window is in front, gone in full screen, none on macOS ([vscode-look.md](vscode-look.md));
  - the startup sound is Phazite's own too, synthesized as it plays since no sounds go in this repository, as the metronome's click is (`src/model/engine/StartupSound.h`), after a recording the owner chose: a quick blip of six steps of 14 to 19 ms that zig-zag (a high line rising by fifths between a low line rising by whole tones, high first), each swelling and falling away, a tone with its third and a metallic partial at 5.4 times its pitch that ring out within the step; then echoes from Phazite's delay that bounce between the sides every 44 ms, each 2 dB quieter, without a room; 1.5 seconds in all, peaking at -6 dBFS. It plays once the project has opened (opening a project stops what the previews play), on the previews' engine at the browser's preview volume, and only through an audio output that opened. **Startup Sound**, in the gear's menu and the command search, turns it off; it is on at first, and turning it on plays it.
- **Motion:** short, purposeful animation (around 100–150 ms). None in places that redraw constantly, and the system's reduced-motion setting is respected.
- **Tokens a designer can edit (decided 2026-10-14):** the tokens move out of C++ into a file in the Design Tokens Community Group's format (DTCG 2025.10, [B56](#appendix-b-sources)), which a designer edits directly or exports from Figma, Penpot or Tokens Studio, and which the build turns into C++. A gallery app shows every control and view in all three themes, so a change can be seen without opening a project. A declarative layout tool (JIVE or foleys_gui_magic) comes only if dialogs become a bottleneck; the playlist, piano roll and meters stay painted in code. This is phase A4 of the [roadmap's architecture track](roadmap.md#architecture-track).

## 5. Building blocks

| Concept | What it is | In the song (`Schema.h`) |
|---|---|---|
| Project | A folder with the project file, its audio and its generated sounds | The song (`PHAZITE`), saved as the project file, in a folder (`model/project/internal/Project.h`) |
| Track | Playlist row, device chain and mixer channel in one | `TRACK`, one kind that holds audio and MIDI clips |
| Folder | A collapsible, colored group of tracks, and a bus that mixes them (its switch can make it plain) | A folder `TRACK` with the tracks in it, a bus or plain |
| Clip | Audio, MIDI or automation on a track | `CLIP` (a MIDI clip with its `NOTE`s, or an audio clip with the part of its file it plays), and `AUTOMATION` curves |
| Alias | A linked copy of a clip: edit one and all change; "Make unique" breaks the link | Clips sharing a `link` |
| Section | A named, colored part of the song (Intro, Drop) that spans all tracks | `SECTIONS`, with Phazite's own section edits |
| Chord lane | The song's chord progression | `CHORDLANE`, with its `CHORD`s and `CHORDKEY`s ([section 14](#14-chords)) |
| Key | A track's key and scale | `keyRoot` and `keyScale` on its `TRACK` |
| Device | An instrument or effect, built-in or plug-in | `DEVICE`: one of Phazite's own, or a plug-in hosted in a helper process |
| Container | A group of devices with macro knobs, saved as one preset | `CONTAINER`, with its devices and eight `MACRO`s |
| Send and return | A level-controlled feed to a return track | `SEND`, to a return `TRACK` |
| Modulator | An LFO, envelope follower, step sequence, random source, breakpoint envelope or note follower driving any parameter | `MODULATOR`, with what it `MODULATES` |
| Macro | One knob driving several parameters | `MACRO`, with what it `MODULATES` |

**Rules:**
- **Dropping creates tracks.** Drop an instrument, a sample or a MIDI file on empty playlist space and a new track appears. It is named after what was dropped and colored by type: for example, drums in one color family and synths in another.
- **Audio and notes don't share a track in v1.** Dropped audio goes on a new track next to the instrument's track. Mixing both on one row, like Bitwig's hybrid tracks, is a later option.
- **Drums without a channel rack.** Two ways are first-class, and neither uses step buttons:
  - one-shots placed straight onto their own tracks (a kick track, a snare track);
  - a drum-sampler track whose clips are edited in the piano roll's drum mode.
- **Reuse without patterns.** Aliases do the job of FL Studio's pattern reuse. "Duplicate section as aliases" repeats a whole part of the song.
- **Clip effects.** Up to five devices can sit on a single clip, which is handy for one-shots without a track of their own (like Ardour's Region FX).

## 6. The playlist

**The Smart Tool is the default pointer (decided).** Over a clip, where the pointer is decides what a drag does, as with Pro Tools' Smart Tool ([B10](#appendix-b-sources)):

| Where on a clip | Dragging does |
|---|---|
| Left or right edge | Trims the clip |
| Top-left or top-right corner of an audio clip | Draws a fade-in or fade-out |
| Upper half | Selects a time range (a clip on a short track has no upper half: all of it moves) |
| Lower half | Moves the clip; with a modifier key it copies or makes an alias (the keymap sets which key) |
| Bottom of the boundary between two touching clips | Draws a crossfade |

**On empty space:**
- dragging selects a time range across tracks, like REAPER's razor edits;
- double-clicking creates an empty MIDI clip on an instrument track, and under the last track adds a track;
- with the FL Studio keymap, a single click places the last-used clip, as FL's draw tool does.

The final mapping will be settled in the usability tests ([section 12](#12-commands-keys-and-help)).

**Single-key tools** use FL Studio's letters:

| Key | Tool |
|---|---|
| P | Draw: a click places the last-used clip |
| B | Paint: drag to repeat it |
| C | Slice |
| S | Slip: moves the content inside a clip |
| T | Mute |
| D | Delete |
| E | Select |
| Z | Zoom |

Esc returns to the Smart Tool. The current tool and its key show on the toolbar and in the hint bar.

- **Digits too (2026-10-13):** in Phazite's keymap, 1 to 9 also pick the tools, in the toolbar's order. They never play notes, so they work while the computer keyboard does.
- **Tool keys spring back (2026-10-13),** as in Bitwig and Ableton Live: a tap of a tool's key switches to the tool, but holding the key while using the mouse keeps the tool only until the key is let go (or until a drag still going on then ends).
- **A held key repeats only the commands that take a step** (nudging, zooming, moving through tracks, undo). Choosing the tool in use again changes nothing, so holding a tool's key never ends a drag.

**Right-click** opens a context menu by default. With the FL Studio keymap it deletes clips, as FL users expect, and a click on empty space places the last clip used, as FL's Draw tool does; Ctrl+drag selects a time range there instead (2026-10-13). Both are keymap settings (`rightClickDeletes`, `clickPlacesClips`). In Phazite's keymap a click on empty space selects, so a click never adds a clip by accident; the piano roll adds a note with a click and deletes it with a right-click in every keymap.

**Editing commands:**
- split at the playhead or pointer;
- duplicate, make alias, make unique. Duplicate with a time range selected copies what lies in it on its tracks right after it, cut at the range's edges as Split cuts (the clips in the range aren't cut there); each piece replaces what it lands on, as REAPER's razor edits trim what is behind them, and the range moves to the copy, so Duplicate again repeats it; Undo puts the range back, as REAPER's undo puts razor edit areas back (2026-10-14, [open question 19](#25-open-questions));
- delete, with or without closing the gap (ripple);
- insert or remove time across all tracks;
- nudge, and quantize clip positions;
- reverse, normalize, clip gain;
- fades and crossfades with curve shapes;
- **Consolidate** (Ctrl+J, 2026-11-08, as Ableton Live's; [recording audit](recording-audit.md)): the selected clips on each track, or what lies in a time range selected across tracks, become one clip on each, a MIDI clip of the notes as they play, or an audio file rendered from the audio clips before the track's devices, as one undo step; and Bounce in Place, a whole track through its devices onto a new audio track ([section 18](#18-projects-saving-and-export)).

**Clips that overlap (2026-10-29, as the owner asked):** clips on a track that overlap all play, mixed together, as in FL Studio and REAPER, so a sample's tail rings on under the next copy of it. The playlist shows them one under another in the track's row, as REAPER's "Offset overlapping media items vertically" does, so each can be told apart and grabbed, and their edges never blend; that is FL Studio's habit of staggering copies on rows, kept inside one track and its mixer channel (decision 1). `getClipSubRows()` in `src/model/arrangement/PlaylistModel.h` lays them out:
- Clips that overlap, directly or through others, share the row out into as many sub-rows as they need, a pixel apart. A clip that overlaps none has the whole row, so a track looks as before until something overlaps.
- The clip made first takes the highest free sub-row. Dragging a newer clip over it never moves it, and a copy dragged off a clip goes under it.
- Two audio clips that overlap only where one fades out and the other fades in (a crossfade) share a sub-row, and the crossfade is drawn where they meet. A crossfade can be drawn only from clips side by side in one sub-row.
- It is view only: nothing is saved, no undo step, and playback doesn't change. A track with many clips stacked can be made taller to give each more room.

Most track-based DAWs play one clip at a time on a track instead: in Ableton Live, Cubase and Pro Tools a clip dropped over another cuts it short or hides it, which would cut off the tail of a sample repeated every bar ([B111](#appendix-b-sources)-[B114](#appendix-b-sources)).

**How a clip shows (2026-11-07, the owner: "they (and also automation tracks) seem to have a large margin on the bottom in order to seperate them from the one bellow, but it seems like a much larger gap than other daws use. also i want the tracks to be semitransparent so you can see the track background (lines and such) through the sample/track/etc."):** a clip fills its row from the top down to the line under the row, which alone keeps it apart from the next track's clips, as clips fill their tracks in FL Studio and Ableton Live (until then a pixel above it, two below and a darker outline left 3 to 5 pixels between two tracks' clips). It is see-through, its track's colour at 50 % (`ClipTint::body`; 70 % until the owner asked for more, the same day: "the background of a track in the playlist (not the notes/clip waveform inside of it) should be more transparent"), as an automation lane is, so that the grid and the sections' lines show through it, inside an edge of the colour itself, opaque; its name and contents are black or white, whichever reads on the colour that shows. It is drawn a pixel short of its end, so that two clips side by side have a line of what is behind them between their edges, rather than the two edges together looking like one thick one (the same day, the owner: "maybe a 1 px line between would help?"), as a folder's row already draws its tracks' clips. An automation lane's wash under its line reaches down to the line under the lane, past the room its points' dots keep at the top and bottom.

**What a clip says over itself (2026-11-07, the owner: "why does each clip have a header with a name and an astrisk? does just having the track lane header (on the left), is that enough...?"; [B143](#appendix-b-sources)):** what a clip holds fills it, without a strip for its name: until then a strip 16 pixels tall along every clip's top repeated its track's name, as a new MIDI clip is called after its track, with a star after an alias's, which many programs use to mark unsaved changes. Every DAW checked names its clips, but those whose clips users praise keep the name out of the content's way: Bitwig 6 made its clip headers smaller, which its users counted among what made the version worth it, and leads its names with the bar a clip starts on, adding its track's only where there is room; Cubase, Studio One and REAPER can hide clips' names, and users complain where a name covers a clip's start. In Phazite the strip did nothing else (the upper half of a clip selects time, as in Pro Tools, where Live and Bitwig drag a clip by its name), so now:
- a **tag** at a clip's top left, in its colour, shows its name only where that says more than its track's header: a name of its own (the inspector's **Name**, which names an alias's copies with it, one undo step; emptied, or set to the track's name, it is called after its track again), a sample's file name unlike its track's, and the take a clip recorded round a loop plays, of how many. A name **like its track's** shows none either (2026-11-07, the owner: "the track header says "Shaker" but the audio clips in the track say "Shaker 85bmp""): the same words but for their capitals and what is between them, and for a tempo or a key that only one of the two names gives, read as the browser reads them from sounds' names, as a sample's file name often has them. "Shaker 82 BPM" or "Shaker Am" over a track called "Shaker", or "shaker_loop" over "Shaker Loop", shows no tag; "Shaker 2", another tempo or another key ("Vox Em" over "Vox Am") still does, and so does a name that is nothing but a tempo or a key. It stays at the playlist's left edge while the clip scrolls past it, as FL Studio's "Keep labels on screen" keeps its clips' labels in view;
- at the top right, a **link** marks an alias, always, bigger than Bitwig's paperclip, which reviewers found too small, and the **metronome** a sample that stretches with the tempo;
- a clip without a name of its own is called after its track, whatever that is called: renaming the track renames it, and one moved to another track takes that track's name. A project saves no name for it;
- with the strip gone, a track is **48 pixels tall** at first rather than 64: its notes have the room they had (43 pixels), and a third more tracks fit;
- a **muted** clip is drawn faint, all of it (`ClipTint::muted`), as a folder's row and the overview draw one; a selected one keeps its outline. Until 2026-11-07 the playlist drew it as any other (known bug 11, which the owner asked to fix). **So is every clip of a track that isn't heard,** muted or kept quiet by other tracks' solos (the same day, the owner: "didn't we plan to dim a track lane that is muted? what happened to that idea?"; nothing had planned it), as Ableton Live greys a switched-off track's clips and FL Studio dims a muted track's; its header keeps its look, with its switch, and screen readers say why a clip doesn't play;
- the tags and marks **stay** when the pointer leaves the playlist (the owner asked whether they should fade then, 2026-11-07; Claude's answer, for the owner to review): they are what the clips say, not controls to reach, needed most when the song is looked over from afar, while it plays; they show only where they add something already; no DAW checked hides its clips' names by where the pointer is; and what comes and goes unasked looks unstable ([B144](#appendix-b-sources), Microsoft's guidance). The overlay scroll bar, a control, does fade ([design language](#design-language)).

**Tracks:**
- **add one** with the "+ Track" button over the track headers (below the selected track), the row after the last track (at the end; right-click it for a folder; return tracks are the mixer's, since 2026-11-01), a double-click under the last track, or the Add menu, as Logic, Cubase and Studio One place it (2026-10-13);
- color and name (automatic from the content, editable), height. The track's header and every clip on it are in its color, strongly enough to tell tracks apart at a glance (2026-10-13); the row behind the clips isn't tinted, so the clips stand out, and a clip can't show another color than its track's (the owner, 2026-10-16);
- **move** tracks by dragging a header from anywhere on it (2026-11-03, as the owner asked: "i don't want a drag handle or the arrow collapse. i instead want it to just be draggable (and the placing action should be cleaned up)"), as Bitwig moves a track by its header ([B128](#appendix-b-sources)); until then a handle at the header's left moved it, and a drag elsewhere selected the tracks it passed. The selected tracks move together, in their order, their headers faded while they move; past the playlist's left edge, a track goes into the browser as a preset. Where they would go is decided by the half of a track's row the pointer is in, before or after it, and shows as a line in the accent colour that starts at the depth in folders they would go to, with a dot at its start, so that going into a folder or out of it is never a guess. On a folder, its middle puts them into it, after its last track (the folder outlined over a wash), its top quarter before it, and its bottom quarter first in it while its tracks show (after it while it is collapsed); under the tracks, at the end. A folder can't go into itself: there is no line there. A click still selects (Ctrl and Shift add), and renaming is Rename on the right-click menu or F2;
- **collapse** a track to a thin row that shows its name: a double-click on its header (2026-11-03, as the owner asked; it was an arrow in the header until then), U, or dragging its bottom edge up past the smallest height (2026-10-13). A collapsed folder hides its tracks instead. **Collapse All** (the button beside "+ Track", and Alt+U as in Ableton Live, where Alt-clicking a fold button does the same, [B128](#appendix-b-sources)) makes every track, folder and automation lane a thin row, or, when they all are, shows them all at full height again;
- **pin** tracks so they stay visible while scrolling, like Pro Tools' Track Pin;
- a **focus mode** that collapses all other tracks;
- mute, solo and record-arm;
- **how loud each track plays now (2026-11-06, the owner: "i also like the little usage indication when a row is actively playing a sound that fl studio has, so add a tasteful one of those to the rows", then: "follow how the graphic for the active track lane looks in fl studio very similarly for ours. i like that it is the full lanes height, right on the edge (small width), and is colored"; [B139](#appendix-b-sources)):** a strip 4 pixels wide down the whole height of the header's right edge, in the track's colour, as FL Studio's playlist shows that a track sounds: the louder channel's peak after the fader and mute, what is heard, from -48 to 0 dB, as a height from the strip's foot in a brighter shade of the colour, over the colour, faint, so that the height tells it, never the colour alone (WCAG 1.4.1). It rises at once and falls 20 dB in half a second without holding a peak, so that each beat shows apart; a peak over 0 dB lights its top 3 pixels in the danger colour for two seconds. It takes no clicks: the header has them. Collapsed rows keep it; a plain folder, which has no sound of its own, and the master's row show none (`src/ui/components/PeakBar.h`). In silence nothing repaints. Its first version, the same day, was a bar 3 by 16 pixels in the header's ink beside the mute and solo switch, whose clip light stayed until it was clicked;
- **the headers' width (2026-11-06, the owner: "are they a good width?"):** 200 pixels still, between FL Studio's (about 150) and Live's with its inputs and outputs shown (about 315), which leaves a track's name about 130 pixels beside its switches; the column's right edge drags it from 150 to 360 pixels, as Logic Pro's and REAPER's can be dragged, and a double-click there puts it back; the settings keep it. A header narrower than 180 pixels gives a folder's or a return's icon's room to its name. No volume or pan in the header: the mixer and the inspector have them (one way per task);
- a CPU readout.
- **Folders, buses and returns hold no clips (2026-11-01, as the owner asked: a bus's row took as much room as a track's, for nothing that can go in it; Claude's choices for the owner to review):** a folder's row, a bus's or a plain one, shows what its tracks play: each of their clips as a bar in its track's colour, on a thin lane for each track that holds clips, faint where muted, so that a collapsed folder still shows its part of the song, as every DAW checked with folders or groups shows its children's clips in their rows (Ableton Live's overview, Bitwig 6's meta clips, Logic's track stacks, Studio One's folder events, Pro Tools' and REAPER's folders, Reason 14's folder clips). It starts shorter than a track's row (40 pixels against 48), and resizes as any row does: no DAW checked makes these rows shorter at first, but they have something to show only in their lanes. A **folder icon** before its name marks it, as most DAWs mark their folders and groups, open while its tracks show (2026-11-03). **Its tracks (2026-11-03):** each is indented, and the folder's colour runs down their left as a rail beside the folder's tint, a bracket round what is in it, as Bitwig and Ableton Live nest the tracks of a group under its colour; a folder in a folder adds a rail of its own. A track's own colour runs on down its automation lanes' headers, so that a lane is seen to be its track's, and the lanes' names line up with their track's. A **return** holds no clips and has no row while it has no automation lanes, as returns, FX and aux channels have none in FL Studio, Studio One, Logic and Reason (the mixer shows them, with their "+" column); with a lane, its thin row comes after the tracks, where the master's shows, as Ableton Live and Bitwig keep theirs at the bottom, marked with the Return key's arrow (Bitwig marks its effect tracks with a downward arrow) ([B124](#appendix-b-sources));

**Sections** (redesigned 2026-10-13) don't take a row of their own:
- they are a thin band in the time bar, above the bar numbers: each section a block of its colour over its time, with its name, and no lines (2026-11-06, the owner: "lets remove the lines in the section (song outline) graphic"; until then the outline's energy curve ran faintly behind the names and a line marked each section's start);
- behind the tracks they show as ghosts: a faint wash of each one's color and a line where it starts, so the song's shape stays in view;
- drag a section to move it, with everything in it, across all tracks (where it would go shows across the tracks too); drag its right edge to resize it; double-click to add or rename one;
- duplicate, duplicate as aliases, delete, rename, recolor;
- right-click the band or the bar numbers to add a section there or apply a song outline ([section 13](#13-getting-started-and-song-outlines)). The Add menu has both too; there is no Song menu.

**The loop (2026-10-13):** playback loops the song, as FL Studio's song mode does: from its start to the end of the bar where its last clip ends, so the loop grows as clips go later. Sections don't count, since an outline can run longer than what is in it so far. That is also where the song ends for Go to End (End); a song without clips ends where it starts, though it loops its first bar (2026-10-14). Loop Selection (Ctrl+L, as in Ableton Live) loops the time range selected in the playlist instead, or the whole song when none is selected; Loop Section loops the selected sections, and Loop the Song goes back to following the song. With FL Studio's keymap, as in FL Studio, a selected time range loops while it is selected, and the song again after. The Loop button in the top bar (L) turns looping on or off, and the rulers show the loop as a band. Playback that doesn't loop stops at the song's end and goes back to where it started, rather than playing on into silence, as Logic stops at its end marker. Where the loop is isn't an undo step, as in other DAWs. **The rulers' loop strip (2026-10-16):** the top strip of the playlist's and the piano roll's rulers is for the loop, as the top of Logic Pro's ruler is its cycle area and Cubase's ruler sets its loop: dragging along it loops that part of the song (Ctrl+drag starts a new loop anywhere), dragging the loop's ends or middle changes or moves it, a click turns looping off and on, and a double-click loops the whole song; lower down, the ruler moves the playhead. **The rulers kept quiet (2026-11-06, the owner: "clean up the timeline rulers to be less busy and fit well with our design"):** a numbered bar's tick reaches from the loop strip down to the ruler's foot, its number beside it in the muted text colour; the other bars and the beats have short, faint ticks at the foot, only where they are 10 pixels apart, and nothing marks what lies between beats, which the grid under the ruler shows; the loop strip has no shade of its own but the hover wash under the pointer, and the loop in it as before (`RulerTint`). Until then every bar, beat and grid line had a tick of its own, as close as 6 pixels apart, and the strip was always shaded. **The playhead drags (2026-11-05, the owner: "i should be able to drag it along the timeline upper track in order to position it. when audio is not playing this is smooth, and when it is playing, the audio pauses while i am dragging, then resumes when i let go"):** a press below the loop strip takes the playhead there and a drag takes it along, to the grid (Alt places it freely), scrolling at the view's edges; while the song plays it is silent from the press, paused where it was (a recording isn't paused), and plays on from where the playhead is let go. Earlier (2026-11-02) a press moved it once and the song played on under the held button, which the owner had found sending the song back. The playhead is in the focus colour, VS Code's light blue, so that it is easy to find: a marker at the foot of the ruler, square at the top and pointing down, with its line from the marker's point over the tracks and nothing of it above the marker.

**Following the playhead (2026-11-02):** while the song plays, the playlist turns the page when the playhead reaches its right edge (View > Follow Playback, on in the playlist, off in the piano roll). Page turning is the default in every DAW that documents one (FL Studio, Bitwig, Cubase, Pro Tools); most offer a continuous mode too, the playhead held still in the middle while the song scrolls under it (Logic Pro's Scroll in Play, Cubase's Stationary Cursor, Bitwig's Continuously scroll, Studio Pro since 7.2). Scrolling the view sideways while the song plays pauses following, as in Ableton Live, until playback starts again, the playhead is moved or the view is centred on it; holding the mouse down in the view keeps the page from turning under a drag. **View > Scroll Continuously** (the owner, 2026-11-02: "an optional toggle", off at first, kept in the settings) holds the playhead in the middle instead, once it gets there from the song's start, and scrolls the song under it, in every editor that follows playback: the view moves by whole pixels, and what scrolls with it is drawn the part of a pixel further, so that the song glides (2026-11-07). It repaints the whole view at each frame of the display: the GPU fills it (Direct2D on Windows), and the playlist keeps its clips as pictures, which the scroll only moves (`docs/performance-audit.md`, section 16). **How the playhead moves (2026-11-07, after the owner saw it jitter):** at each frame of the display it shows the beat heard at that frame's own time, as the engine's blocks place it on a clock smoothed over them, less the delay compensation and the output's latency, so that the line is where the sound is; it is drawn between pixels, so that it glides at any zoom, and on its column in the middle, scrolling continuously, on a whole pixel, while the song glides under it (`docs/performance-audit.md`, section 16).

**Starting from silence (2026-11-02):** playback started, or jumping while it plays, cuts off what still rings from before (a reverb's or delay's tail): every device is reset, Phazite's own and the plug-ins in their helpers, as Ableton Live and Bitwig reset plug-ins as playback starts and FL Studio's "Reset plugins on transport" does. Tails ring on after Stop, and through the loop's end.

**Grid, zoom and scrolling:**
- The grid adapts to the zoom level, with snap on by default; hold a modifier to bypass it. Triplets are available.
- Scrolling is smooth in both directions, never jumping a whole track at a time (an Ardour complaint).
- **The overview (2026-11-06, the owner: "lets copy the layout and transparency of the fl studio scroll bars in the playlist on the top ... lets have the similar visual as fl studio where you can see a mini version of the whole playlist layed out above the timeline, right underneath the horizontal semitransparent scroll bar (similar idea to code document previews on the right which some people like)"; [B142](#appendix-b-sources)):** a strip 20 pixels tall along the top of the time bar, over the sections and the bar numbers, is the whole time line in miniature and its scroll bar, as FL Studio's mini playlist preview shows the clips on its scroll bar and VS Code's minimap shows a whole file: each track that holds clips a thin row of it, its clips in its colour, faint where muted, and the part in view a see-through thumb over them, brighter under the pointer. Dragging the thumb scrolls, and dragging either of its ends zooms, as FL Studio's scroll handle's ends do, within the time line as it was when the drag began, however far past the strip's end the pointer goes (the same day, the owner found that a drag carried off the screen ran the view out, further with each move of the mouse, until Phazite froze); a press elsewhere brings that part into view, centred, and drags on from there; the wheel scrolls. No view starts later than beat 1,000,000, far past any song (`TimelineView::latestStart`). It spans what the scroll bar under the tracks spanned, which it replaces: the song and 16 bars more, at least 64 (of 4/4). The miniature is drawn once for each range and size, so scrolling only moves the thumb.
- **The tracks' scroll bar** lies over the right edge of the lanes, thin and see-through ([design language](#design-language)).
- Dragging anything (clips, edges, ranges, rectangles, tracks, sections, notes, the playhead) near or past an edge scrolls the view along, faster the further out the pointer is (2026-10-13).
- Zoom to selection.

**Clip picker:** a searchable list of every clip in the project, like FL Studio's Picker panel. Drag a clip into the playlist, or pick it as the clip the draw tool places.

## 7. The piano roll

This is the editor the target users spend most time in, and FL Studio's is its most-praised feature: "designed… like a paint program" ([B11](#appendix-b-sources)). Gaps here are what sent FL Studio users away from Bitwig and Reason ([appendix A](#appendix-a-research-summary)).

- **Time counts from the clip (2026-10-13),** as in FL Studio and Live: bar 1 is where the clip's content starts, and a clip opens there. The grid and snapping count from it too; the header says where the clip is in the song, and the playhead shows when the clip plays. With several clips it counts from the first. Later, a switch to song time like Bitwig 6's could edit a whole track.
- **The playhead follows the notes, not only the clip (2026-10-16):** while an alias of a clip shown (a linked copy, which shares its notes) plays elsewhere in the song, the playhead shows, fainter, which of the notes are playing, as FL Studio's piano roll follows its pattern wherever the playlist plays it. Edits change every alias, so the playhead shows every time they play. The view doesn't turn pages while the song plays, so the notes being edited stay in view; View > Follow Playback turns that on.
- **What it shows:** the MIDI clips selected in the playlist, or, when a track is selected, its clip at the playhead or the nearest one. A track without clips shows from the bar the playhead is in, and the first note drawn starts a clip there.
- **Clips grow with their notes, into empty space only (2026-10-14):** a note drawn, moved, lengthened or duplicated past its clip's end makes the clip end at the end of that bar, as FL Studio's patterns grow, but never past where the next clip on the track starts; a note that doesn't fit stays in the clip without playing, as outside a trimmed clip, until the clip's end is dragged out. A looped clip doesn't grow.
- **Mouse model from FL Studio,** with what users of seven DAWs praise and hate in theirs (2026-11-06, the owner's comparison of FL Studio, Live, Logic Pro, Cubase, Studio Pro, Bitwig and REAPER; its recommendations 1 to 10 were built):
  - left-click adds a note and right-click deletes it; a right-click on empty grid that deletes nothing clears the selection, as in FL Studio;
  - new notes reuse the length and velocity of the note pressed last with a tool that places or edits notes (deleting or muting one doesn't choose it). The toolbar shows them, as FL Studio's silent carry-over surprised beginners, and its menu chooses others, or a step of the grid;
  - dragging a note's body moves it and dragging an edge resizes it. Both edges have a 6-pixel zone inside the note; a note too short for that is moved from inside and grabbed 4 pixels outside its edges, as Live 12.4 widened its zones;
  - Ctrl+drag copies, as in the playlist (Phazite's one meaning for Ctrl), Ctrl+click and Shift+click add a note to the selection or take it out, and Alt leaves the grid;
  - Escape during a drag takes it back, leaving nothing to undo or redo, as a modifier during the drag rather than a command; otherwise Escape closes a transform, then deselects the notes (Deselect All Notes), then picks the Smart Tool;
  - a click on a key plays it and selects its notes, Ctrl+click adds them, and a drag along the keys selects a range, as in Live and Logic Pro;
  - the wheel scrolls, Ctrl+wheel zooms and Alt+wheel makes the rows taller, as in the playlist; over the velocity lane the wheel raises or lowers velocities; a middle drag moves the view and never edits;
  - a clip opens zoomed to show it with a quarter of its length after it, at least two bars of 4/4, never smaller than the editors' usual zoom, so that a quarter-beat note is wide enough to grab; a clip shown again opens as it was left.

  A menu button and Shift+right-click open the context menu: the menu button in the toolbar since 2026-11-03, when the owner couldn't find the menu (Shift+right-click was its one way), as FL Studio's piano roll has its menu in its corner. Shift+right-click on a note selects it first, as the playlist's menu does for a clip.
- **Velocity on the notes (2026-11-06):** quieter notes are paler, toward the background, as Live shades them, with a bar along each note's foot as long as its velocity's share of the most, as Logic Pro's line. In the velocity lane, a drag from a selected note's bar moves every selected note's velocity by as much, keeping their differences, as Logic Pro's Velocity tool; a drag elsewhere sets the velocities under the pointer; a right drag draws a straight ramp, as in FL Studio.
- **Slide and portamento notes (2026-11-03, as the owner asked, from FL Studio):** the notes' menu makes the selected notes slide notes (Slide Note) or portamento notes (Portamento), and plain again. A slide note plays nothing itself: the notes sounding as it starts glide to its pitch, evenly, over its length, then stay there, as FL Studio's slide notes make an 808 glide; a portamento note glides in from the note played before it, over the instrument's Glide time, or a tenth of a second while Glide is off. Phazite's synth and wavetable synth play both; plug-ins get portamento notes as plain ones and no slide notes, as FL Studio documents its own for native instruments only ([B129](#appendix-b-sources)). A slide note shows a triangle at its start, pointing on, as FL Studio's slide icon does, and a portamento note a rising stroke. The other DAWs checked bend single notes another way: Ableton Live 12's MPE note expressions draw a pitch and a slide curve inside each note, for plug-ins in MPE mode ([B129](#appendix-b-sources)); that is a larger feature, for later. Legato, which makes each note last until the next, was already in the menu and the transform bar, as Logic Pro's Force Legato does.
- **The overview and the scroll bars (2026-11-06, the owner: "we might think of making the same changes to the piano roll's timeline lines and scroll bars"):** the playlist's overview runs along the top of the piano roll's ruler too, as its horizontal scroll bar, which it replaces: the notes shown in miniature, from the lowest to the highest, in their clips' colours, faint where muted, under the see-through thumb of the part in view, which scrolls and zooms as the playlist's does ([section 6](#6-the-playlist)). Its rows' scroll bar lies over the notes' right edge, thin and see-through. The audio clip editor shows its whole sample and doesn't scroll, so it has neither.
- **Sharp notes (2026-11-03, as the owner saw them blurry):** notes and the velocity lane's stems are drawn on the screen's pixels, their outlines inside their edges, and each velocity ball is centred on its stem (it was half a pixel off).
- **Tools:** paint (drag either way to lay notes along the grid, in the key with Snap to Key and as chords with a chord stamp, like Ardour's note brushing; the first painted replace the selection, as a click's note does), slice, select, mute and delete, on the same keys as in the playlist. The Smart Tool draws, so in the piano roll the Draw Tool's key picks the Smart Tool and its button is hidden (2026-11-06: one way to do one thing).
- **Ghost notes:** notes from other tracks, chosen per track, show faintly behind the clip being edited. Click one to jump to its track.
- **Key and chords:**
  - **a key is a track's (2026-10-13),** chosen in the piano roll's toolbar, and off until chosen: then the rows are a piano's, black keys darker. With a key, the rows outside it are shaded and notes can snap to it. A new track gets the key chosen last in the song, and one command gives every track the same key. The top bar shows no key;
  - the tones of the current chord on the chord lane are highlighted;
  - transposing in steps of the key needs one; without, it is in semitones.

  Out-of-scale shading should be subtle and explained, because an Ardour user found its version confusing.
- **Chord stamps:** pick a chord type and click to place it. No "stamp the current chord" (planned until 2026-11-07, when the owner decided against it: a chord made once is copied as easily, and the rows of the chord lane's chord there are tinted to draw it by).
- **Transforms with live preview:** quantize (with strength, swing and ends, counted from bar 1 as the grid is), legato (with a gap), chop, transpose (diatonic or chromatic), and since 2026-11-06 strum (a step between a chord's notes, up or down), humanize (timing and velocity, the same for the same settings, so the preview is what Apply makes) and arpeggiate (a step of the grid, a gate, up, down or up and down). Still to come: flam, velocity scaling, reverse and flip.
  - Each shows a preview before it is applied.
  - Its settings stay editable until you commit, which avoids Live 12's gotcha where arpeggiated notes become permanent once you select another clip. A bar closed without Apply leaves the settings as they were before it opened.
  - Quantize and Legato, without the bar, use what Quantize... and Legato... were given last, and their names in menus say so ("Quantize (50%)", "Legato (1/16 beat gap)").
  - Each is meant to have a hotkey; FL Studio's keymap has Alt+S for strum and Alt+A for arpeggiate, as FL Studio does.
- **Several clips at once:** edit the notes of several selected clips together, each in its track's color.
- **Lanes under the notes:** velocity is always shown. CC, pitch-bend and per-note expression lanes appear on demand.
- **Drum mode:** for drum-sampler tracks, rows show pad names instead of piano keys and notes are drawn as hits without length. Rows can be reordered or hidden. It comes on by itself with such tracks, and the grid's menu and the command palette turn it on or off; it has no button (2026-10-13).
- **Slides:** pitch glides between notes are sent as MPE note expression to plug-ins that support it. FL Studio's slide notes only work with its own instruments.
- **Capture:** press Capture after playing and the last phrase becomes a clip ([section 17](#17-recording-and-capture)).

### The audio clip editor

**Decided by Claude for the owner to review (2026-10-17, roadmap step 2.6).** The editor dock's first tab shows it in place of the piano roll while the selection is an audio clip, or a track that holds audio, and the tab is then called "Audio clip". It edits one clip at a time: the first audio clip selected. What the reference DAWs show of an audio clip ([B76](#appendix-b-sources) to [B80](#appendix-b-sources), Appendix A): Ableton Live's Clip View has a gain slider in decibels, a pitch control in semitones with a second field in cents, Reverse (which makes a reversed copy of the sample) and Warp with the sample's tempo, over the sample with its start and end markers; Logic Pro's region parameters are gain, transpose and fine tune, Reverse, and Follow Tempo; Studio Pro's audio events have a file tempo that they stretch from to follow the song's; Bitwig's have a stretch mode, their original tempo, fades, and gain and pitch; FL Studio's sampler has pitch, time and stretch modes, reverse and fades.
- **The sample on its own (2026-10-30, as the owner asked):** the editor shows the clip's whole audio file, as wide as the editor, and never scrolls or zooms, as Ableton's Sample Editor and FL Studio's sampler show a sample, so the clip can't be lost in it; until then it was a stretch of the song's time line that scrolled and zoomed as the playlist does. Its ruler counts the song's bars where the audio plays, so that trims and fades line up with the song's grid: a recording seldom starts on a bar of its own, as a pattern does. Its grid is the editor dock's, which the piano roll shares (the project keeps it); Studio Pro's editor also snaps apart from its arrangement ([B81](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **The audio, large:** all of the file: the clip's part in its track's color, and the audio its edges trim off shaded, so that the clip can be trimmed back out to it. Gain shows in the waveform's height, as in the playlist.
- **One way to edit, with visible handles and no keys:** the clip's edges, each a bracket with a grip (ticks along its top and bottom into the clip, thicker under the pointer), trim it; round handles at its top corners fade it in and out, larger under the pointer, and a double-click on one takes its fade away; a drag on the clip's part moves it along the audio, the audio staying where it is on the screen and the clip where it is in the song, so that the clip plays another part of its audio (the Slip tool's edit in the playlist, seen from the sample). Alt places freely, as everywhere, and there are no tools to choose.
