The four fade shapes are on a right-click elsewhere on the clip, as on the clip in the playlist, and in the inspector. **A fade's curve bends (2026-11-03, as the owner asked: "the fader curve should be controlled like all our other interactive lines, with a bendable mid-point that can switch between the different bend types"):** a hollow handle half way along it, on the curve, dragged up or down, bends it as an automation lane's line bends (half the clip's height from straight to all the way), a double-click on it straightens it, and a right-click on it switches it between a curve and an S-curve, as a right-click on an FL Studio automation point picks its curve type and its tension handle bends it. A fade is a curve or an S-curve bent from -1 to 1 (`FadeCurve` in `src/model/arrangement/PlaylistValues.h`): a curve bent to 1 is convex (a quarter of a sine, quick at first), to -1 concave, an S-curve bent to 1 slow at both ends and to -1 quick at both; the four fade shapes of the menus and the inspector set the four ends of that range, so they sound as they did, and anything between is between them. Ableton Live bends a fade the same way, by its slope handle dragged up or down ([B128](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **How the clip plays its audio, down the left,** as Ableton's Clip View and Logic's and Bitwig's inspectors list it: fields to drag, scroll or type in, as the top bar's tempo is, each change one undo step and each drag one:
  - **Gain,** from -60 to +24 dB, with **Normalize** at its right (2026-11-07, the owner asked for it after the research of the same day, [B151](#appendix-b-sources)): a button with an arrow up to a line that sets the gain so that the loudest peak of the part of its file the clip plays plays at -1 dB, a little under full scale, as Apple and Avid advise leaving room. It is one undo step that writes no file, and the gain shows what it did, as Bitwig's, Logic Pro's, REAPER's and Studio One's normalizing set a clip's gain; Live 12's writes a new file, which its users regret, and Cubase and Pro Tools render the audio again. It reads the part as recorded: pitch and stretch barely move a peak, and fades only lower one. A silent clip keeps its gain, and one too quiet goes up to +24 dB. Every DAW checked draws a clip at its own level, after its gain, and none fits each clip to its height, so the playlist doesn't either: a quiet clip looking small is what tells that it is quiet. People normalize now and then (a quiet take, one-shots to one level, a working level), not to mix, so it is one button, with no level to choose, and nothing normalizes by itself;
  - **Pitch,** up to 48 semitones up or down, and **Fine,** 50 cents either way, as Ableton's and Logic's pitch is in semitones and cents; the clip keeps its length;
  - **Reverse:** the same part of the audio plays, backwards, where the clip is;
  - **Follow tempo,** with the clip's **Tempo** (Ableton's Warp and its sample's tempo, Studio Pro's file tempo, Bitwig's tempo): following, the clip stretches with the song's tempo, keeping its pitch, from the tempo its file gives, or else the song's where the clip starts, so that nothing changes until either tempo does. Setting the tempo a vocal was sung at puts it in time: the clip keeps its start and the audio in it, and gets shorter or longer.
- **Reverse, Follow Tempo and Normalize are commands too,** for the selected audio clips: on a clip's right-click menu in the playlist, and in the command palette. Normalize sets each clip's gain from its own peak.
- **Warp markers (2026-11-08, the owner, after Ableton Live's warping; [recording audit](recording-audit.md)):**
  - A marker pins a time of the clip's audio to a beat. Between two markers the audio stretches to fit; before the first and after the last it plays straight; the clip's start keeps its audio.
  - A clip with markers follows the tempo, and stopping following it takes them away. Markers keep their beats when the clip's tempo changes. An alias shares its clip's.
  - Along the top of the audio: the hits Phazite finds in it (once for each file, in the background, by the sampler's onset detection) as small ticks, and the markers as flags in the warning colour (Live's are yellow), each with a line down the audio.
  - A double-click pins the audio there with a marker. A marker drags, to the grid unless Alt is held. A hit drags as the marker it becomes. A double-click on a marker removes it.
  - A right-click offers Remove Warp Marker, Quantize Audio and Remove Warp Markers.
- **Quantize Audio (2026-11-08):** each hit a clip plays moves to the grid line nearest it, through a marker of its own, as Live's Ctrl+U in its sample editor; the clip's start holds, and so do markers no hit replaces. In the audio clip editor it is Quantize (Ctrl+U), to its grid; in the playlist, the clip menu's Quantize Audio, to the playlist's. One undo step.
- **Stretched, pitched and reversed audio is prepared in the background (step 2.18, 2026-10-28):** Phazite renders the whole file with Rubber Band's R3 engine into the project's Cache folder, and the clip plays the render, so it is silent for a moment after such a change (under a second for a loop, about 20 seconds for a three-minute recording), and the editor says so meanwhile. Clips that play a file alike share a render, and undo plays the one before at once. The waveform shows the file itself, backwards while the clip plays it backwards.
- **Not yet:** a choice of stretch algorithms (Phazite uses one, Rubber Band's R3 engine, [section 23](#23-phazites-engine); a Beats mode that slices drum loops at their hits could join it, or Rubber Band's R2 engine for drums: measured on 2026-10-29, it puts every hit within 0.5 ms of a shift that could be taken off, where R3 is 0 to 1.5 ms out, give or take 1 ms, but it smears held chords), warp commands from tempo detection (Live's Warp From Here), a sensitivity for the hits, gain changes inside a clip, and formants; and a waveform zoom for the whole playlist, which Ableton Live 12, Logic Pro, Cubase and Pro Tools have, should quiet clips be hard to see ([B151](#appendix-b-sources)).

## 8. The mixer

**Redesigned 2026-10-13.** The owner gave a track with notes an effect instead of an instrument, heard nothing, and found "+ Device", returns and routing confusing. Research into the mixers people praise (summed up in [Appendix A](#appendix-a-research-summary)) showed the cause: Phazite's single device list followed Waveform's model, where instruments and effects share one list. The DAWs that don't trip people up (Logic Pro, Reason, Live, Studio One) give the instrument a place of its own above the effects.

- **Where it is (2026-10-17):** the centre's second view, beside the playlist ([section 4](#4-the-window)), with the editor dock below it, as Bitwig's Mix view has its Device panel and Ableton's Session view its Device View. A click on a device's name in a strip shows the device in the dock's Devices view; a plug-in's own window opens too, as FL Studio's mixer opens it.
- **Channels:** one per track, in playlist order and colors. Folders appear as their own channels, with their tracks after them. The return tracks come last, together next to the master as in Ableton Live, set apart by a gap.
- **A strip reads top to bottom as the sound goes,** as in Logic Pro and Reason's Track Panel:
  - **the name**, and what the channel is and what feeds it: "Bus: mixes its tracks", or "Return, from Lead, Pad"; at the name's right, the track's switch for mute and solo (and its arm switch, while it records audio), as FL Studio's mixer has its mute switch at the top of each insert (2026-11-01);
  - **the instrument** (tracks only): the device that turns the track's notes into sound, in a slot of its own above the effects. A track with notes but no instrument asks for one ("Choose instrument", tinted), and so does the piano roll's toolbar. Its menu lists instruments only; it has no on/off switch (2026-11-01, as the owner asked: with one instrument a track, its switch was a second way to silence the track): the track's switch mutes it, and Freeze takes its CPU away while keeping its sound. Seven of the eight DAWs checked do have one, as every device in their chains has, mostly to free CPU without deleting the device (Ableton's Device Activator, Bitwig's Deactivate, Studio One's and Cubase's Activate Instrument, REAPER's Offline, Pro Tools' inactive inserts), and none leaves it out on purpose ([B122](#appendix-b-sources)); Phazite's Freeze frees the CPU and keeps the sound, so the switch would only be a third way;
  - **effects**, which change the sound, each a slot of one line, its on/off switch and its name, whose click shows it in the Devices view, where its controls are (2026-10-30, as the owner asked: the slots' knobs for effects' parameters took room the list needed; the strip lists the chain, the dock controls it). A drag on an effect's name moves it up or down the chain, a line showing where it goes. "+ Effect" lists effects only; on a track whose notes have no instrument, it offers the instrument first;
  - **sends:** a knob for every return the track can send to, off until first turned, which makes the send after the fader in the same undo step (question 13, built in step 2.9), with a Pre/Post switch once it is there. "+ Send" makes a new return, with an effect or empty;
  - **where the sound goes:** "Out: Master", or the bus it is in; a click offers the master, the buses (choosing one moves the track into that folder, as the playlist then shows it) and a new bus from the selected tracks (question 12, built in step 2.9);
  - pan, fader and meter, the fader as tall in every strip however many devices and sends are above it, which scroll in what is left (2026-10-30, as the owner asked), and along the bottom a thin bar of how much of each block's time its devices take, amber above half (step 2.9; record-arm later; Phazite's engine doesn't time each device yet, so the bar stays empty for now: `getCpuLoad()`, `src/model/mixer/MixerModel.h`).
  - **the fader's travel and its scale (2026-11-07, the owner: "are the volume sliders standardized to how other DAWs values go? is dB correct, and what about the linear scale?"; [B145](#appendix-b-sources)):** every volume fader shows and takes decibels, as every DAW's mixer does (FL Studio's channel knobs show percentages, for which its own manual gives two different values of -5.2 dB). No fader's travel is linear, in decibels or in loudness: Phazite's follows one curve, 20 ln(position) + 6 dB, which puts 0 dB at 74 % of it and +6 dB at the top, where Live, Logic, Bitwig and Cubase stop too, within the 72 to 85 % at which other DAWs put 0 dB and the 75 % of consoles. Near 0 dB it moves 0.27 dB per 1 % of its travel, finer than a console's 0.42; below -20 dB it gives less travel than a console (27 % against 40 %), which mixing seldom needs. Volume automation lanes draw the fader's position, as Cubase's and Live's do. Left of each strip's fader, a scale marks +6, 0, -6, -12, -18, -24 and -36 dB and silence where the fader puts them, 0 dB in the text colour, as consoles print one and Pro Tools draws one by every fader. (The browser's preview volume had a slider's own range, 0 to 10, which put 0 dB a tenth of the way along; a fader shown in decibels now always runs from 0 to 1.)
- **Returns** hold effects that several tracks share. A new return with an effect comes in one step, as in Studio One: the return, named after the effect, and a send to it from the track. A built-in reverb on it gives only its wet sound. Returns are solo-safe, as Studio One's effect channels are, so soloing a track keeps its reverb audible. **A send gives the return a copy (2026-10-30, after the owner asked why a track sent to a return still went to the master, and whether that was normal):** it is, in every DAW: the track still plays through its own Out, and the return's effect plays alongside it, as a reverb's wet sound joins the dry track. To run several tracks' whole sound through one effect, they go into a bus instead (a folder: Out > the bus, or a new bus from the selected tracks), whose effects their sound passes through on its way to the master. The send knobs', "+ Send"'s, "+ Return"'s and the Out button's tooltips say so.
- **Folders are buses (2026-10-16, [question 11](#25-open-questions)):** every new folder mixes its tracks through its own effects, fader and meter, as group tracks do in Ableton Live and Bitwig and folders in REAPER. Its Bus switch can make it plain, so that its fader only scales its tracks' volumes (a VCA); a plain folder becomes a bus again when it gets an effect, and turns plain when its last one goes. Folders in projects made before keep what they were.
- **Routing shows on selection:** selecting a channel outlines the channels it feeds (the returns it sends to, the bus it is in) and the ones that feed it, and, since 2026-11-01, those its sidechains link it to, either way round: a kick outlines the bass that ducks under it, and the bass the kick. This shows routing without cables, whose views (REAPER's, FL Studio's) get mixed reviews; Logic's Single view, Cubase's "Show Channels that are Connected", Pro Tools' bus interrogation and FL Studio's cables for the selected track show what feeds what from the selection too ([B115](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **Mute and solo: one small switch (2026-11-01, as the owner asked: "the mute and solo buttons everywhere seem to be redundant... one small toggle that left click turns it mute or active, and right click makes it solo"; `src/ui/components/TrackSwitch.h`):** a light, as FL Studio's mute switch is, in a track's playlist header (at its right end), its mixer strip (beside its name) and the inspector (with its state in words). Lit green, the track plays; a click mutes it, the light going out to a hollow ring, and another plays it again. A right-click solos the track alone, as FL Studio's does, and again unsolos it; Ctrl+right-click solos it along with the tracks soloed already, as Ctrl makes Ableton Live's exclusive solo add a track. A soloed track's light is yellow, ringed; a track that other tracks' solos silence shows its light faint. Shape tells the states apart as well as colour (filled, hollow, ringed, faint). A solo never changes the mute switches (the decision of 2026-10-13, kept): FL Studio's solo turns the others' mute switches off and back on, which loses earlier mutes, a complaint on its forum since 2007 that its 2026 beta's testers still make; Renoise has the same one control, left-click mute and right-click solo, with a preference to keep the others' mutes, which is Phazite's way ([B116](#appendix-b-sources)). Mute and Solo stay commands too, in the track's right-click menu and the Edit menu, with keys (S in Ableton Live's keymap), the way from the keyboard and screen readers. Soloing a folder solos its tracks, and soloing a track in a bus keeps the bus audible. Until 2026-11-01 each track had separate M and S buttons, right-click on M soloing alone.
- **Sidechain (step 2.17, 2026-10-21, as the owner asked: it was hard to see and didn't work well; Claude's choices for the owner to review):** a device with a sidechain input, Phazite's compressor, its vocoder or a plug-in, has one place to choose what it listens to: a **Sidechain** button in its header in the Devices view, showing the track's colour and name (or a hollow dot and "Sidechain" while it listens to the sound passing through it). Its menu lists Off and every other track in the song's order, each with its colour; one whose sound comes from the device's own track, which would make a loop, is greyed, with why ("its sound comes from Bass"). The submenu in the mixer slots' menus and the compressor's own box went (one way). In the mixer, a slot whose device listens to a track has a stripe of that track's colour down its left, and its tooltip names the track. The source must be a track, not a folder bus (an engine limit). **Duck Under** in a track's menu does "kick ducks bass" in one step ("Duck Bass Under Kick"): Phazite's compressor after the track's effects, listening to the other track. Choosing a source for Phazite's compressor while it is still as it was made also sets it up to duck, in the same undo step: 1 ms attack, 150 ms release, 8:1 from -30 dB, and, when the source plays a drum sampler, its key cut above 150 Hz, so that the kick ducks and the snare and hats on the same track don't.
- **The mixer has no row of its own (2026-11-01, as the owner asked for the height the strips need):** "+ Return" became a slim column after the returns, left of the master, whose "+" adds one, as FL Studio puts its "+" after its inserts and Ardour under its strips (a strip's "+ Send" also makes a return with its send, in one step). **Routing is set on the strips, with no view apart (the owner's decision of 2026-11-01, [question 21](#25-open-questions)):** the audit the owner asked for found that every DAW checked puts everyday routing on the strip and none needs a second view for it, so the routing view went, and its Routing switch with it (built in step 2.9: every channel a box with jacks along its bottom, wired by dragging cables from its Out, like Reason's back panel). The Out menu, the send knobs and a device's Sidechain button do what it did, and selecting a channel outlines what it is linked to.
- **Pan (2026-11-02, decided by Claude as the owner asked, after research, [B127](#appendix-b-sources)):** constant power, with the sound at its level in the middle: panned hard to a side, that side is 3 dB louder and the other silent, and a sound keeps its loudness wherever it is panned. A stereo track's pan works the same way on its two sides, as a balance. It is Ableton Live's ("constant power panning with sinusoidal gain curves", +3 dB fully to a side) and Logic Pro's default ("−3 dB compensated", 0 dB in the middle); FL Studio's default circular law, Cubase's equal power and Pro Tools' −3 dB are constant power too, quieter in the middle by 3 dB, where REAPER and Studio One keep each side's level (0 dB) and let a sound panned out sound quieter. It replaces the linear law Phazite had by default until then, without choosing it, under which a sound grew 3 dB louder as it panned out; nothing changes for a sound in the middle.
- **Master:** its effects, fader and meter, ending with a loudness meter (LUFS and true peak), which Phazite's engine measures by EBU R128 on the audio thread without locks or allocation, the true peak by ITU-R BS.1770-5's filter, as the limiter finds it (`src/engine/nodes/LoudnessMeter.h`).
- **One output per track:** FL Studio's "route one insert to several inserts" is done with sends.
- **Open:** see [section 25](#25-open-questions), questions 11 to 15.

## 9. Devices, containers and presets

**Device chain:**
- each track has an instrument first, then its effects;
- drag devices to reorder them: an effect's name, dragged up and down the Devices view's stack or a mixer strip's slots, moves it there as one undo step ("Move Device"), with a line where it would go (2026-10-30); bypass, dry/wet and A/B comparison for each.

**The Devices view (the owner's decision, 2026-10-17; two tabs since 2026-10-21):** the editor dock's Instrument (F6) and Effects (Shift+F6) tabs are two pages of one view of the selected track's devices, as Ableton Live's Device View, Bitwig's Device panel, Studio Pro 8's Channel Overview and Reason 14's Track Panel show theirs ([section 4](#4-the-window)):
- **first, on both, a narrow column, 168 pixels wide (2026-11-06):** the channel's name, then its devices under a header that folds them, as VS Code's side bar folds its sections, then its modulators and macros under another. The instrument's name, its Presets and its window's button are in it (the owner: "should the selected instrument bar and preset and popout buttons live in the area of the bottom panel left of the divider? it seems like they take up a chunk of space that they don't really need"); until then they were a header as wide as the dock, in the track's colour, over the instrument's editor;
- **the modulators and macros, under the devices (2026-11-06, the owner: "is the place modulators live the best ui/ux? it seems large and maybe it should be a popout instead of inline when you click on it?"; [B141](#appendix-b-sources)):** a chip for each: its grip, to drag onto a knob it then modulates, its name, and a meter of its output, faint while it is off, or a macro's knob. A click on a chip pops its editor out over the window beside it, above it where there is room, away from the dock, so that the knobs it moves and their rings stay in view, as Bitwig opens a modulator's panel and Pigments keeps its sources apart from what they move; its On switch is there. One shows at a time: a click elsewhere or Escape closes it, as Apple's popovers close, and so does a click on its chip again. Until then the editor opened beside the column, in line, and squeezed the devices. The mixer's strips don't show them;
- **the Instrument page (2026-10-21, as the owner asked: it looked like another effect, in the same place and colours)** shows the instrument's editor alone beside the column, as wide as the rest of the dock, as Logic Pro gives the instrument a slot of its own above the effects and FL Studio puts instruments in channel windows apart from the mixer's effects. A track without one has a button in the column that chooses one (in the warning colour while its notes make no sound); a folder, a return and the master say why they have none, and where their effects are;
- **the Effects page stacks the effects (2026-11-06, the owner: "i also don't like that the more effects you add, the less space those effects have horizontally. so i am thinking of stacking the colllapsed effects similar to how fl studio's mixer works (and vertically scrollable), but then clicking them opens them on the right in the bottom pannels remaining area"):** one under another in the column, in the order the sound goes through them, as FL Studio's mixer stacks a channel's slots, each a row 24 pixels tall of what every effect has: its on/off switch, its name and its mix knob (the limiter has no mix). A click on its name shows it beside the column, in the whole rest of the dock, and lights its row, with its Presets, its window's button and its sidechain under its line; a right-click opens its menu, and a drag moves it up or down the chain, with a line where it would go. A container's row has a chevron that folds its devices, which are under it, indented. "+ Effect" comes after them, and the column scrolls up and down when it holds more than fits. An effect just added shows, and so does a device shown from elsewhere (the mixer, the inspector), on its page; which effect shows is remembered for each channel for the session, and each page keeps its own while the other shows. From 2026-10-21 the effects were cards in a row, each folding to 112 pixels, one at a time open to its whole editor, so the more there were, the less room the open one had;
- **Phazite's own devices show their whole editors there** (the EQ's curve, the compressor's graph, the drum sampler's pads), and a container its name and its eight macros, beside the editor of its device that shows. Where the dock is narrower than an editor's parts side by side, its graphs narrow, then its parts show in rows where the dock is tall enough, or else in pages, whole, without scrolling (see fitting the dock, below). And any of them (not a container) can also show in a window of its own when the user wants (2026-10-21, as the owner asked; the owner had chosen one place on 2026-10-17): its window's button opens it, named after it and its channel ("EQ - Bass"), above the main window, where it was last (its size isn't kept: it opens as large as its editor), made larger or smaller by its edges, its editor wrapping its parts into rows where the window is narrower, down to its widest part; every shortcut works in it (Ctrl+Z after a knob's turn), and the computer keyboard, as in the main window, since it is Phazite's own, not a plug-in's. Meanwhile the dock says where it is, its button lit, so that one editor shows it at a time, and its row keeps its switch, mix and sidechain; the button, a click on its name or its slot in the mixer brings the window to the front; closing the window puts it back in the dock. A window of a device in a container closes as the container's track goes. The whole dock still moves to a second monitor too;
- **a plug-in shows a knob for each of its key parameters and a button that opens its own window,** since none of the DAWs researched shows a plug-in's own interface in line: Studio Pro shows eight chosen parameters, Ableton a set chosen with Configure, Bitwig a list of them all;
- **which track:** the one selected, or, with none, the one live MIDI plays, as the inspector chose its channel before; a click on a device's name in the mixer shows that device's channel, the master's too.

**Fitting the dock (2026-11-06, the owner: "i also want all in-house plugins/effects to be default sized to fit in the default size/shape of the bottom panel without having to scroll etc. you can rearrange sections, have multiple tabs for plugins, etc."; decided by Claude for the owner to review; [B140](#appendix-b-sources)):** every built-in device shows whole in the dock at the default window size (1280 by 800 pixels), with no scroll bar, which a test checks for each one: in 552 by 268 pixels beside the column (until the stack, the instrument had 568 by 240 under its header, an effect alone in the row 532 by 240). As Ableton Live's devices are made for its Device View's height, the editors' parts are laid out for the dock's (`src/ui/devices/DeviceLayout.h`):
- **one part to a column** where a column of several wouldn't fit the height: the synth's oscillators, filter, amp, filter envelope and LFO, then its unison over how it plays; the wavetable's two oscillators, its filter, its amp, its sub and noise over its unison, how it plays, then the rest of its modulation in a list down the side. So a column needs no tabs of its own, and there is one level of tabs at most, as more levels of disclosure lose people (NN/g);
- **a graph narrows first,** down to 200 pixels, before anything goes to another page: the EQ's beside its band's controls, the filter's, the delay's, the reverb's;
- **narrower still, pages:** as many columns on each as fit side by side, under a bar of their names, pills as the dock's own tabs are, each part under its title still (the owner, 2026-11-06: "in instrements, all blocks that function in different ways should have a header saying what they are"; earlier that day the pages' names stood in for their columns' first titles, to save 18 pixels of height). The synth has four pages in the default dock ("Oscillators · Filter", "Amp · Filter Envelope", "LFO · Pitch", "Unison"; three until 2026-11-08, when its pitch, osc 2's sync and the sub's shape came), the wavetable and the multiband four, the compressor, the phaser, the flanger and the vocoder two, the others none; a page shown stays shown;
- **the multiband,** one panel of 1000 by 470 pixels until then, is seven parts: the band tabs with the band's Solo, which lead every page; the band's curve, square; its mode and stereo; its gains and saturation; Monitor, whose seven trace switches are now its Traces menu; the band's envelope and curves; and the crossovers with LMH. Its drags, menus and behaviour are Maximus's as before (the owner, 2026-11-01);
- **the drum sampler's pads** size to the width and the height there is, under their title (Pads, as every block has one), and its knobs sit closer together;
- **the frame gives the editors more room:** they sit 4 pixels from the dock's top and bottom (8 until then), their edges are 4 pixels at their tops and bottoms, and nothing more at their sides;
- **a device's own window** opens at most 1096 pixels wide, its parts wrapped into rows: the multiband in Maximus's two, its curve, its band's controls and Monitor across the top, the rest under them.

The research suggested two things more: keeping several effects open where they fit, folding the one opened longest ago (Microsoft's guidance on expanders that close themselves), which the stack makes moot, as it shows one effect in the whole rest of the dock; and rows that show a value or a small picture of their effect, as Studio Pro's Channel Overview shows its devices' crucial parameters, for the owner to decide.

**Mix (2026-10-13):** each effect has a Mix knob, in its row in the Devices view's stack (in the mixer's slots until 2026-10-30, then in its card's header until 2026-11-06): how much of what it puts out is its effect, from 0 (only the sound coming in) to 100 % (only the effect). The owner asked for it, from FL Studio. Research found it isn't standard: FL Studio's mixer slots and REAPER's effect windows have one for every plug-in; Bitwig and Ableton Live put one on their own devices and blend a plug-in through a container or rack; Logic Pro, Cubase, Pro Tools and Studio One leave it to the plug-in's own control or to sends. Phazite's engine mixes every effect alike, its own and plug-ins (`src/engine/nodes/DeviceRunner.h`): a linear crossfade of the sound coming in and the effect's, the sound coming in delayed as much as the effect delays its own, and a change ramped across the block, so a fast move doesn't click (Ableton's "equal-loudness" option could come later for reverbs and delays). Keep effects on returns at 100 %.

**Phazite's own devices (step 1.6, 2026-10-15):** each is engine-neutral DSP (`src/devices/`), which Phazite's engine plays through `engine::DeviceRunner` and keeps from plan to plan as an `engine::DeviceNode` (`src/engine/nodes/`), with an editor that uses only the model's handles (`src/ui/devices/`), which the Devices view shows (in its own window until 2026-10-17); until a device has an editor of its own, it shows a knob per parameter, each in its unit. Their parameters move smoothly, so turning a knob doesn't click; each passes a guarded real-time audit scenario.
- **EQ:** eight bands, each a low cut, low shelf, bell, notch, high shelf or high cut (cuts 12 to 48 dB per octave, their Q a resonant bump), then an output and the mix. A new EQ changes nothing: its cuts are off and the other bands flat (defaults, proposed: a low cut at 30 Hz, a low shelf at 100 Hz, bells at 250, 800, 2,500 and 6,000 Hz, a high shelf at 10 kHz and a high cut at 18 kHz). Its bands are matched to their analog shapes (Vicanek's matched second-order filters, since 2026-11-06, [dsp-audit.md](dsp-audit.md)), so that a bell or a shelf set near the top keeps its width and symmetry, where the bilinear transform squeezed it. Its editor draws its curve over an analyzer of what it plays: drag a band's dot to move its frequency and gain as one undo step, the wheel for its Q, a double-click to turn it on or off, a right-click for its shape; below, the selected band's controls, which the keyboard and screen readers reach.
- **Compressor:** **a character (2026-11-01, as the owner asked for "depth with different compression types (tube, etc)", sidechain and parallel options, and a much simpler, cleaner picture for a quick compressor; Claude's choices for the owner to review):** five buttons in a row pick how it hears and reacts, as Logic's circuit types, FabFilter's styles and Bitwig's characters do ([B125](#appendix-b-sources)): **Clean** (at first: as it always was, each peak turned down as set, with no colour of its own), **VCA** (it hears the average level, RMS over about 10 ms, calibrated so that a steady sine reads as its peak: short peaks pass, punchy, as the dbx 160's true RMS detector and SSL's bus compressor are used), **FET** (it hears each peak, and adds odd harmonics the further it turns down, as an 1176's distortion grows with its gain reduction: a tenth of Drive's range once it turns down 12 dB; aggressive), **Opto** (it hears the average, and lets go in two stages, as the LA-2A does: the release set for the first half of the way back, then six times slower, and up to twelve times slower again after it has held for a while, as the LA-2A's release depends on "the amount of previous reduction") and **Tube** (it hears the average, and adds even harmonics the further it turns down, as a variable-mu tube's bias shifts). **Since the curve (2026-11-01, the owner's third round), every character hears what comes in (feed-forward):** FET, Opto and Tube had listened to what they put out, as the hardware does, and settled elsewhere than the curve drawn, and a feedback design runs away where a curve expands; Opto's wide knee and Tube's growing ratio were the curve's to draw, so they went with the threshold. **Drive** (none at first) saturates what it puts out in the character's colour, as Pro-C 3's Character, Logic's Distortion section and Cubase's Tube Compressor's Drive do: up to 18 dB into a soft curve, tanh for Clean and FET (odd harmonics), with a share of tanh squared for VCA (a little), Opto (more) and Tube (the most: even harmonics), its DC filtered out, back out at the level it went in, so it colours peaks without making the sound louder; first-order antiderivative antialiasing (Parker, Zavalishin and Le Bivic, 2016) keeps its harmonics from folding back without oversampling or latency. The **Saturator stays a device of its own**, as Ableton, Logic, Bitwig, FL Studio and Cubase each ship a saturator beside compressors that colour, and saturation alone (on bass, drums, a bus) is common: merging it would make the compressor the place to look for distortion ([B125](#appendix-b-sources)). An input gain first (2026-10-30, as the owner asked: "essential"; Logic's, Pro-C's and Bitwig's compressors have one), which drives the sound into it, and its detector too unless a sidechain feeds it, gliding as the makeup does; **its curve, drawn as the Multiband's are (since 2026-11-01, as the owner asked: "i like this interface and interaction with how i can use it as an upwards/downwards compressor/expander, so i want this graph to also be used the same in the compressor plugin"):** Maximus's curve with its axes, its points, lines, drags and menu, the same component for both devices (`src/ui/devices/DynamicsCurve.h`, `src/devices/dynamics/DynamicsCurve.h`), in place of the threshold, the ratio and the knee, so that it compresses or expands, downward or upward, limits or gates by its shape, and a curve copied from a Multiband's band pastes into it; at first 4:1 over -18 dB (its corner at -18 dB, exact again at 0 dB and at +12 dB; a straight line on Maximus's axes is no ratio, two decibels off at 4:1 over -36 dB, so each line of a curve made as a threshold, a ratio and a knee set it is bent by the tension nearest that curve: within a tenth of a decibel for the default and the presets, whose knees get a line of their own), the menu's shapes to start from; its table of gains is spaced every quarter of a decibel, so that quiet levels, which Maximus's axes crowd together, are as exact as loud ones (the Multiband's too); the input gain is Maximus's Pre and the makeup its Post, and the attack takes the gain down and the release back up, whichever way the curve turns. Attack, release and makeup gain, a low cut (12 dB per octave) and a high cut (24 dB per octave, off at 20 kHz) on what it listens to, its key, so that bass notes don't pump it and hats on a drum track don't duck what a kick should, and the mix for parallel compression (defaults, proposed: 4:1 over -18 dB, 10 ms, 120 ms, no makeup, the low cut at 20 Hz, the high cut off, 100 %). **Listen** beside the key's cuts plays the key, as the compressor hears it through them, instead of the sound (whatever the mix, faded over 5 ms), while it is on and the editor shows: the compressor stops half a second by the clock after the editor stops asking, whether it played meanwhile or not, so nothing plays a key while its editor is hidden. It turns both channels down alike, by the louder one's peak, so the stereo image holds, and times its gain in decibels (Giannoulis, Massberg and Reiss, 2012), so attack and release are time constants: after the attack time it has gone 63 % of the way. **Its editor (plainer since 2026-11-01):** its characters as a row of buttons; one picture, its curve, lit up to the level the compressor hears as the Multiband's is, over its menu, Freeze and Snap, with a slim meter beside it of how far it turns the sound down (from its middle down) or up (from its middle up), as compact compressors keep the gain reduction in view and little else (Pro-C 3's compact layout, Ableton's collapsed view, Kilohearts' one meter); Attack and Release large, then In, Drive and Out, and a Sidechain part with the key's cuts and Listen; its sidechain's source is chosen in its header, and its mix, for parallel compression, is its header's knob too. The history beside the curve went: two displays made it busy.
- **Limiter:** the master's last device: a gain that raises the sound, a ceiling it never goes over, and a release (defaults, proposed: no gain, a ceiling of -1 dB, which streaming services' loudness rules ask for, and 100 ms). No mix (since 2026-10-30, after an audit of the effects' parameters): below 100 % the dry sound would take peaks past the ceiling, and neither FabFilter's Pro-L nor Ableton's Limiter has one. It looks 2 ms ahead, so it turns the sound down in a smooth ramp before a peak arrives rather than clipping it, and finds the peaks between samples (true peaks) as ITU-R BS.1770-5 finds them, with its 48-tap interpolating filter, the one the master's loudness meter reads them by (since 2026-11-06, [dsp-audit.md](dsp-audit.md): its 8-tap interpolation had let 18 kHz past the ceiling by 3.8 dB), so that what a converter or a lossy encoder makes of it stays under the ceiling too; its samples never go over it. The look-ahead delays the sound by 101 samples at 48 kHz, which Phazite's engine makes up for on the other tracks. Its editor has meters of the loudest sound coming in and going out, with the ceiling marked, and of how far it turns the sound down.
- **Reverb:** a room or hall around the sound: size, decay (the time the bass takes to fade by 60 dB), pre-delay, damping (past which the treble fades sooner), a low cut and a high cut on the tail (the high cut since 2026-10-30, as Fruity Reeverb 2, Ableton's Reverb and Valhalla's have one apart from their damping, which darkens the tail as it repeats but leaves its start bright; 12 dB per octave, off at its top), width, and the mix (defaults, proposed: 50 % size, 2 s, 10 ms, 6 kHz, 100 Hz, the high cut off, full width, and 25 % mix on a track; a return's reverb is all effect). It is a feedback delay network: after the pre-delay and four allpass filters that smear the attack (from Dattorro's plate, 1997), eight delay lines feed each other through an energy-keeping Householder matrix. Each line loses its treble on a pass in proportion to its length, as its level (Jot and Chaigne, 1991; since 2026-11-06), so that the tail keeps its colour as it fades, and its modulated reading is a 4-point Lagrange interpolation. Each line's length moves slowly, at a rate of its own, so the tail doesn't ring like metal, from a fixed seed, so a song renders the same every time. Its editor draws how the bass and the treble fade after the pre-delay.
- **Synth:** subtractive, with 16 voices. Each mixes two band-limited oscillators (sine, triangle, saw or square with a pulse width; the second tuned in semitones and detuned in cents), the triangle, the saw and the square read from band-limited tables as the wavetable synth's are, the square two saws a pulse width apart (since 2026-11-06, [dsp-audit.md](dsp-audit.md): PolyBLEP had left aliases 25 dB under an A7), a sine an octave down and noise, through a resonant filter (low-pass, high-pass, band-pass or notch at 12 or 24 dB, Comb + or Comb -, Vowel, Phaser or Sample & Hold: steps 2.14 and 2.15, the slope at 12 dB at first) with an envelope of its own and key tracking, then an amp envelope (each envelope's attack, decay and release bending by a curve of its own since 2026-11-01); one LFO moves the pitch, the cutoff or the level, and the mod wheel adds to its depth while it is on. Mono mode plays one voice legato, gliding between the keys held and back to the one still held when a key lets go, as a bassline does; glide works in poly mode too, from the last note. Velocity counts squared (proposed: 60 % of the level depends on it), pitch bend has a range (2 semitones), and the sustain pedal holds notes. When it runs out of voices it takes the one ringing out longest, then the oldest. **Unison (step 2.10):** each oscillator plays up to seven times at once, detuned against each other by up to a semitone (25 cents at first) and spread across the stereo field (80 % at first), at a level that keeps the voice as loud, for the wide, thick sound of a "supersaw"; off at first, three voices when it is switched on. Its noise and its unison's starting phases come from fixed seeds, so it renders the same every time. Its editor shows its parts in columns (`src/ui/devices/SynthEditor.h`; see the pictures below). **Parts switched on as they are needed (2026-11-06, the owner: "the synth instrument doesn't have to have all blocks active to start out with. they could become enabled"):** the filter, its envelope, the LFO and unison each have an On switch at their title's right end; a part switched off is dimmed in the editor and does nothing (with the filter off, the oscillators go to the amp as they are). A new synth's filter and filter envelope are on, as before, and its LFO and unison off, the LFO's depth at 25 % and unison at three voices, so that either is heard as soon as it is switched on. The factory presets switch on what they use.
  - **What classic sounds need (2026-11-08, the owner: presets that sound as people expect, and in-house plugins that miss nothing; [presets.md](presets.md)):** research into 27 classic synths' sounds ranked what the synth lacked by how many of them need it, and the synth now has all of it but a chorus, which is an effect (the Chorus plays the Juno's, below): **Sync 2**, osc 2 starting its cycle again with each of osc 1's, its jump smoothed as the wavetable synth's sync smooths it, and **Env 2**, the filter envelope sweeping osc 2's pitch (±48 semitones), as a Prophet-5's poly-mod sweeps its synced oscillator; the sub a **sine, triangle, saw or square, an octave down or two** (the Juno's and the SH-101's square; the wavetable synth's choices); **Drift**, each voice's oscillators wandering up to 8 cents and its cutoff 2 semitones, slowly and from seeds, as analog voices do; **Filter Drive**, 0 to 24 dB, the wavetable synth's, at twice the rate, for a Minimoog's or an MS-20's grit; **Filter Velocity**, a softer note's cutoff falling, up to four octaves at velocity 0; the LFO's **Pulse Width** target (±45 %, a Juno's PWM) and its **Fade In** (a Juno's LFO delay); a **pitch envelope** (Pitch's Envelope and Decay, as the FM synth's: an 808's drop, a tom's, a zap); and **Glide Mode** Legato, gliding only into a note played over a held one, as a TB-303 slides (Always, as before, at first). At their defaults the synth plays as it did, bit for bit. The editor keeps its first two pages as they were, Oscillators · Filter (the Filter's Drive and Velocity beside its knobs) and Amp · Filter Envelope, and shows the rest on a third, LFO · Pitch (the LFO's Fade In; Pitch's Envelope, Decay and Drift, over Sync and Sub: Sync 2, Env 2 and the sub's octave and shape), before Unison and Voice (its Glide Mode) on the fourth.
- **Wavetable (step 2.13, 2026-10-18, decided by Claude for the owner to review):** a wavetable synth in Serum's layout, kept as simple as Ableton Live's Wavetable (`src/devices/wavetable/`). The owner asked for one in the style of Serum, which they used in FL Studio, after research into the wavetable synths out there. The ones people praise share a core ([B87](#appendix-b-sources)–[B95](#appendix-b-sources)): wavetable oscillators that morph through a table's frames (two in Serum 1, Ableton's and Massive X's; three in Serum 2 and Vital), a sub and noise, unison with detune and stereo spread, a resonant filter, envelopes and LFOs that move things for each note, oscillators that don't alias, and the user's own tables from WAV files. Where they differ is in how a wave is reshaped (Serum's warp modes, Vital's spectral warps, Massive X's ten read modes, Ableton's FM, sync, pulse width and fold) and in how plainly modulation shows. Sound On Sound credits Serum's "unparalleled directness and welcoming clarity", its clean tables and its one-second drag-and-drop modulation ([B87](#appendix-b-sources)); Ableton's manual sets the goal of good results with little effort and depth for whoever looks for it ([B90](#appendix-b-sources)); MusicTech finds Vital easier to program than Serum 2, whose added depth (five oscillator types, an arpeggiator, a clip sequencer) it rates highly ([B89](#appendix-b-sources)). So Phazite's has Serum 1's essentials and Ableton's restraint:
  - **Two wavetable oscillators,** each switched on or off (by the switch at its title's right end since 2026-11-06), with its table, its position (where it plays in the table, crossfading between frames), pitch (±36 semitones) and fine tune, level, and a warp with an amount: Sync (hard sync, the wave read 1 to 8 times a cycle), Bend + and Bend −, Pulse Width (the whole wave squeezed into part of the cycle, as Ableton's PW does for any table), Mirror (read forward, then back), FM and Ring Mod from the other oscillator (Serum's "from B" warps). Those are the warps that stay clean; Serum's and Vital's quantize, remap and spectral warps wait.
  - **Sub and noise:** a sub oscillator (sine, triangle, saw or square, one or two octaves down) and white noise, in the middle.
  - **Unison** for both oscillators: up to eight voices (Ableton's most, [B96](#appendix-b-sources); Serum and Vital have 16), detuned up to a semitone (20 cents at first), spread across the stereo field (80 %) and, as Ableton's "position spread" does, across the table, at a level that keeps a note as loud.
  - **One filter** (Serum 1 has one main filter): low-pass, high-pass, band-pass or notch at 12 or 24 dB per octave (24 at first), Comb + or Comb - (step 2.14), Vowel, Phaser or Sample & Hold (step 2.15), with resonance, drive and key tracking, built as Phazite's filter device is, switched on and off at its title's right end. A new synth's is on: low-pass 24 at 2.5 kHz.
  - **Three envelopes and two LFOs** (Ableton's counts; Serum 1 has three and four): the first envelope shapes the level, the others are for modulation, each stage bending by a curve of its own (2026-11-01); each LFO (sine, triangle, saws, square or random) runs in hertz or on a note value at the song's tempo, free or starting again with each note.
  - **Eight modulation slots,** each a source (Envelope 2 or 3, LFO 1 or 2, velocity, the note, the mod wheel, or a random value for each note: Ableton's sources) moving a destination (each oscillator's position, warp, pitch or level, the pitch of both and the sub, the sub's and noise's levels, cutoff, resonance, volume, pan, unison detune) by an amount, for each note on its own, so an envelope sweeps every note's table from its start: the move wavetable sounds are made of. Phazite's modulators and macros (step 2.3) still move any of its knobs for the whole track; the slots are for what moves inside each note. A new synth's first slot is Envelope 2 to the cutoff at 25 % (two octaves), so it starts as a saw that opens with each note, finished rather than blank (design principle 5).
  - **Voices:** 16, or mono with legato and glide; pitch bend (2 semitones), the sustain pedal and velocity (60 %) work as on the synth. No effects of its own, unlike Serum's and Vital's racks: Phazite's devices follow it (one way per task).
  - **Sound:** each frame is kept band-limited at every pitch: for each half octave, a copy with only the harmonics whose aliases stay above 20 kHz, made with an FFT when the table is made, read with cubic interpolation and crossfaded between frames, so a plain table plays without audible aliasing, as Ableton's manual promises of its own ([B90](#appendix-b-sources)). A warp reads the copy its fastest reading allows, and sync's jump back is smoothed (PolyBLEP). Its filter's drive runs at twice the sample rate, played by its antiderivative, so that it doesn't alias (since 2026-11-06). Its noise, unison phases and random values come from fixed seeds, so it renders the same every time.
  - **Tables:** 13 come with it, made from formulas when first needed, so Phazite ships no sound files: Basic Shapes (sine, triangle, saw, square: the first, a saw at first), Pulse Width, Saw Build and Square Build (harmonic by harmonic), Vowels (A to U), Sync, FM, FM Hollow, Fold, Digital (bit steps), Comb, Growl (a talking bass) and Spectra. Projects keep a factory table by its name. A sound of the user's becomes a table when it is dropped on an oscillator's drawing (from the browser or Explorer) or picked with Load Wavetable File... from the oscillator's table menu: Serum's WAV tables, whose 'clm ' chunk gives their frames' length, as Surge XT reads them too ([B93](#appendix-b-sources), [B94](#appendix-b-sources)), any sound of whole 2048-sample frames, a single cycle of any length, or else a sound's first frames of 2048, up to 256. The project plays its own copy, in its Audio folder, as with the drum sampler's pads, and a preset carries it to another project.
  - **Editor:** each oscillator's table drawn as its frames one behind another, as Serum and Ableton draw theirs, with the frame the newest note plays in the accent color, moving as modulation moves it; dragging across the drawing moves the position, and the table's name above it opens the table menu. Under each drawing its oscillator's controls, then the rest in groups, each under its title (`src/ui/devices/WavetableEditor.h`); an oscillator switched off is dimmed. It passes a guarded real-time audit scenario and has a golden render.
  - **Later:** a third oscillator and a second filter (Serum 2, Vital), drawing and editing tables, Vital's spectral warps, modulation dragged onto its own knobs, drawn LFO shapes, an arpeggiator.
- **Drum Sampler:** 16 pads on the notes from 36 up, General MIDI's drums, laid out as FL Studio's FPC and an MPC lay them: the first at the bottom left. Each plays its sound once, whole, however short the note (a one-shot), at its level, pan and tuning, with an attack that softens its start and a length that cuts it short with a 10 ms fade (at its longest, 10 s, the whole sound). A pad tuned, or whose sound is at another rate than the engine's, is read by a windowed sinc whose cutoff follows its pitch, so that it keeps its top octave and a pad tuned up doesn't alias (since 2026-11-06). Pads in the same choke group stop each other; the closed, pedal and open hats start in group 1. A new sampler plays a kit that Phazite synthesizes from the same code as the synthesized drums in Phazite Sounds (`src/devices/drums/DrumSynthesis.h`), so it needs no files. Phazite's kits, its drum presets, each have sixteen sounds of their own, synthesized the same way to fit their names (2026-10-30, as the owner asked: the kits had all played the default kit's sounds, changed a little): a house kit, a lo-fi kit read at a low rate and few bits as an old sampler plays it back, a punchy kit, a tight kit and a wide one in stereo (`src/devices/drums/FactoryDrums.h`); a pad keeps its kit's sound by its name. **Classic drum machines (2026-11-08, the owner: "add a lot of classic synths as presets. for example TR-808"; [presets.md](presets.md)):** the 808 Kit is the TR-808's sixteen voices, each made as its circuit makes it (`src/devices/drums/DrumMachines.h`: its bridged-T kick at 50 Hz with its sigh, its snare's two resonators and noise, its toms and congas at the service notes' pitches and decays, its hats, cymbal and cowbell from its six square oscillators through its bands, with no noise), General MIDI's drums where it has them and its claves, maracas, congas and cowbell where it hasn't; the 909 Kit the TR-909's analog voices and its six-bit metal; the Rhythm Box Kit a CR-78's fourteen voices; the Trap Kit what a trap kit needs (a short kick over a long 808, rolls, a phonk cowbell); and the Percussion Kit hand percussion. They replace the earlier 808 and techno kits, which had been a General MIDI kit in each's colours. A sound dropped on a pad from the browser or Explorer, or tried on it with Swap and kept ([section 11](#11-the-browser)), plays over the pad's kit sound, and the project plays its own copy, in its Audio folder (`src/model/devices/internal/DrumKit.h`); Reset puts the kit's back. The sampler swaps a sound in while it plays without the audio thread waiting or freeing memory: a voice of the old sound stops at the next block, and the old sound is freed two blocks later. Clicking a pad, or Space or Enter on it, plays it and shows its controls; pads light up as they play. The piano roll's drum rows are its pads, named after what they play.
- **Sampler (step 4.6, 2026-11-07, the owner's request: "add them to the roadmap for right now ... a robust plan of what controls they each need, and how best to hook them up ... the ui/ux should be solid"; Claude's choices for the owner to review):** one sound played across the keys (`src/devices/sampler/`). After a synth, a wavetable synth and a drum sampler, a sampler is what producers use most: all eight DAWs researched ship one, and in FL Studio's forum poll its samplers (Channel Sampler, Slicex, Fruity Slicer, DirectWave) took 17 % of the votes, more than any single plug-in ([B152](#appendix-b-sources)). Its controls are the core Simpler, Quick Sampler, FL Studio's Sampler and Slicex and Bitwig's Sampler share, with what their users praise and without what they complain of ([B153](#appendix-b-sources)):
  - **Three modes,** buttons at the drawing's top: **Classic** plays the sound at the note's pitch against its root key for as long as the note is held, through an ADSR whose stages bend (the synth's), with a loop; **One-Shot** plays it from its start marker to its end whatever the note, **Trigger**, or while it is held, **Gate**, with a **Fade In** and a **Fade Out** (10 ms at first), as Simpler's and Bitwig's Shot do; **Slice** cuts it into slices, each on its own key from C2 (note 36, as the drum sampler's pads) up, at its own pitch, triggered or gated, faded alike. The amp envelope shows faint outside Classic, where it does nothing.
  - **Its sound:** a new sampler plays an 808 bass at C2 that Phazite synthesizes (`FactorySamples.h`), so that it plays a finished sound at once; its menu also offers every factory drum by kit, Load Sound File... and Find Root Key; a sound dropped on its drawing from the browser or Explorer, or loaded, is put on it as one undo step ("Put Vox on the Sampler"), the project playing its own copy in its Audio folder (`src/model/devices/internal/SamplerSound.h`), its first two minutes, read once and kept while anything plays it. A new sound keeps the mode and every setting but those that belong to the sound: its root key and fine, its markers (the whole sound) and its loop (its file's, or off).
  - **The root key** (a note, shown as "C4"), so that a note plays the sound in tune: the note its file names (a WAV file's smpl chunk), else a note in its name ("808 Long F#") in the octave its pitch is in, else its pitch, found past its attack by YIN, else middle C, in Logic's order of reading a root key; **Fine** takes back how far its pitch is off that note, within half a semitone, so that an 808 tuned between notes plays in tune. Transpose ±48 semitones, Fine ±50 cents.
  - **The markers, on the drawing:** Start and End, and Classic's Loop Start and Loop End with its band, each dragged (one undo step each), snapping to the zero crossing within 10 ms of the pointer (Alt places it anywhere, Shift moves it in fine steps), a double-click putting it back; the band drags the loop whole. The loop's seam crossfades at equal power with what comes before its start (**Crossfade**, a part of the loop's length, 10 % at first; up to as much as there is before it), so that a held note loops without a click; its file's own loop is put on with the sound, else Loop Start is two thirds of the way in, a held note's sustain. Every marker has a knob too.
  - **Slices,** by **Transients** (found as the sound loads, by the spectral flux of its log magnitudes, each placed where its rise starts and on a zero crossing; **Sensitivity** adds them the strongest first, half of them at first), **Beats** (a note value, a sixteenth at first, at the sound's own tempo where its loop data or name say how many beats it lasts, else the song's), **Equal** parts (8 at first, up to 64) or only by hand (**Manual**). Slices put in by hand stay whatever slices it, and one it found that was moved or taken away stays away, as Simpler's, Logic's and Bitwig's do (praised; FL Studio's Slicer 2 users complain of the opposite): a click on the strip under the drawing adds one, a drag moves one, a double-click takes one away, each one undo step, the hand's in the accent colour. **Choke** (on at first) makes a slice cut off the one playing, as a chopped break's slices do; **Play Through** plays on past a slice's end. **Slices to Clip**, on the drawing in Slice mode, writes a slice's note at each slice's beat into a new MIDI clip on the track at the play start (after the track's last clip where one is in the way), as one undo step, so that the clip plays the loop in time with the song at any tempo: only on command, never by itself (FL Studio's users ask for its auto dump off).
  - **Playing:** 16 voices, or **Mono**, legato, gliding between the keys held without starting the sound or its envelopes again, as an 808 line glides (Cubase's Sampler Track restarts its sound, its users' complaint), FL Studio's slide and portamento notes too; Glide, Bend Range (2 semitones), Velocity (60 %), Level (-6 dB), the sustain pedal. A press on the drawing plays the sound at its root, or in Slice mode the slice under the pointer, while it is held.
  - **The filter, its envelope and the LFO,** switched on at their titles, the synth's (off at first, the sound as it is); the LFO moves the pitch, the cutoff or the level.
  - **Sound:** read between its samples by the windowed sinc whose cutoff follows its pitch (the drum sampler's), forward or reversed, a note pitched up an octave or more from the sound's octaves, made as it loads (each at half the rate of the one before), so that it costs a third to a tenth as much and sounds the same ([performance-audit.md](performance-audit.md), section 17); a start or an end in the middle of the sound fades over 32 samples, a voice taken for another note over 3 ms, and one still on a sound swapped out within the block, so that nothing clicks; it plays the same every time.
  - **Its editor** (`src/ui/devices/SamplerEditor.h`): the sound alone on the default dock's first page, as wide as the dock: its modes and its name's menu, the drawing with every voice's place in it as it plays, and under it the controls of the mode it plays in; then Pitch over Voice and the Amp; then the Filter and its Envelope; then the LFO.
  - **Its presets** (`resources/presets/Sampler/`): ways to play the sounds that come with it: 808 Glide, 808 Punch, Lo-Fi 808, Distorted 808 (the trap kit's 808), Cowbell Lead (the 808's cowbell, saturated, across the keys, as phonk plays it), Reverse Crash, Ride Chops and Rising Toms.
  - **Later** (the research's "all five have it"): follow the song's tempo for loops; ping-pong loops; what happens at a note's release; a pitch envelope; settings for each slice; Swap for its sound. Multisampled instruments read from SFZ files, pianos and orchestras from the CC0 libraries Phazite Sounds can carry ([B155](#appendix-b-sources)), became a device of their own on 2026-11-08: the Multisampler, below (step 4.10).
- **FM (step 4.7, 2026-11-07, the same request; its editor and what it shares with the FM synths people use most, 2026-11-08):** four operators in Yamaha's eight 4-operator algorithms (`src/devices/fm/`). FM synths are next after samplers: six of the eight DAWs ship one, Sytrus was second only to FLEX in FL Studio's poll, and Ableton's Operator was among the instruments fewest people leave unused ([B152](#appendix-b-sources)). Four operators, not the DX7's six, and fixed algorithms drawn as pictures, not a matrix: the synths praised as approachable (Operator, Digitone, reface DX, Meld) have four or fewer, every classic FM sound (electric piano, bells, basses, brass, mallets, organ) fits in four, and the complaints gather on six operators and matrices (the DX7, Sytrus, FM8) ([B154](#appendix-b-sources)). The owner then asked (2026-11-08) for research into the FM synths most used and best rated, what people expect of them and how their editors are laid out, as the algorithms' pictures were clunky ([B156](#appendix-b-sources)): Dexed, FM8, the Digitone, Sytrus, Operator, the opsix, DX7 V and the reface DX lead; every one has operators at ratios or fixed frequencies with envelopes of their own, velocity and key scaling, algorithms and feedback; most of the newer ones feed back on any operator, and seven of the eight DAWs' FM synths have unison or spread; and the editors praised show every operator's level and ratio at once (Operator's shell, the opsix's operator mixer, PX7's rows, DX7 V's overview, the reface DX's pages), only the algorithm played, drawn large (none of them keeps every algorithm on its panel), carriers and modulators in two colours (the opsix), and what the sound does as it changes (Phase-4's and opsix native's scopes, FM8's spectrum, DX7 V's operators lit by their output). So:
  - **Each operator:** a sine, or a band-limited triangle, saw or square; **Feedback** of its own wave into itself, from -100 % to 100 %: to the right toward a saw, as the DX7's feedback (its wave fed back), to the left toward a square (its wave squared fed back, the same in both halves of its cycle, so that only odd harmonics are left), as the reface DX's goes both ways, on every operator, as on Operator, the reface DX and DX7 V (at 100 %, 2π radians for what the operator puts out at its full level, the DX7's feedback 7 as Dexed's engine, msfa, works it out, so that 50 % is its feedback 6, the brightest saw and the squarest square, and beyond it the sound turns rough and at 100 % toward noise, as the DX7's 7 does; averaged over its last two samples as the DX7 does, so that it doesn't hunt); a **Ratio** of the note's pitch, from 0.5 in halves to 32, and **Fine** between them (up to 0.49), so that each ratio is set one way; **Detune** ±50 cents, which Operator's and Digitone's users missed; **Fixed** at a frequency from 1 Hz to 10 kHz, whatever the note, for drums and metallic sounds; its **Level**, a carrier's loudness or a modulator's depth (to 4π radians, both as the square of the knob, so that it turns evenly to the ear); **Velocity** (how much a note's velocity changes its level: on a modulator, how much brighter it plays harder); **Key** scaling (at 100 %, half the level each octave above middle C, double below, as the DX7's level scaling and Digitone II's key track keep high notes from going harsh); and an ADSR whose stages bend, the synth's, in times rather than the DX7's rates. Each switches on and off.
  - **The algorithms,** named by their shapes: **Stack** (4 > 3 > 2 > 1), **Two Into Stack** (the TX81Z's Lately Bass), **Chain And One** (mallets), **Three Into One**, **Two Pairs** (the electric piano's, bells, brass), **Stack And One**, **One Into Three** (brass, strings) and **Four Carriers** (organs). The carriers' sum is a square root of their number down, so that changing the algorithm keeps the level, and 5 dB more, so that a new FM synth's chords are about as loud as a new synth's and stay under full scale (its electric piano's two carriers start in phase and add up).
  - **For the whole synth:** **Timbre** turns every modulator and every operator's feedback up or down at once (0 to 200 %, the feedback no further than its full), as FM8's Brightness and Operator's FM Drive do; **Key Time** makes the envelopes shorter up the keys (25 % at first), the DX7's rate scaling; **Phase**, each note's operators from the start of their waves (**Reset**, at first: a note sounds the same every time and can't click) or running free; a **pitch envelope** (±48 semitones, falling over its decay) for drums and plucks; the filter (off at first) with key tracking; one LFO to the pitch, Timbre, the level or the cutoff; 16 voices or mono legato with glide; **Spread** (0 to 50 cents, off at first): each note played twice, that far apart, the lower on the left and the higher on the right, as Operator's Spread does, at twice the work while it is on; Level (-6 dB).
  - **Sound:** phase modulation at twice the sample rate through the half-band filters of the wavetable synth's drive, always, live and in renders alike (no hidden quality switch, unlike Sytrus's draft and render rates), so that sidebands up to about 72 kHz don't fold back; an operator above what the rate can play is silent; a DC blocker at 5 Hz takes away what FM leaves off centre: a heard operator fed back hard toward a saw sits up to half its peak off it, and a modulator at its carrier's ratio, a few cents off it, makes a sideband at 0 Hz that wanders slowly up and down (the electric piano's did; its render is about 0.5 dB quieter without it).
  - **A new synth** plays a soft electric piano, two pairs as the DX7's E.PIANO 1 is built: operators 1 and 3 its carriers a few cents apart, operator 2 at 14 times the pitch its tine's bright attack, operator 4 at the pitch, fed back a little, its body; velocity mostly changes how bright it is, and nothing sustains.
  - **Its editor** (`src/ui/devices/FmEditor.h`, 2026-11-08): its operators alone on the default dock's first page, laid out as the editors praised lay theirs out ([B156](#appendix-b-sources)). Down its left, the algorithm played: its name between arrows that step through the eight (the arrow keys too), and its picture, large, each operator a box with its number, the carriers (heard) in the device's colour along the line to what comes out, the modulators in modulation's colour over what they modulate, a loop at the side of each operator fed back (dashed toward a square), the boxes glowing with the newest note's envelopes; a click on it shows all eight, as opsix native does, each with its picture and what it is good for, a click on one playing it (one undo step). Under the picture, the harmonics the operators make together as a note strikes (or as the newest note plays), a bar for each up to the 16th, as FM is taught: the more modulation, the more of them and the higher they reach, where the ratios put them; then Timbre. Beside it, the four operators side by side ([question 24](#25-open-questions), answered): each column headed by its switch, its number in its role's colour and what it does in this algorithm ("Heard", or what it modulates: "→ 1"), then its envelope (points drag, stages bend), its Level as a bar in its role's colour, and its Ratio and Fine (its frequency instead, while it is fixed); under them, tabs as wide as their names show one group of all four operators' controls at a time, as opsix native's tabs and the reface DX's pages do: **Envelope** (times, sustain and each stage's bend, drawn), **Tone** (the wave, each drawn, and the feedback), **Pitch** (Fixed and the detune) and **Scaling** (velocity and key). The operator under the pointer lights its box. Each control's tip says what it does in its operator's role (a carrier's Level is how loud it plays, a modulator's how bright it makes what it bends). Then Pitch over Voice (with Spread), the Filter and the LFO.
  - **Its presets** (`resources/presets/FM/`, 36 since 2026-11-08, [presets.md](presets.md)): keys (E-Piano, Tine E-Piano, Clav, Harpsichord, Organ, House Organ), basses (FM Bass, Pick Bass, Lately Bass, 16-Bit Bass, Feedback Bass, Sub Pluck), leads (80s Lead, 16-Bit Lead, Hollow Lead, Chip Square: one operator fed back toward a square), mallets (Marimba, Vibraphone, Tubular Bells, Bell, Kalimba), Brass, strings (String Section, Warm Strings), Flute, plucks (Koto, Nylon Pluck, Glass Pluck), Glass Pad, percussion (FM Kick, Snare, Tom, Hat and Cowbell, and Amapiano's Log Drum) and FM Zap; the classics from the DX7's, the TX81Z's and the Genesis's own voices, decoded and converted as Dexed's engine and ymfm work them out.
  - **Left out:** the DX7's six operators and its patches (which need them), and the TX81Z's 4-operator banks, for now; a routing matrix and algorithms of the user's own (power users ask for them; eight with feedback on every operator make the classic sounds); unison beyond Spread's two; waves drawn by the user; looping envelopes, DX-style rates and levels, and level scaling with a breakpoint (one key amount instead); an LFO amount for each operator (the track's modulators reach every operator's level); a pan for each operator (Spread widens the whole); copying an operator's settings onto another; effects (Phazite's own devices follow it); and a randomizer (SOS rarely got anything useful from opsix's).
- **Multisampler (step 4.10, planned 2026-11-08, the owner: "What is the best way to get piano and guitar instruments into Phazite? ... What would it take for it to be high quality?", then "it would be great to support other real instruments as well"; Claude's choices for the owner to review):** real instruments, pianos, orchestras and basses first, guitars later, each played from many recordings of it ([B158](#appendix-b-sources)). After the synths, a sampled piano is what DAWs ship most: six of the eight researched ship one, as many as ship an FM synth, and five an orchestra ([B152](#appendix-b-sources)). None of them makes each instrument a plug-in of its own: their real instruments are content that one engine plays, as Ableton Live's Sampler plays its Packs, Logic Pro's Sampler its library, Fender Studio Pro's Presence XT, Cubase's HALion Sonic and FL Studio's FLEX their sound sets (FLEX the most used of FL Studio's instruments in Image-Line's poll), with an engine of its own only for a flagship such as Logic's Studio Piano. Guitars are where all of them are weakest: Logic's Session Players play drums, bass, keys and synths, and Fender Studio Pro lists no guitar among its instruments, its guitar tones coming from amp and pedal models; the convincing virtual guitars are other companies' (Ample Sound's, Native Instruments' Session Guitarist, AAS's Strum), each with an engine that plays as a guitarist does. The Multisampler is a device of its own beside the Sampler, as Ableton's Sampler stands beside Simpler and Logic's Sampler beside Quick Sampler: playing an instrument of many recordings is another task, with another editor, than playing one sound across the keys, and the Sampler stays one sound.
  - **What it plays:** zones, each a recording over a range of keys and velocities at its own root key; **round robins**, several takes of a note played in turn or at random, so that repeats don't sound alike; **release recordings**, played as a key is let go, quieter the longer it was held; **keyswitches**, keys outside the instrument's range that switch its articulation; controllers that choose zones or crossfade them, as the mod wheel crossfades an orchestral section's dynamics apart from velocity; groups that stop each other, as a closed hi-hat chokes an open one; and each zone's envelope and loop. Up to 128 voices; when they run out, the quietest fades out over a few milliseconds rather than stopping.
  - **A piano played as a piano:** the sustain pedal at every position, half-pedal shortening how long the dampers take, pedalling again catching notes still ringing; the pedal's noises where the instrument has recordings of them; a key struck again while it sounds fading its old note over a short time instead of cutting it; and, with the pedal down, the open strings ringing with what plays, through Phazite's own tuned resonators where the instrument has no recordings of that.
  - **Its files are SFZ,** the open text format free libraries use ([sfzformat.com](https://sfzformat.com/)), read by Phazite's own reader for the part real instruments use: the control, global, master, group, region and curve headers, `#define` and `#include`, and what maps, layers, alternates, switches, crossfades, chokes and releases recordings, their envelopes and loops, the sustain pedal and the names of an instrument's controls. What a file uses that it doesn't read is listed as it loads. Not sfizz, the reader most free players use, which is archived, its last change in March 2025, nor liquidsfz, under the MPL 2.0, which isn't among Phazite's licences: Phazite has the hard parts already, the Sampler's windowed sinc, its voices and their hand-over to the audio thread, and the read-ahead that plays audio clips.
  - **Streamed from disk:** a piano is hundreds of recordings, so only the start of each is kept in memory, and the rest is read ahead by the engine's reading thread as a note plays, as audio clips are (`src/engine/audio/AudioFile.h`). A recording at the engine's rate, played at its own pitch, is copied as it is; any other is read by the windowed sinc.
  - **Its instruments are its presets,** named for what they are: Grand Piano, Upright Piano, Violins, Cellos, Trumpet, Flute, Electric Bass, Upright Bass. The browser lists them under the Multisampler, each with its size until its recordings are downloaded; a click downloads them, with progress, and only then (Phazite connects only when the user asks: [section 22](#22-privacy-licences-and-trademarks)). An SFZ file of the user's, from the browser or Explorer, plays on a new Multisampler on the track it lands on, kept where it is by its path, as large libraries are, not copied into the project; the Project view lists it, and finds it again if it moved.
  - **Its editor:** the instrument's name and menu (its presets, Load SFZ File...); a keyboard along the bottom, the keys it plays marked and its keyswitches in another colour, the keys held lit; and above it the instrument's own controls, as its file names them, then what every instrument has: Level, Velocity (how much velocity changes the level, and its curve), Release, Transpose and Tune, and, where the instrument has them, Pedal Noise, Key Noise and Resonance.
  - **Phazite Sounds' instruments:** each a pack of its own in the phazite-sounds repository, CC0 only, in FLAC, pinned by its address, size and SHA-256 as the first pack is, and never in the installer. Their recordings are edited before they ship (levels matched across keys and velocity layers, tuning checked, noise and silence taken out), then leveled as the presets are ([presets.md](presets.md)). The first: a grand piano, chosen by ear among VCSL Keys' Steinway B and Kawai, Versilian's and Karoryfer's Osiris Piano and Scarypiano; an upright, from VCSL Keys; the VSCO 2 Community Edition orchestra (violin, viola and cello sections, solo violin, double bass, harp, horn, trumpet, trombones, tuba, flute, piccolo, oboe, clarinet and bassoon); and an electric bass and a double bass of Karoryfer's. Salamander Grand Piano, the best-known free grand, is CC BY 3.0, so it is not for Phazite Sounds.
  - **Checked as the presets are:** the preset report plays a velocity sweep on every key, which must get louder at every step with no jump where layers meet, neighbouring keys alike in level and in tuning (against the instrument's own tuning, as a piano's octaves are stretched), and no click where a voice is taken; the benchmarks time 128 voices; a guarded real-time audit scenario plays an instrument streamed from disk; and a golden render plays a test instrument that the tests make from synthesized sounds (the repository holds no sounds).
  - **After v1** ([roadmap](roadmap.md#after-v1)): **articulations in the piano roll**, each note's in a lane under it, as Cubase's Expression Maps, Logic's Articulation Sets and Studio Pro's Sound Variations show them, for keyswitched instruments, plug-ins' too, rather than switch notes among the melody's; **guitars**: one note per string, a new note on a string stopping the last; a strummer that turns a held chord into a strum, down or up, at a speed; articulations and legato for each note, bends, fret and release noises; and an amp and cabinet effect for electric guitars. Their recordings are the hard part: Karoryfer's Emilyguitar and Shinyguitar are CC0, but no well-sampled acoustic guitar is, so one is recorded for Phazite Sounds (a good guitar, a quiet room and thousands of edited recordings) or modelled. And three real instruments that sound better synthesized than sampled: a drawbar organ with a rotary speaker, an electric piano modelled after the tine and reed ones (the FM synth plays the DX7's), and plucked strings by physical modelling.
  - **Left out:** SoundFont (SF2) files, the older General MIDI sets, which sound dated; Kontakt's, Logic's and HALion's formats, which are closed (their instruments play in their own players, as plug-ins Phazite hosts); Decent Sampler's format, for now; recordings under CC BY, such as Salamander, or anything less free; and MPE controllers (after v1).
- **The devices of step 2.10 (2026-10-18, decided by Claude for the owner to review):** each a panel of its controls in the order the sound goes through them, with switches for what is on or off, until it gets an editor with a graph of its own; each passes DSP tests and a guarded real-time audit scenario, and waits for A/B listening against reference tools.
  - **Filter:** a resonant low-pass, high-pass, band-pass or notch at 12 or 24 dB per octave (the two sections of a fourth-order Butterworth, the second carrying the resonance), or Comb + or Comb - (step 2.14), which ring on after the sound, Vowel, Phaser or Sample & Hold (step 2.15), which have no slope, with a drive that saturates what goes in, at twice the sample rate and played by its antiderivative, so that it doesn't alias (since 2026-11-06; undriven, the sound takes a plain delay as long, so that the filter's latency stays the same). Its cutoff glides in octaves over about 10 ms, so lanes, modulators and knobs sweep it smoothly. A new filter changes little: a low-pass open all the way (20 kHz), at 24 dB and 10 % resonance.
  - **Multiband (one device since 2026-11-01, as the owner asked: "a single effect called multiband ... basically the same as FL Studio maximus. i want the same layout, the same abilities"; until then a multiband in OTT's style and a maximizer in Maximus's, which it replaces; `src/devices/dynamics/Multiband.h`):** the sound, through a low cut (20 Hz at first), splits into low, mid and high bands at two crossovers (120 Hz and 5 kHz at first) of 12 or 24 dB per octave (Linkwitz-Riley, which add back up flat: 24 at first).
