Each band goes through its stereo separation (mono to twice as wide), a pre gain, a compressor whose curve is drawn rather than set by a threshold and a ratio, a post gain and a saturation (two types, soft and firmer, each lowering its threshold as it is turned) under a ceiling, at twice the sample rate and played by its antiderivative, so that it doesn't alias (since 2026-11-06; every band goes that way, saturated or not, so that they stay in step); the bands, mixed with the sound as it came in by **LMH Mix** (Maximus's parallel processing), go through the **master band**'s same chain. The bands add up to the sound through an all-pass at each crossover (half a turn at a 24 dB crossover's frequency), so the sound they are mixed with goes through the same all-passes: otherwise half of each would cancel out there. The curves' axes are Maximus's: an amplitude at ln (1 + 2a) / ln 9, silence at one end, 0 dB in the middle and +12 dB at the other; each curve has up to eight points, the line before each bent by a tension or shaped as an S, a hold, stairs or smooth stairs, as FL Studio's envelope editor shapes them; at first every band is a limiter at 0 dB, Maximus's Default, so a new Multiband changes nothing quieter than that. Each compressor hears its band's peak, held for the **sustain** (20 ms at first), or its average over it (RMS); grips with its **attack**, through as many smoothing stages as its **curve** box (1 to 8) says, so a higher number starts more roundly; and lets go through two releases, **Release**, which speeds up and is done in its time (its own curve box how much), and **Release 2**, which slows into the gain, the two together an S, as the manual's figures show them. The bands look ahead by **LMH Delay** (up to 5 ms, 2 at first), the master by its attack (up to 5 ms), so that a band limits: the latency is always 10 ms, the most both look-aheads take, which Phazite's engine makes up for on the other tracks and the Low Latency switch bypasses. Each band is **on**, its **compression off**, **muted** or **off**: off, it passes as it came, and a low or high band off goes into the mid band, which then covers its room, as the manual's tutorials show; a band can be **soloed**. **Mid Drives Master** has the master's compressor hear the mid band instead of what it plays, the low and high bands' compressors off meanwhile (Maximus's Master Mid mode: ducking, gating, de-essing). No mix in its header: LMH Mix is its blend, before the master, which still limits what comes out. **Not built:** Maximus's oversampling, its linear-phase crossovers and its spare state (Phazite's undo and presets do the comparing) ([B126](#appendix-b-sources)).
  - **Saturator:** drives the sound (6 dB at first, from -24 to +36 dB: below 0 for a gentle bend on a loud sound, as Ableton's Saturator allows, since 2026-10-30) into one of five curves: soft (like tanh), tape (gentler), tube (lopsided, adding even harmonics, its DC filtered out), hard (clipping) or fold (peaks folded back); auto gain (on at first) takes the drive back off so quiet parts keep their level; a tone tilts it ±6 dB around 1 kHz; then an output gain. It runs at twice the sample rate through half-band filters and plays each curve by its antiderivative (first-order antiderivative antialiasing, since 2026-11-06, [dsp-audit.md](dsp-audit.md)), so that the harmonics it adds don't fold back as aliasing however hard it is driven; a short linear-phase filter after the curve keeps what it leaves straight level to 20 kHz.
  - **Delay:** echoes on a note value at the song's tempo (a dotted eighth at first) or in milliseconds, with feedback (35 %) that saturates softly so it never runs away, each echo darker and thinner through a low cut (100 Hz) and a high cut (8 kHz), and ping-pong, which bounces them between the sides. A change of time glides over about 50 ms, as a tape's would. It puts out the echoes alone; its mix starts at 25 % (a return's is all effect). An echo between samples is read by 4-point Lagrange interpolation, so that it keeps its treble pass after pass (since 2026-11-06: read linearly, it lost 2.4 dB at 10 kHz a pass at 44.1 kHz; now 0.7 dB).
  - **Stereo Expander (step 2.17, 2026-10-21, as the owner asked; Claude's choices for the owner to review):** wider or narrower without changing the mono sum. Width (0 to 200 %, through mid and side); Spread (0 at first), which makes stereo from the middle, for a mono sound, by adding to the side a copy of the middle smeared over 20 ms by velvet noise (Alary et al., 2017): wide, no delay, and since only the side changes, the left plus the right never does, unlike a Haas delay's comb in mono (iZotope's Stereoize works so); what it spreads is cut below the Bass Mono frequency at 24 dB per octave, so the bass stays in the middle. Bass Mono (off, 120 Hz) takes the side's bass away through a Linkwitz-Riley crossover, the middle through its matching all-pass so the two stay in phase, both running all the time so switching it crossfades; each side's polarity flips first, so the rest works on the sound put right, and a gain (±36 dB) follows. Balance went: a track's pan does it. It changes nothing at its defaults and has no mix; its picture is a half disc of what it plays, mono standing up and sides out of step lying flat, with a fan as wide as its width, over what comes in, and how alike the sides are (-1 to +1, red below 0, where the mono sum loses sound), as iZotope's Imager shows them.
  - **Phaser (step 2.17, 2026-10-21, as the owner asked; Claude's choices for the owner to review):** the sound mixed half and half with itself through all-pass filters whose phase an LFO sweeps, so that notches move up and down it, as the classic four-stage phasers (MXR's Phase 90) do. Notches, 1 to 6 (2 at first, the classic: a second-order all-pass each); Frequency, the sweep's centre (50 Hz to 10 kHz, 800 Hz); Depth, up to two octaves each way (60 %); the LFO in time with the song (a bar at first) or in hertz (0.05 to 20 Hz, 0.5), a sine or a triangle; Stereo, the right side up to half a cycle ahead (50 %); Feedback (-85 to 85 %, 30 %), which rings it as a filter's resonance does: above 0 at the peaks between the notches, below 0 at the notches themselves, which fill in and then turn into peaks, solved within each sample so that it never runs away; Spacing, the notches drawn together or spread apart (the all-passes' Q); Safe Bass (off, up to 1 kHz), which leaves what is below it as it was; and the mix. Its picture: its notches over the spectrum of what it plays, moving with the LFO, the left side's response lit and glowing down to the bottom so that each notch reads as a valley, the right side's fainter; how far the sweep reaches, along the bottom; and the LFO under it, one cycle with a dot for each side, as Ableton's Phaser-Flanger shows its LFO. A dot at the frequency, as high as the feedback, drags both; the wheel turns the depth.
  - **Flanger (the same):** the sound mixed with a copy of itself a few milliseconds behind, the delay swept, so that a comb of notches slides through it, the whoosh of tape flanging. Delay, the longest the copy lags (0.2 to 15 ms, 3 ms); Depth, how far the LFO shortens it, up to five octaves (70 %); the LFO as the phaser's (two bars at first, a triangle); Feedback (-100 to 100 %, 30 %): above 0 the comb rings at the multiples of the delay's frequency (metallic), below 0 it hollows out there instead (and takes the bass, which Safe Bass keeps); its notches are as deep at any feedback, and its peaks rise by up to 8.5 dB while what lies between them falls as much (the comb of the filter's Comb + and Comb -); its sign glides through 0 over 10 ms, so that a turn across it doesn't click. Then Stereo, Safe Bass and the mix. No through-zero flanging, which would need latency. Its picture: the comb over the spectrum, a tooth at each notch, and under it the delay as a tape's heads, how far the sweep reaches and where each side's copy lags now; a dot at the delay's frequency (the comb's first peak, or its first notch with the feedback below 0), as high as the feedback, drags the delay and the feedback, and the wheel turns the depth.
  - **Chorus (2026-11-08, the owner: in-house plugins that miss nothing; Claude's choices for the owner to review):** the effect every DAW ships, and what ten of the 27 classic sounds of the preset pass want ([presets.md](presets.md)): the sound with copies of itself a few milliseconds behind, each copy's delay swept so that its pitch wavers a little. **Classic**, a copy each side, the sides opposite at first, as the Juno-60's chorus (its mode I at first: 1.75 to 5.25 ms, a triangle at 0.5 Hz); **Ensemble**, three copies a side a third of a cycle apart, each with a sine ten times as fast and a tenth as deep on it, as the string machines' ensembles sweep theirs with a slow LFO and a fast one, 120 degrees apart. Delay, the middle of the sweep (1 to 25 ms, 3.5 ms); Depth, how far it goes each way, as a part of the delay (50 %); the LFO as the phaser's and the flanger's, but in hertz at first (0.5 Hz; a bar in time with the song), a triangle or a sine; Feedback (0 to 90 %), its peaks rising by as many decibels as what lies between them falls; Stereo; High Cut, which darkens the copies and what they feed back, as a bucket-brigade line does (off at first); Safe Bass; and the mix, half and half at first, at full only the copies, which in Classic is a vibrato (no mode of its own for that: one way per task). Three copies sound as loud together as one where they don't line up, most of the spectrum, so that Ensemble is as loud as Classic; where they line up, the deep bass, they are up to 4.8 dB louder, which Safe Bass keeps out. No latency. Its picture, as the flanger's: the comb its copies make with the sound now, over the spectrum, the left side's lit; under it the delay as a tape's heads, a head for each copy; then the LFO, a dot for each copy; a dot at the delay's frequency, as high as the depth, drags the delay and the depth, and the wheel turns the rate (in time with the song, the note value). Its presets: Chorus I, II and I + II, the Juno-60's three modes; String Ensemble and Lush Ensemble; Subtle Chorus, Doubler and Subtle Width; Vibrato, Tape Wobble and Metallic Chorus. The Flanger's chorus presets went to it.
  - **What the phaser, the flanger and the chorus share:** their LFOs start at the bottom of the sweep on the downbeat and, while the song plays, take their phase from where it is, so that a loop sweeps alike each time round and a render is the same each time; when the song jumps, they glide there over 10 ms; stopped, they run free.
  - **Vocoder (step 2.17, 2026-10-21, as the owner asked; Claude's choices for the owner to review):** put on a voice, it makes another sound speak with it, as the vocoders of Ableton Live, Bitwig, Cubase and Studio One do on the voice's track (FL Studio's Vocodex goes on the carrier instead). The voice, both sides together, is split into bands (8 to 40, 16 at first, from 100 Hz to 10 kHz, each two band-pass sections, as wide as Band Width says), each band's level followed (Attack 2 ms, Release 20 ms, never faster than two cycles of the band); the carrier is split into the same bands, each turned up and down by the voice's level in its band. Carriers: **Buzz**, a bright saw at C3 (Pitch moves it ±24 semitones: automate it to play a tune), so it speaks the moment it is put on a voice; **Noise**, a whisper; **Voice**, the voice itself, smeared; **Track**, the sound of the track picked as its sidechain (a synth's chords, for the classic robot choir; mute that track to hear only the vocoder, as a muted track still feeds sidechains), silent until one is picked, and its picture says so in the warning colour. Formant (±12 semitones) moves the carrier's bands, for a smaller or larger voice; Unvoiced (50 %) adds noise where the voice has consonants (more of it above 4 kHz than below), up to twice as loud as the carrier near 5 kHz, so that an "s" stays clear; Brightness tilts the bands by up to 3 dB an octave; Stereo sends the bands left and right in turn, its mono sum unchanged; Output; the mix. The carrier is evened out band by band, by the louder of each band and its neighbours (so that a lone harmonic isn't raised in the bands around it), and never by more than 12 dB over what pink noise as loud as the carrier has been lately would give the band (so that its tail, and the bands it hardly reaches, stay down): the vocoder is about as loud as the voice whatever the carrier (within 3 dB with the buzz, noise or the voice, and 4 dB with a quiet track, tested), and silent with it. Every other band plays upside down, so that neighbouring bands add up where they overlap rather than cancel, as filter banks need. No Flatten Carrier switch, as the research proposed: evening out band by band always does it, and a switch that flattened a buzz's few harmonics into its neighbours' bands came out 7 dB too loud. A change in the number of bands fades out and in over 10 ms. Its picture is a hardware vocoder's bank of lights: a column for each band, as high as the voice is in it, standing where the carrier plays the band, over a mark where it listens, so that Formant shows as the columns leaning away; what the carrier is, how loud it plays, and a light for consonants; a drag sideways moves Formant.
- **Comb filters (step 2.14, 2026-10-18, decided by Claude for the owner to review):** the owner asked for a comb filter wherever a filter type is chosen: in the synth, the wavetable synth and the Filter device (`src/devices/dsp/CombFilter.h`). The EQ's bands are an EQ's shapes, and no EQ the research looked at has a comb band. Comb filters are in Serum (comb and flange types in its Flanges category, [B103](#appendix-b-sources)), Vital (a Comb model with comb, positive and negative flange styles, [B99](#appendix-b-sources)), Surge XT (Comb + and Comb -, [B97](#appendix-b-sources)), Bitwig's Filter+ (with timed feedback and dampening; its Comb device has bipolar feedback, [B100](#appendix-b-sources)), Massive X (with a feedback polarity switch, [B101](#appendix-b-sources)), Pigments ([B102](#appendix-b-sources)) and Ableton's Auto Filter since Live 12.2 (peaks or notches, [B98](#appendix-b-sources)); FL Studio's Love Philter has none ([B104](#appendix-b-sources)). Each is a short delay, a period of the cutoff, fed back into itself, and most offer the feedback either way round. So Phazite's:
  - **Two types, Comb + and Comb -,** after the low-pass, high-pass, band-pass and notch. Comb + lets through the cutoff and its multiples (the harmonics of a note at that pitch) and cuts what lies halfway between; Comb - lets through its odd multiples only, hollow like a square wave, at the same pitch, as Vital's negative flange does. (Surge XT's negative comb sounds an octave lower, so its cutoff no longer says the pitch.)
  - **Cutoff and resonance only,** which every filter type has, rather than a damping or a mix of its own as Bitwig's and Massive X's have: one way per task. With no resonance it is a gentle comb of notches, as a flanger standing still makes; resonance feeds back up to 98 %, each step of the knob dividing what the loop loses by the same factor, until it rings at its pitch like a plucked string, for about 0.8 s at 440 Hz. Its peaks rise by up to 8.5 dB as they narrow, while broadband sound falls as much, so turning it up neither drowns a sound nor makes it jump out.
  - **Sweeps:** its delay glides with the cutoff, read between samples by 4-point Lagrange interpolation, which never adds gain, so the loop stays stable while it moves; envelopes, LFOs, modulation slots and lanes sweep it without clicks, into the flanging and talking sweeps combs are used for. Key tracking tunes it to each note in the synths. The Filter device reports how long it rings, so that a render waits for it.
- **More filter types (step 2.15, 2026-10-18, the owner's choice from [question 20](#25-open-questions)):** every filter type is now one piece of code that the synth, the wavetable synth and the Filter device share (`src/devices/dsp/MultimodeFilter.h`), so each offers the same types and each type sounds the same in all three. Since 2026-10-18 the three offer them as one list, in one order (low-pass, high-pass, band-pass, notch, Comb +, Comb -, Vowel, Phaser, Sample & Hold), each with a Slope beside it: the wavetable synth's Low Pass 12 and 24 and High Pass 12 and 24 became Low Pass and High Pass with its Slope (24 dB at first). The synth turns resonance into a 12 dB section's Q by a sharper curve of its own (from 0.5 to 25); the others from a Butterworth's 0.71 to 16.
  - **The synth's notch and slope:** a notch, and a Slope of 12 or 24 dB per octave (12 at first), as the Filter device has, so all three filters offer the same shapes.
  - **Vowel:** three band-passes at the first three formants of a sung vowel, at their levels and widths (a tenor's, from the Csound manual's table of formant values, [B105](#appendix-b-sources)). The cutoff is where the second formant is: from U (600 Hz) through O, A and E to I (1870 Hz) the formants glide from vowel to vowel, and beyond U or I they move with it, so an envelope or an LFO on the cutoff makes a sound talk, as Ableton's Vowel, Bitwig's Vowels and Vital's Formant do ([B98](#appendix-b-sources), [B100](#appendix-b-sources)). Resonance narrows the formants, from twice the table's width to half of it; a gain after them, larger as they narrow, keeps a saw within a few decibels of its level on average.
  - **Phaser:** six first-order all-pass stages, mixed with what goes in, whose three notches the cutoff moves (at about a quarter of it, at it and at nearly four times it), as the phasers of Serum, Pigments and Vital do ([B103](#appendix-b-sources), [B102](#appendix-b-sources)). Resonance feeds the stages back, up to 85 %, raising the peaks between the notches, the level kept within about 3.5 dB.
  - **Sample & Hold:** the sound held at twice the cutoff's rate, so that what lies above the cutoff folds back below it, aliasing on purpose, as Surge XT's Sample & Hold and Ableton's Resampling do ([B97](#appendix-b-sources), [B98](#appendix-b-sources)). Resonance feeds the held sound back with its sign turned, up to 60 %, ringing at the cutoff (12 dB more there at the most) while what is steady keeps its level.
  - Not built: Ableton's Morph and DJ, which need a control of their own, a peak, which an EQ's bell does already, and analog models, which are worth doing only well.
- **Sidechain:** a device that has one, such as the compressor, can listen to another track instead of the sound passing through it, to duck a bass to the kick (`src/model/mixer/internal/Sidechain.h`). A muted source still feeds it, so a "ghost" kick can duck without being heard. Only tracks whose sound doesn't come from the device's own track are offered, since a bus, a send or another sidechain in the way would make a loop that Phazite's engine can't play; deleting the source turns the sidechain off in the same undo step. A plug-in with sidechain inputs uses the same routing, chosen from its header (step 2.17).
- **A picture for every device (step 2.17, 2026-10-21, as the owner asked: "each plugin to have its own character", easy for beginners with some pro interactions, live where it shows what a knob does; Claude's choices for the owner to review):** each of Phazite's devices shows what it does, drawn as the sound plays, in its own colour by kind (the dynamics warm, the EQ and the filter cyan, the saturator red, the delay and the reverb blue; `DeviceFamily` in `DesignTokens.h`), its knobs' arcs in it too. Every drag on a picture is one undo step; each picture's handle is a second grip on a parameter that also has a knob, so everything works from the keyboard and screen readers.
  - **The frame** (`src/ui/devices/DeviceEditorBase.h`, `DeviceLayout.h`): an editor is columns of titled parts as the sound goes through them; it is as wide at any height, so a shorter dock never makes it wider: a column whose parts don't fit shows one at a time under tabs of their titles (the one chosen stays chosen), and a narrower space (the Instrument tab, a window) wraps the columns into rows instead. Knobs are compact (40 pixels), a device's one or two main controls large (64). An effect's mix is in its header, the Devices view's, for every effect and plug-in (step 2.10's effects had none in the view); its editor has none. One timer refreshes every editor that shows, 30 times a second, and none that doesn't, with the level meters in the same pass (`src/ui/components/DisplayTicker.h`); a picture repaints only when what it draws moved, so that with nothing playing and nothing turned, nothing repaints. What goes in and out of each device reaches its editor through a lock-free scope that costs nothing while no editor listens (`src/devices/core/DeviceScope.h`), checked by a guarded scenario of the [real-time audit](realtime-audit.md).
  - **Multiband** (since 2026-11-01, Maximus's layout, its colours and drawing Phazite's): at the top left the selected band's curve, square, level in across and level out up, in the band's colour (low red, mid orange, high yellow, master green, as Maximus colours them), its points dots and the tension handles between them hollow, the area under it shaded and, as Maximus's is, lit up to the level the band's compressor hears, so that the light moves with the sound (until the owner's third round a dot rode the curve instead, and every device graph drawn with Phazite's curve helper drew no line at all and a wedge for the area under it, from a bug: each point began a path of its own); under it, its menu (shapes to start from: Limit at 0 dB, No Compression, two compressors, a gate, an expander, a lift and a squeeze both ways; copy and paste, flip, smooth, reset), Freeze and Snap. Beside it the analysis display: **Monitor**, the last seconds of the selected band, what comes in, what comes into the band, what goes out, what its compressor hears, its gain, every band's gain and its pre and post levels, each by its switch below, scrolling at the speed set and paused by a click; or **Bands**, the bands across the spectrum, as high as their pre gains, the spectrum of what it plays over them. Below, the band tabs with Solo; the band's mode (On, Comp Off, Muted, Off) and stereo; Pre and Post; Sat and Ceiling; Attack, Release and Sustain, the curve boxes, Peak or RMS, and Release 2; LMH Delay and LMH Mix; the crossovers with their slopes, the low cut and Mid Drives. **Its drags are Maximus's (the owner: "all of the drag points should be the same (same interactions)"):** a point dragged moves (it snaps to the grid while Snap is on; Shift keeps it level, Ctrl keeps it where it is across); a tension handle dragged up or down bends the line before it (Ctrl in fine steps), and a right-click straightens it; a right-click on a point offers its line's shape and Delete, and Alt and a click deletes it; a right-click elsewhere adds a point there; on Bands, a band's edge drags its crossover and a band up or down its pre gain, and a crossover or pre gain changed under Monitor brings Bands up for a moment. These keys are Maximus's, not Phazite's elsewhere (where Shift drags in fine steps and Alt places off the grid), as the owner asked of this device; each edit is one undo step.
  - **Limiter:** the last four seconds, as FabFilter's Pro-L shows them: what comes in, pale, what goes out, in its colour, and red icicles hanging from the top where it turned the sound down, the deepest numbered; the ceiling a line to drag; In, GR and Out meters beside it. **Compressor:** its curve with the dot riding it, and a slim meter of how far it turns the sound down (its history beside them went on 2026-11-01).
  - **EQ:** as before, with what comes in drawn faint behind what it plays, and the band's controls beside the curve rather than under it, so it fits a short dock without scrolling. **Filter:** its response lit over the spectrum it plays (faint, what comes in), so each type looks like itself, a vowel's formants or a comb's teeth; a dot at the cutoff, as high as the resonance, drags both; the wheel turns the drive.
  - **Saturator:** its curve lit as far as the sound reaches into it, so turning the drive up shows the sound pushing into the bend, as Ableton's Saturator shows it, and beside it the wave coming in and going out, held still on the wave's start, so the rounded or folded tops show; five buttons, each drawn with its curve, pick the shape; a drag up the curve turns the drive.
  - **Delay:** the sound coming in as a bar, then its echoes after it on the song's beats, fading with the feedback, above and below the middle in turn when they ping-pong, as FabFilter's Timeless shows them; what plays lights each echo in its turn. A drag puts the first echo under the pointer: across for the time (a note value while synced), up for the feedback. Under them, the band its echoes keep, between the low and the high cut, whose ends drag. **Reverb:** a map of its tail, time across and pitch up, each pitch lit until it has faded, as Pro-R shows its decay: long in the bass, shorter past the damping, after the pre-delay's gap, nothing below the low cut; hits drift into it and fade. Its edges drag: the end for the decay, the gap for the pre-delay, the slant's corner for the damping, the floor for the low cut.
  - **Drum Sampler:** the pads fit the dock's height (44 pixels at least), each with its choke group in its corner; the selected pad's sound drawn, with its attack's ramp and its length's cut to drag, and a line where it plays.
  - **Synth** (the owner: its controls ran far off to the side): three columns, Oscillators (one cycle of what the two oscillators, the sub and the noise play together), Unison and Voice; Filter (its response, which follows the newest note as its envelope and LFO move it, with a dot to drag) and Filter Envelope; Amp and LFO, each envelope and the LFO drawn, with how far the newest note has got. About 780 pixels wide at the default dock's height, where it ran over 3,000; in a dock 500 pixels tall, every part shows. **Wavetable:** its oscillators (each its table, drawn as before), Sub and Noise and Unison under tabs; its filter's response; and a list down the side of its three envelopes, two LFOs, its modulation matrix (a row a slot) and its voice settings, as Ableton's Wavetable keeps its modulation. About 950 pixels wide, where it ran over 6,000.
  - **Pictures as controls (2026-11-01, as the owner asked: the pictures of envelopes, compressors and the like should be "interactable": "move the key points around" and "scroll on lines in order to bend them", where the device has that):** the synths' envelopes drag by their points, as Ableton's envelopes drag by their breakpoints: the peak sideways for the attack, the corner where the decay ends sideways for the decay and up and down for the sustain, the end sideways for the release; a double-click puts a point back, and the picture keeps its scale while a point moves, so the point stays under the pointer. **Since the owner's second round (2026-11-01), each stage bends:** an Attack, Decay and Release Curve each, from -100 % (fast at first) through 0 (straight) to 100 % (slow at first), at first the analog shape the stages always had, so no sound or preset changed; a hollow handle in the middle of each stage that rises or falls drags up or down to bend it, as Vital's, FabFilter Twin's, Ableton Operator's and FL Studio's envelopes bend theirs, and a double-click on a stage puts its curve back. Each curve has a knob too, under the picture. The synth's LFO picture sets its depth with a drag up or down (the wave drawn as tall as it moves) and its rate with the wheel; the wavetable synth's LFOs, their rate or note value. The compressor's curve is the Multiband's since the owner's third round, with Maximus's drags (until then a ring at the threshold dragged across, the line above it dragged for the ratio as Kilohearts' Dynamics does, and the line below it for the makeup). The stereo expander's fan opens to the pointer's angle, and the wheel opens or closes it. The breakpoint envelope modulator, the one picture besides automation lanes whose lines have curves to bend, shows its points over its cycle: each point drags, a handle in each line's middle bends it up or down, as Ableton's diamonds between breakpoints set their slope, and a double-click straightens it, as Alt and a double-click do in Ableton's automation ([B119](#appendix-b-sources)). **The wheel bends no line (2026-11-01):** the owner asked that scrolling on a line bend it, and to find out first what other plug-ins and DAWs do. None of about 40 envelope editors and 25 compressors checked bends a line with the wheel: most bend a segment by a handle in its middle (Vital, Ableton's Operator and Meld, FL Studio, Pigments' functions, Surge XT's MSEG, FabFilter Twin) or by dragging the segment itself (Logic's Alchemy, HALion, Zebra, Phase Plant's curves), and over a graph the wheel zooms or scrolls; on a compressor's graph a few let the threshold and ratio be dragged (Kilohearts' Dynamics, Ozone, Vital, Ableton's Compressor) and none turns anything with the wheel ([B123](#appendix-b-sources)). So Phazite's lines bend by their handles, and the wheel over a picture scrolls the view it is in, which a wheel that bent lines would have taken away. Each drag is one undo step, and so is each turn of the wheel until it rests for half a second (a press ends it); Shift drags in fine steps, as on knobs.

**Plug-ins that aren't Phazite's own (the owner, 2026-10-16, [question 6](#25-open-questions)):** none is built into Phazite, however good or freely licensed, such as Airwindows' effects (MIT) or Surge XT (GPL-3.0). Phazite may offer some as optional downloads, as it offers Phazite Sounds, for people who want them; its own devices then keep one design language, which the owner can make stricter later.

**Containers:**
- Select some devices and choose "Group into container". The container shows its devices in a row, with up to eight macro knobs that can drive any parameter inside, and saves as one preset. This is like Reason's Combinator and Ableton's racks.
- Parallel chains inside a container come later.

**Presets:**
- device presets, for built-ins and for plug-in state;
- track presets: drag a track into the browser to save its device chain and settings, as in Studio One;
- container presets.

**Containers as built (step 2.4, 2026-10-16; Claude's choices for the owner to review, each what most DAWs do):**
- **Grouping, as in Ableton Live and Bitwig:** Ctrl-click (or Shift-click) devices' names in a channel strip to select them (a plain click still opens a device's window), then Group into Container: Ctrl+G, which groups tracks into a folder while tracks are selected, or right-click a device. The selected effects go into a container in the first one's place, in their order on the channel, keeping their settings, automation and what the track's modulators do to them; a device right-clicked without being selected is grouped alone. Ungroup Container (Ctrl+Shift+G, or the container's right-click) puts them back. Each is one undo step. Devices are selected on their own: selecting one deselects tracks and clips, and the other way round.
- **Effects only for now:** an instrument can't go in a container (instrument containers, Ableton's Instrument Rack, come later), nor can a container (containers don't nest). A device in a container hears the sound passing through it: grouping one whose sidechain listened to another track turns that off (later: sidechains in containers).
- **Its slot** in the strip shows its name, its mix (a blend of the sound coming in with its devices', which Phazite's engine delays to match the devices' latency) and its first macros as knobs; a click shows it in the Devices view (it had a window of its own until 2026-10-17). **There** it shows its name and eight macros on the left, as Ableton's racks show theirs, and its devices in a row on the right, with "+ Effect" after them. Double-click a name to rename it.
- **Eight macros, always there**, as Ableton's racks and Bitwig's remote controls show eight: "Macro 1" to "Macro 8", each at 0 when made, so that a parameter it modulates doesn't move until the macro is turned (a track's macros, from step 2.3, now start at 0 too). Drag a macro's grip onto a knob of one of the container's devices, or right-click such a knob > Modulate, where the container's macros come before the track's modulators; a macro moves only its container's devices. The track's modulators reach the devices in its containers too.
- **A container's devices are devices:** they turn on and off, move (Move Left and Right), save presets, are automated in lanes under the track, and are modulated, as any device is.
- **Each container is its own:** a duplicated track's containers are copies, which the copy's own modulators and macros modulate.

**Presets as built (step 2.4, 2026-10-16; Claude's choices for the owner to review):**
- **One kind of file, `.phazitepreset`,** in `Documents/Phazite/Presets`: a folder for each device's presets ("EQ", or a plug-in's name), Containers and Tracks, as Ableton's User Library keeps presets by device. The browser shows that folder as a Presets place after Effects, each preset with what it holds ("EQ", "Container", "Track"), and search finds them. A preset is written in Phazite's own format (since 2026-10-30, roadmap A5): JSON naming one of Phazite's devices by its type and every parameter by its ID, a plug-in by its format, name, maker and identifier with the settings it gives itself, and containers, modulators and macros as Phazite names them, with what they modulate by where it is (the instrument, an effect, a device in a container, the volume or pan); the model makes the song's elements from it as it loads, with new IDs, so a change in how the song keeps a device can't break the user's presets (`Schema.h`: `schema::preset`). Like projects, presets carry no format version while Phazite is pre-alpha ([section 18](#18-projects-saving-and-export)).
- **Saving:** a device's or container's right-click > Save Device Preset..., or a track's right-click > Save Track Preset..., opens a save dialog in its folder, named after it, as FL Studio's "Save preset as" does. Dragging a track by its handle out of the playlist into the browser saves it at once in Tracks, named after it, as in Studio One, and the browser shows the new preset.
- **What a preset holds:** a device's settings and mix (a plug-in's whole state), not its automation, what modulates it from outside the preset, its sidechain's source or whether it is on (a preset loads on). A container's devices and macros with what they modulate in it, its mix and name. A track's name, color, volume and pan, instrument, effects and containers, and its modulators and macros with what they modulate on it; not its clips, automation, sends or routing (Studio One's and Cubase's track presets leave clips out too). A drum sampler's sound files go by their full paths, and a project that takes the preset copies them into its Audio folder; its kit sounds, as the wavetable synth's tables, by their names.
- **Loading:** drag a preset onto a track or a mixer channel, or press Enter (or double-click) for the selected track, as a device: a device's or container's goes where that device would (after the effects; an instrument in place of the track's, or on a new track), a container named after its preset; below the tracks, an effect's goes on the master. A track's preset makes a new track after the one it is dropped on, or at the end. Each is one undo step, "Add" and the preset's name, with new IDs throughout, so a container's macros modulate the devices that came with them. Preset files dropped from Explorer load the same way. **Into a device (2026-10-30):** a device's **Presets** button, in its card's header in the Devices view while the card is open (an instrument's always), lists Phazite's presets for it by kind of sound, then the user's, and loads the one chosen into that device in place of its settings, every parameter the preset doesn't name at its default, so that a preset is the same starting point whatever the device was set to (2026-10-30, as the owner asked), as one undo step ("Load Preset Warm Pad"), as FL Studio's preset arrows and Ableton Live's Hot-Swap do; Save Preset... is there too. A plug-in's window's Load Preset does the same (step 2.12, below). **Later:** a preset dropped on a device of its kind replaces its settings, and renaming presets in the browser.
- **Phazite's own presets (2026-10-30, as the owner asked: effects with presets, and instruments with presets for a wide variety of styles):** data files in Phazite's preset format, in `resources/presets/<device>/<kind of sound>/`, which the build copies next to the app (as it copies the fonts) and the browser shows as a **Phazite Presets** place before the user's Presets, searchable with them; nothing writes to them. The instruments have presets across styles (bass, lead, pad, pluck, keys, effects; the wavetable synth's moving tables, growls and bells; the drum sampler's kits, each with sixteen synthesized sounds of its own), the effects the settings producers reach for (an EQ's low cut and presence, a compressor's vocal leveler and drum glue, a reverb's room, hall and plate, a delay's dotted eighth and slapback, and so on), drafted from what each device's parameters do, each naming every parameter of its device (2026-10-30: the first ones named only those they changed, so loading one left the rest as the device had them), and checked by tests that each is written as Save Preset writes it, names only parameters its device has, within their ranges, and plays: sound comes out, only numbers, never far over full scale (`tests/FactoryPresetTests.cpp`). They want listening by the owner. **The pass of 2026-11-08 ([presets.md](presets.md); the owner: "i don't want our presets (or our in-house plugins for that matter) to be missing anything, or for people to turn away from our presets because they don't sound correct for what they expect it to sound like"):** each instrument's presets compared with what Ableton's, Logic's, Bitwig's, Serum's, Vital's, Diva's, FM8's, Dexed's and the drum samplers' factory libraries ship, the classic instruments' sounds added from their factory patches and circuits, named by their sound rather than a trademark, measured by `tests/PresetReport.cpp` (what was wrong, fixed: a lead that faded out and in, a pluck 15 dB quieter than the rest, no synth preset brighter for a harder note, FM basses an octave below the note), filed under the kinds the others use (brass, strings, mallets, winds and percussion of their own), and leveled alike: one note's loudness toward its kind's, and no note or chord over -1 dBFS, which the tests check.

**Plug-in windows:** each plug-in's own interface opens in its own window, kept above the main window. Phazite's own devices show in the Devices view, and in a window of their own when the user wants one (2026-10-21; [section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets)); containers only in the Devices view.
- **Where it was (step 2.12, 2026-10-18, decided by Claude for the owner to review):** a plug-in's window opens where it was last, in the same session and when the project opens again; one last seen on a screen that isn't there any more comes back on the nearest. Its place is view state, as track heights are: saved with the project, never an undo step or an unsaved change.
- **Its menu (step 2.12):** a menu bar with one menu, **Plug-in**, as FL Studio's plug-in wrapper has a menu of its own with the plug-in's presets ([B85](#appendix-b-sources)). **Save Preset...** saves the plug-in's settings and mix as a device preset (as Save Device Preset does from its slot), in a dialog that starts in its folder of the presets, named after it; **Load Preset** lists the presets saved for it, in that folder and the folders in it, and puts the one chosen into this plug-in in place of its settings and mix, as one undo step ("Load Preset" and its name), as FL Studio's preset arrows step the open plug-in through its presets and Ableton Live's Hot-Swap swaps a device's preset for another of the same device ([B86](#appendix-b-sources)); and **Send All Keys to the Plug-in** (below).

**Keys over plug-in windows (2026-10-13):** (a built-in device's window, Phazite's own, has every shortcut, as the main window has) play and stop (and pause), and the computer keyboard while it plays notes, work while a plug-in's window has the keyboard focus, so a sound can be played while it is tweaked (`src/ui/input/PluginKeys.h`; commands opt in with `Command::overPlugins`). Every other key goes to the plug-in. DAWs differ here: Bitwig gives a focused plug-in every key, REAPER and FL Studio have a per-plug-in switch (FL Studio's Typing Keyboard Priority, [B85](#appendix-b-sources)), and Ableton Live keeps Space on Windows. On Windows, a plug-in's native window gets keys before JUCE sees them, so a message hook on the message thread catches these keys on their way to it. The hook is installed at startup, before any plug-in window: Windows calls the hook installed last first, and a plug-in built with JUCE installs its own hook, which offers each key to its focused control (a preset name being typed) and passes on the keys it doesn't use. A key typed into a native text field goes to the plug-in too. **Decided (2026-10-16):** a "Send All Keys to the Plug-in" switch in each plug-in window's menu, as REAPER and FL Studio have, for plug-ins whose own text fields Phazite can't see; off at first, and remembered for that plug-in in every project (step 2.12, [question 16](#25-open-questions)). **Built (2026-10-18):** while it is on, every key typed into that plug-in's window goes to the plug-in, so a name can be typed into a field it draws itself, and play, stop and the computer keyboard don't work there; the switch is kept in the settings for that plug-in (by its format, maker and name), not in the project, so it is on in every window of that plug-in, in every project.

**Track limit:** none: a track holds as many devices as it needs. FL Studio's 10 effect slots per mixer track is a known complaint ([B12](#appendix-b-sources)).

## 10. Automation and modulation

Producers in Phazite's audience automate "all sorts of sliders", so this has to be effortless.

**Right-click any control,** built-in or a plug-in parameter, and choose:
- **Automate:** an automation lane appears under the track, ready to draw.
- **Modulate:** adds an LFO, envelope follower, step sequence, random source, breakpoint envelope or note follower. It shows as a ring on the control; drag the ring to set the amount.
- **MIDI learn.**

A hotkey automates the last-touched control (FL Studio's "last tweaked", Cubase 15).

**Lanes:**
- draw with the pencil, or use line and curve shapes (sine, ramp, steps);
- bend segments; select and move points;
- edit points from the keyboard;
- write modes per track: read, touch, latch and write.

**Lanes as built (step 2.1, 2026-10-16; Claude's choices for the owner to review, each what most DAWs do):**
- A lane shows under its track in the playlist, as in Ableton Live, Bitwig, Logic, Cubase and Studio One: a header with the track's colour down its left, the parameter (in a lane without automation, a button as wide as its name, which chooses another of the track's: its volume, pan and sends, then its devices') and the track's write mode; and the curve, drawn as the engine plays it, in the track's color. Lanes are view state, saved with each track ([section 18](#18-projects-saving-and-export)): showing or collapsing one is no undo step. The master's lanes show under a Master row below the tracks while it has some, as Live shows its main track.
- **Automation never hides (the owner's choice, 2026-10-17):** every parameter with automation has a lane, even one written while no lane showed (by recording), so nothing plays from an unseen place, as Bitwig deletes a lane's automation when its lane goes. To make room, a lane collapses to a thin row that still draws its line, by a double-click on its header (as a track's header collapses, since 2026-11-03; a chevron did it until then), by dragging its bottom edge up, or with its track: a collapsed track's lanes are thin rows too, and a double-click on the master's row does the same for the master's lanes. A double-click on a thin lane or its header expands it (and its track, if the track kept it thin). A lane goes only with its automation: its menu's Delete Automation (Remove Lane, for a lane without automation) is one undo step, and an undo brings the lane back with the automation. Focus mode is the one view that leaves lanes out, those of the tracks out of focus.
- **Two clicks:** right-click any of Phazite's knobs or faders (the mixer's, the inspector's, a device's in the Devices view) → Automate; its lane shows, its track opens, and the playlist scrolls to it. A plug-in's own window is the plug-in's, so its parameters are automated from its slot in the mixer (right-click → Automate → the parameter), from a lane's parameter menu, or by moving the knob in the plug-in's window and pressing Ctrl+Shift+A, Automate Last Touched Parameter (FL Studio's "last tweaked", Cubase's last-touched control), which works over plug-in windows.
- **The tools in a lane (made simpler at the owner's request, 2026-10-17: the Smart Tool needs no keys):** a click adds a point, on the grid; pressed on the line, the new point sits on the line, so nothing sounds different until a drag pulls it, as Studio One's arrow tool does. Dragging a point moves it with the other selected points; selected points show larger, in the text color with an accent ring, on a track of any color. The line under the mouse shows a hollow handle in its middle, as Studio One's and FL Studio's do: dragging it up or down bends the line, no further than where it still runs from point to point, so the points stay put. A double-click deletes a point, or straightens a line at its handle, and a drag on empty space selects the points in a rectangle. Keys only refine, with the meanings they have everywhere in Phazite: Alt places off the grid, Shift moves in fine steps (as on knobs and faders), and Ctrl adds to the selection or takes out of it. Draw draws a step for each grid cell it passes, as Live's draw mode does, or a free line with Alt. Paint draws a shape between where its drag starts and ends, a cycle a grid step long, as high and low as it drags (sine, or from the lane's menu: triangle, square, saw up, saw down or a line), as Studio One's paint tool does. Delete deletes the points it passes; Select selects. With FL Studio's keymap a right-click deletes points, as it deletes clips; otherwise it shows the lane's menu.
- **Keys:** the arrow keys raise and lower the selected points by a hundredth of the range (with Shift a tenth) while points are selected, as they move selected notes; nudging moves them a grid step; Delete and Duplicate act on them. A expands the selected tracks' lanes, or collapses them all while one shows expanded, as Live and Logic Pro show and hide automation with A (a track without lanes gets one for its volume). Automate Last Touched is Ctrl+Shift+A, not Shift+A, because Shift and a letter must run the letter's own shortcut while the computer keyboard plays notes.
- **Write modes** are each track's, as in Logic Pro, Pro Tools, Cubase and Studio One (Live has one arm button instead), and Read by default; changing one is no undo step, as mute and solo aren't, and a mode that writes shows in red in the lane's header. Phazite's engine writes them (`src/model/playback/internal/AutomationWriter.h`, since 2026-11-02): Read plays the lanes, and a control moved by hand goes back to its lane at the lane's next change; Touch writes while a control is held, and the lane takes over again when it is let go; Latch writes from a control's first move until playback stops; Write overwrites every automated parameter of the track from the moment playback starts until its control is let go, and then the track is in Latch, as Pro Tools can switch after a write pass. What a pass records is one undo step, "Record Automation", or the step of the control being dragged.
- The song keeps automation in beats, as it keeps clips, so it stays on its beats when the tempo or the time signature changes. What automation plays changes nothing in the song.

**No automation clips (the owner's decision, 2026-10-17):** lanes are the one way to automate. Step 2.2 had built FL Studio's automation clips; trying them, the owner found that they lay over the track's own clips, hiding them and catching the mouse, and that they broke the rule that everything in a track's row belongs to its instrument, since a clip moved anywhere still set what it was made for. Asked whether any DAW has both, the research found two philosophies:
- **Lanes:** Ableton Live, Bitwig, Logic Pro, Cubase, Studio One, Pro Tools and REAPER keep automation with its parameter, in lanes under the parameter's track.
- **Free clips:** FL Studio (and LMMS), whose playlist rows aren't tied to an instrument, which is what lets a clip go anywhere. Phazite's rows are tracks with instruments, so the model didn't fit.
- **Where both exist, the second kind is tied down:** Ableton's clip envelopes, Bitwig's clip automation, Logic's region automation and Studio One's part automation live inside the instrument's own clips; REAPER's automation items live inside a parameter's own lane, and so do the automation clips Bitwig 6 added in 2026, each linked to the note or audio clip it belongs to, which it follows when that clip is copied or moved (Bitwig's release notes for 6.0; Sound On Sound's review). None lets a clip on one track set another's parameter.
- **FL Studio's own users ask for lanes:** "collapsible automation lanes attached directly to tracks" (Image-Line forum, March 2026), and that new clips stop landing "on any random track" (February 2026).
- **What clips do well** is go with the music when parts are rearranged (KVR, "The automation problem and which DAWs have a solution?", 2022). Lane DAWs do that by moving automation with its clips: Ableton Live ("Live normally moves all automation with the clip"), Bitwig (Automation Follow, on by default) and Studio One (Automation Follows Events, on by default); Cubase, Logic and Pro Tools have it as a setting.

So Phazite keeps lanes, which are also the lightest for the engine, and gives up linked automation (aliases sharing a curve), which no lane DAW but REAPER has.

**Automation goes with the music (2026-10-17):** moving or copying clips along their track (dragging, Ctrl+drag, nudging, Duplicate) takes the automation of the track's parameters under them along, in the same undo step, in place of what is where it lands, as Ableton Live, Bitwig and Studio One do by default; so do deleting clips with Close Gap, which takes their time out of the track, and deleting or duplicating a time range selected across tracks (and their folders). Each stretch keeps its shape at its edges wherever it goes, and where a moved stretch was, the line runs straight across. A clip moved to another track leaves its automation, since that track's devices are others, and a clip deleted without closing its gap leaves it too. A drag moves the automation once, when the drag ends. Automation on a folder or the master doesn't follow the clips of the tracks inside (the shortcoming users find in Cubase's Automation Follows Events): a selected time range across the folder carries it.

**Modulators and macros as built (step 2.3, 2026-10-16; Claude's choices for the owner to review):**
- **Each track's, as Cubase 15's modulators are:** a track, a folder and the master each have modulators of their own (an LFO, an envelope follower of how loud the track is, a step sequencer, a random source, a breakpoint envelope, and a note follower of the pitch or velocity of the notes it plays) and macros, knobs that move several parameters at once (on tracks and returns only). They modulate the parameters of the track's own devices and its volume, pan and sends. The song holds them (`MODULATOR`, `MACRO`) and Phazite's engine plays them (`src/model/modulation/ModulationModel.h`, `src/engine/modulation/ModulatorNode.h`).
- **Two ways to assign one:** right-click a knob → Modulate → one of its track's modulators (ticked while it modulates the knob; choosing it again stops that), or New LFO … New Macro, which adds one already modulating the knob, as one undo step. Or drag a modulator by its grip, from the Devices view's first column (a channel strip's Modulators section until 2026-10-17), onto a knob of one of its track's devices, as Bitwig drags modulators. **What a drag shows (2026-11-01, as the owner asked: nothing showed that the right part was dragged until a knob was reached):** the grip is in the modulation colour and lights under the pointer; once dragged, a chip with the modulator's name follows the pointer (right of and below it, so the knob under it stays in view), the slot it comes from is outlined, and every knob it can modulate shows a faint ring from the start of the drag, in every window, the one under the pointer a full ring over a wash, as Vital rings every valid target as a drag starts and FabFilter highlights every modulation target ([B117](#appendix-b-sources)). A modulation starts at a quarter of the parameter's range, and moves the parameter around where its knob is set: both ways for a bipolar modulator (LFOs, random sources and step sequencers start bipolar), the other way for a negative amount.
- **The ring:** a modulated knob shows a ring in the modulation color: the range its modulations can move it over, and a dot where it is now, while the knob itself shows its own value (or its automation's), as in Bitwig. Dragging the ring (the knob's outer edge) up or down changes its first modulation's amount, with Shift in fine steps, as one undo step; the rest of the knob turns it as before.
- **Editors:** a click on a modulator's chip pops its editor out over the window, beside it (2026-11-06; in line beside the Devices view's column from 2026-10-17 until then), as it opens when the modulator is added: its name and On switch, its output over the last two seconds, a knob for each of its parameters (rate, wave, depth, polarity, sync...), and a step sequencer's steps, drawn with the mouse, one undo step per drag. A macro's slot is its knob. Right-click a modulator's name to stop it modulating a parameter, or to remove it, with what it modulates.
- What modulation plays is no unsaved change, and steady playback of every kind allocates nothing on the audio thread (a guarded scenario of the [real-time audit](realtime-audit.md)). Modulators' own parameters can't be automated or modulated yet.
- **On and off (2026-10-21, as the owner asked; Claude's choices for the owner to review):** each modulator has an On switch in its editor (in its slot until 2026-11-06, when the slots became chips) and its right-click menu, as Max for Live's LFO and Cubase's modulators have, to hear a sound with and without it without taking it apart. Off, it moves nothing, and keeps what it modulates and by how much; its meter goes faint, and so do the rings of knobs that only modulators switched off modulate (showing where they would move them, with no dot). Switching is one undo step ("Turn Modulator Off"), saved with the project, as a device's switch is; presets load modulators on, as they load devices. A macro has no switch: it is a knob, turned down to do nothing.

**How the two layers combine:**
- the lane sets the base value, and modulators move around it;
- each control shows both: its automated value and the modulation range.

**Engine notes:**
- **Modulation moves parameters on the song's control points (roadmap A5, part 4, 2026-10-30, for Phazite's own devices; for everything modulated since 2026-11-02):** Phazite's engine works out each modulator's value on control points, one every 32 samples from the song's start, the stretch the devices' parameters already glide in (`src/engine/modulation/ModulatorNode.h`, `src/devices/modulation/Modulators.h`), rather than once an audio block, which is coarse for fast modulation and sounds different at each buffer size; an LFO by its own wave, so a square or a sixteenth-note wobble keeps its edges on time and the song plays the same at any buffer size. A parameter moves from the value automation leaves (`src/devices/modulation/Modulation.h`). Plug-ins' parameters are moved on the same points, each change handed to the plug-in at its sample (`src/model/plugins/internal/SandboxedProcessor.h`), and so are the faders' volume and pan, which keeps an LFO moving a plug-in, a device and a fader in step. **For each note, next:** modulation is added in a parameter's range as its control spans it (`devices::modulate()`), one sum for the whole device; an instrument will move a parameter for each voice with the same function and a sum for each voice, as CLAP's polyphonic modulation does, starting with the note follower (each note's own pitch and velocity) and envelopes started by each note;
- parameters inside containers can't be recorded live yet.

## 11. The browser

**Built in step 1.5 (2026-10-13)** (`src/ui/browser/Browser.h`, `src/services/Library.h`, `src/model/engine/Preview.h`, `src/ui/browser/PluginPicker.h`):

- **One browser, two ways in:**
  - **a tree of places**, as FL Studio's browser has it: Favorites, Recent, Collections (2026-11-07), Phazite Sounds (the free sounds, once downloaded), Instruments, Effects, then your own folders as they are on disk (FL Studio users ask other DAWs for this), and **Add Folder...**. One click opens a folder, as in FL Studio. The folders open when Phazite closes are open again at the next start;
  - **search** across your sounds and the devices, as you type, best matches first (names that start with what you typed, then names with a word that does). Ctrl+F (Phazite's keymap) puts the cursor in the search field; Down or Enter moves to the results. The browser's icon doesn't: it gives the browser itself the keys, so that Space plays on while it is looked through (2026-11-07, the owner: "when i click the icon to open the browser, im automatically highlighting and entering text into the search bar ... spacebar is now taken by the text input of the search bar"; [section 12](#12-commands-keys-and-help)).
- **Tags** come from the words in a sound's name and folders: what it is (Kick, Snare, Clap, Hat, Cymbal, Tom, Percussion, Bass, FX, Foley, Vocal, Synth), Loop or One-shot, its tempo ("120 BPM") and, since 2026-11-07, its key ("F# minor", or a note alone for a one-shot tuned to it). Search finds them, so "kick loop" finds a kick loop in a folder called Loops. A background thread indexes the sounds (up to 50,000), first by their names, then again with what their loop data say (below).
- **Preview:**
  - click a sound, or move to it with the arrow keys, to hear it; Esc stops it. A click on a loop that plays stops it, as in Logic Pro's Loop Browser, and a one-shot clicked again plays again from its start. Starting or stopping the song stops a preview: the transport plays the song, as FL Studio's Stop ends a preview (2026-11-02, the owner: loops "dont stop when i start playing the track, and i cant just click the sample again to stop"). The headphones switch under the tree turns previews off, and a fader sets how loud they play;
  - loops play in time with the project's tempo, stretched as their clips would be, by Rubber Band's R3 engine (since 2026-10-28), as Ableton Live (unless Raw is on), Logic Pro, Studio Pro and FL Studio play them; one-shots play as they are, never stretched, as in every DAW that documents it (research of 2026-11-02). While the song plays, a loop started then plays on its bars, from where the song is in it. Phazite knows a loop by its loop data (a WAV file's acid chunk, an AIFF file's Apple loop data), or by a tempo in its name ("Break 120 BPM") when it lasts whole bars at that tempo (`src/model/engine/AudioFiles.h`). Effects made for some bars ("Riser 4 Bars 128 BPM") play in time but once. One-shots play at their own pitch. MIDI files play on Phazite's synth;
  - **the sound selected, under the tree (2026-11-02):** its waveform, and a line of what it is: a loop, at what tempo and how many bars, or a one-shot; how long it lasts, to the hundredth of a second; its sample rate, bits and channels, as FL Studio's browser, Bitwig's and Ableton Live's show a file before it goes into the song;
  - loops keep their own pitch for now: pulling every loop toward one pitch for the whole song would pull it toward C in a song without a key, and ignore major and minor (an A minor loop would go up to C in C major). **Later:** in key, once songs have a key: the selected track's, treating relative major and minor as the same key, as Logic Pro does;
  - **later:** previews through the selected track's devices, and tempo detection for loops whose names don't say.
- **Putting things into the song:**
  - **drag and drop:** onto empty space below the tracks, a new track; onto a track, a clip at the grid line before the pointer, or a device; onto a mixer channel, a device; onto the master, an effect. An instrument dropped on a track that can't hold one (a folder, a return, a track with audio clips) gets a new track next to it, named and colored after it. onto one of the drum sampler's pads, in its editor, the pad's sound (step 1.6). **Later:** onto a device slot replaces it;
  - **Enter or a double-click** puts a sound on the selected track at the play start (where Space plays from), or on a new track; a device goes onto the selected track.
- **Plug-in picker (F8, as in FL Studio):** type part of an instrument's or effect's name or maker, then press Enter (or click it) to add it to the selected track, or to a new one. Instruments come before effects. Phazite's keymap moves the browser's show-and-hide key to Ctrl+Alt+B, Ableton Live's; FL Studio's keymap keeps Alt+F8.
- **Favorites and recent items:** the star at the end of a row (on the selected row, and filled on favorites) or the right-click menu makes an item a favorite. Recent lists the last 50 items put into the song. Collections, named sets, came on 2026-11-07 (below).
- **A type's sounds from every library** (all the kicks, Phazite's and the user's), as Ableton Live's labels and Bitwig's "Drum Hits" list them, are what the Type filter shows (below). Sounds by Type, a place with a place of its own for each type, did it until the filters came, and went then (2026-11-07), for one way to find kicks.
- **Phazite Sounds (2026-10-13):** 85 free sounds for a new user to start with (9.3 MB of FLAC): acoustic and electronic drums, 808s, loops at 90 to 174 BPM, risers, impacts and other effects, and foley.
  - **An optional download, not part of Phazite.** They live in their own public repository, [phazite-sounds](https://github.com/august-byrne/phazite-sounds), whose releases hold the pack as a zip, so Phazite's repository and installer stay small and contributing a sound never touches Phazite's code. Phazite offers them once, in the welcome window ([section 13](#13-getting-started-and-song-outlines)), and the browser's Phazite Sounds place has a **Download** row until they are installed; Help > Phazite Sounds offers them again. This follows Bitwig's and Studio One's sound packages, which install after the program, rather than FL Studio's bundled library; experienced users skip them.
  - **Download (`src/services/SoundPack.h`):** only when the user asks, over HTTPS from GitHub, on a thread of its own, with progress in the browser and the welcome window. Phazite pins the release it installs by address, size and SHA-256, so a changed or damaged file is refused, and a released zip is never replaced; a new release means a new pin in Phazite. The zip unpacks into `Documents/Phazite/Packs/Phazite Sounds` (entries that would land outside are refused), whose `sources.json` names the pack and version. A zip downloaded by hand installs the same way (**Install from a File...**), for computers without a connection. Linux builds have no TLS without curl, so there the button opens the release page; **later:** curl on Linux, and updates when a new version is pinned.
  - **They are CC0 only**, so songs made with them carry no conditions; CC BY and CC BY-SA would make every released song owe credits, and "royalty-free" licences forbid redistribution. Third-party sounds come from libraries whose licence files say CC0 and whose descriptions show no one else's material: VCSL, Virtuosity Drums, Big Rusty Drums, body_percussion, FreePats, screened Sonic Pi samples, EatMe's breakbeat and Kenney's foley. Phazite synthesizes the rest (808s, analog-style drums, risers, sweeps and impacts) and plays its loops from these sounds to its own patterns: `phazite_soundkit` (`tools/soundkit/`) renders the pack from the recipes in the sounds repository, the same bytes every time, and zips it the same way every time. The pack's `sources.json` records each sound's source, licence evidence and hash, and the sounds repository's CI checks that every sound is listed there as CC0. Drum machines' own recordings (the 909's cymbals), sampled records (the Amen break) and the makers' names are kept out.
- **Later:** automatic tags for your own samples, from analysis (one-shot or loop, drum type, brightness, key, tempo), and "find similar".

**What it grows into (researched 2026-11-04, the owner: "are there other daws that have ultra-functional or intuitive or otherwise superiour browser views that everyone is praising? looking to improve upon what we have right now which is a fine, but plain folder view"; [B130](#appendix-b-sources) to [B134](#appendix-b-sources)):**
- **The browsers praised most:**
  - Bitwig Studio's pop-up browser: a device, preset or sample tried on the track while the song plays, then kept or cancelled. One user gives it "11 out of 10".
  - Cubase's MediaBay: called the best in a DAW, for its columns of attributes and its ratings.
  - Ableton Live 12: filters by tag, automatic tags for the user's own samples, similarity search, and Similar Sample Swapping on drum pads.
  - Sononym, for similarity.
  - XO and Atlas: drum sample maps, auditioned while the beat plays.
  - Splice: filters by loop or one-shot, tempo and key, and previews in the song's key.
- **What users value most:** trying a sound in place while the beat plays, with cancel; similar sounds found from the audio; and filters that offer only what has results.
- **What they complain of:** tags that replace folders (the backlash against Live 12's and Bitwig 5's redesigns); slow scrolling and indexing (libraries of 250,000 sounds); automatic tags shown as fact; tags lost when a file is renamed; filters left on with nothing to show it; and cloud gates.
- **Recommendation (decided by Claude for the owner to review, and placed after v1 beside the steps the owner moved there the same day; its first phase could come before v1 instead):** the tree of places stays the backbone, with two phases on top of it ([roadmap](roadmap.md#after-v1)):
  - **First (M):**
    - a row of filters over what is shown: type, loop or one-shot, tempo and key, each offering only what is there, with counts, as chips that one Clear empties with the search words;
    - keys read from sounds' names and loop data;
    - **Swap** on a drum pad or one of Phazite's devices, which plays each sound or preset the browser moves to in that place, in the beat and through the track's devices, Enter keeping it as one undo step and Esc putting the old one back;
    - collections, kept by file.
  - **Then (L):** the user's own sounds known by analysis, in the background and paused at will, shown as guesses the user corrects; Show Similar, and a pad's previous and next similar sound with a lock; loops in key.
  - **Not:** sample maps (divisive, drums only), recorded previews of plug-ins' presets, or ratings, which favorites and collections cover.

**Built: the first phase (2026-11-07, the owner: "do the first part of" [#24](https://github.com/august-byrne/Phazite/issues/24), "the filters and swapping in place"; the choices below are Claude's, for the owner to review):**
- **Filters** (`src/services/BrowserModel.h`: `BrowserFilters`, `FilterOptions`; `src/ui/browser/FilterBar.h`): under the search field, a chip for each of Type, Loop, Tempo and Key. A chip opens a menu of the values that what the browser shows has, as the other filters and the search's words leave it, each with how many have it (so a value chosen never leaves nothing, and switching to another of the same filter does neither), the one set ticked; set, the chip shows its value in the accent's wash, and its x takes it away. **Clear**, there while a filter is set or the search has words, takes all of them away, as Splice's and VS Code's filters do. The research's complaint of filters left on with nothing to show it is why each shows on its chip, and why nothing keeps them from one start to the next.
  - While any is set, the tree stays the backbone: each place shows how many of its sounds have the filters' values and lists them as one list, its folders' within it, as Ableton Live 12 and Bitwig list a location's results; places with none don't show, and the folders that were open before the filters open again once they are taken away. Search's results take the filters too. A list shows 2,000 at most, then says how many more there are.
  - Type is what a sound is (Kick, Snare...), Loop is loops or one-shots, Tempo the tempos the sounds' tags give, and Key the keys, each root alone (a tuned one-shot), then major, then minor, from C. MIDI files take the filters as sounds do; folders, devices and presets have none of their values, so with any filter set they don't show.
- **Keys** (`src/model/engine/AudioFiles.h`: `SoundKey`, `getKeyFromName()`): from the name as sample packs write keys, a note with a sharp or a flat and "m", "min", "minor", "maj" or "major" after it or as the next word ("Am", "C#min", "F# Minor", "Cmaj7"). A note alone counts only where the name sets it apart: its last word ("808 Long F", as Phazite Sounds name their 808s), in brackets ("Kick (F)"), with a sharp or a flat, or after "key" ("Key of G"); a capital M is never a minor ("FM Bass"), nor a word that merely starts with a note. A folder named for a key with minor or major ("Loops/F# Minor") gives it to the sounds in it whose names give none. Then from the loop data: a WAV file's acid chunk names a root note, an AIFF file's Apple loop data a root with minor or major. Loop data also make a sound a loop or a one-shot whatever its name says, and give a loop its tempo from its beats and length. A note alone on a drum's variant ("Kick A") can be taken for its key; the analysis of the second phase is to correct such guesses.
  - The index reads the loop data of WAV and AIFF files on its own thread, after it has listed every sound by name, and keeps what it read in `Library facts.txt` beside the settings, by each file's path, size and time, so that a start reads only what is new or changed.
- **Swap** (`src/model/devices/SwapModel.h`, the browser's swap): on a drum pad, a **Swap** button beside Reset in the drum sampler's editor; on one of Phazite's own devices, **Swap: Try Them in Place** at the top of its Presets menu (the Devices view's row has no room for another button beside Presets, its window's and the sidechain's). The browser shows, with a strip over its search that says what it swaps and what is in its place now, with **Keep** and **Put Back**, and has the keys: each sound (for a pad) or preset of the device's type (for a device) it moves to, by the arrow keys or a click, plays in that place, in the beat while the song plays and through the track's devices; while the song is stopped the pad plays once, or an instrument a short note. Enter, a double-click or Keep keeps it, as one undo step ("Put Kick 03 on Pad 1", "Load Preset Warm Pad"), the sound copied into the project only then; Esc or Put Back puts the old one back, leaving nothing to redo. Previews stay silent meanwhile, and what can't be tried shows muted. A pad's swap shows the sounds of its sound's type (a kick's, kicks). A device's shows its presets alone, Phazite's for it and then the user's, each folder of them open, from the top, so that Down tries one after another, as Ableton Live's Hot-Swap expands the device's folder for the arrow keys ([B86](#appendix-b-sources)), with the filters hidden, as presets have none of their values; it first opened the device's folder in place among every other device's, where it could be out of view (2026-11-08, the owner: "I did get a bit lost"). Once the swap ends the browser is as it was before: its search, its filters, its folders open, the row selected and how far it was scrolled. The pad being swapped is outlined, and its Swap lit, which a click then keeps.
  - **Anything else that changes the song meanwhile keeps what is tried, as a step of its own, and ends the swap** (the undo convention's `Trial`, `src/model/commands/internal/Undo.h`): another edit, a drag, an undo (which then takes it back, as a step that can be redone), a save, another swap, or the browser hidden; so the next change is never mixed into the swap's step, and what is heard is what stays, as in FL Studio, where the sound landed on stays. Auto Save waits while a swap has tried something, and while a drag goes on, as either may yet be taken back.
- **Collections** (`src/services/Library.h`): named sets of sounds, presets, folders and devices, under Collections: right-click an item, **Add to Collection**, ticked in those it is in (choosing one again takes it out), or **New Collection...**, named in a field over the tree as VS Code names a new file; a collection's menu renames it or deletes it, once asked (its sounds stay where they are). **Kept by file:** a sound, MIDI file or preset is known by its size and the SHA-256 of its first 64 KiB besides its path, so that one renamed or moved stays in its collections, found again in the folder it was in, or anywhere in the library once the folders are indexed again; favorites are kept the same way. Sets live in the settings.
- **Decided by Claude, as the owner asked (2026-11-07: "for my calls, I want you instead to use your best judgement and do whichever ones you think would be good to do"):**
  - **Sounds by Type went:** the Type filter lists a type's sounds over every place, with the other filters and their counts, so there is one way to find the kicks.
  - **A drum pad's Load... went:** Swap tries the browser's sounds on the pad in the beat, and a sound dropped on it from the browser or Explorer goes on it, as Ableton Live's Drum Rack and Bitwig's Drum Machine take sounds onto pads, neither with a file dialog. A folder outside the browser is one **Add Folder...** away, and then stays there for the filters and the next swap. A wavetable oscillator keeps its Load Wavetable File..., as Swap doesn't try tables.
  - **The Presets menu stayed as it was, Swap its first item:** it is the one button for presets on every device, a plug-in's too, whose presets Swap doesn't try in place yet, and a preset known by name is one pick away in it. Presets opening Swap directly would make the one button work two ways, by the kind of device.

## 12. Commands, keys and help

**Command registry:**
- every action has an ID, a name, a description, search synonyms (searching "cut" finds Split), a category and a default key;
- the registry itself has no UI code (2026-10-14): it lists the commands, runs each inside its named undo step, says whether one is enabled or ticked, and searches them. The UI connects it to keys and menus through JUCE's `ApplicationCommandManager` ([section 24](#24-code-structure));
- later, commands take parameters and return results, so that scripts and the assistant can run them, as MuseScore's newest commands do ([B46](#appendix-b-sources)).

**Command palette (Ctrl+K):** a fuzzy search over every command, showing each one's shortcut. Enter runs the selected command.

**Menu bar (2026-10-13): File, Edit, Add, View and Help.** Like FL Studio's, Live's and Bitwig's, it has no Track, Song or Transport menu. **No Options menu (2026-11-05, the owner: "all settings that are available in the settings gear icon on the bottom left do not need to also be represented in the top left menu tabs"):** the settings' windows, Scan for Plug-ins, the themes and the keymaps are in the menu of the gear at the activity bar's foot, as in VS Code's Manage menu, and in the command search; the computer keyboard's switch is on the transport bar.
- **Add:** a track, a folder or a return track, a section, and the song outlines;
- **Edit** also holds the selected tracks' edits (color, mute, solo, folders, order) and inserting and removing time;
- **View** shows and hides the browser, the song, the editor and the inspector, the full screen, the piano roll and the mixer, with the rest in six submenus (2026-11-04, the owner: "reduce the number of items in the view tab ... without losing much functionality"; 13 items where there were 33, and no command left out): **Panels** (maximize, detach, the layouts, focus mode), **Zoom**, **Playback** (going to the start or end, centring on and following the playhead, scrolling continuously, the loop), **Grid**, **Tracks** (heights, collapsing, pinning) and **Interface Size**;
- **Help:** the command search, the keyboard shortcuts and hover help;
- play and stop are on the transport bar and the keys. Every command stays in a menu as well as in right-click menus, as Apple's and Microsoft's guidelines ask.

**Keymaps (decided: whatever users find most intuitive; FL Studio style is fine):**
- **First launch asks** "Coming from FL Studio, Ableton Live, Logic, Pro Tools, or new to music software?" and loads a matching keymap. Digital Performer 12 has switchable keysets that copy other DAWs' shortcuts ([B13](#appendix-b-sources)). v1 ships the Phazite default, FL Studio and Ableton Live keymaps (step 3.7).
- **The Phazite default** follows conventions that most DAWs and the operating system share:
  - Space plays, and stops back at the play start: where playback last started (the last click in a time ruler, or the song's start in a project just opened), so a sound can be replayed quickly while it is tweaked. FL Studio, Ableton Live, REAPER and Waveform do this; Logic Pro, Studio One and Bitwig pause instead, with an option to go back. Shift+Space pauses and plays on from there, as in Ableton Live (Ctrl+Space in FL Studio's keymap, as in FL Studio);
  - Ctrl+Z and Ctrl+Shift+Z undo and redo;
  - Ctrl+S saves;
  - Ctrl+C, Ctrl+X and Ctrl+V copy, cut and paste.

  It adds FL Studio's single-key tools, which reviewers praise.
- **Any command can be rebound.** Ctrl+Alt+click on a menu item assigns a shortcut directly, as Logic's Control-click does. Changed keys are saved by the command's ID (`edit.split`): JUCE's own format saves numbered commands, and the numbers change when commands are added (step 1.2).
- **Keys that depend on what is selected:** several commands can share a key, and the enabled one with the highest priority runs. With notes selected, the arrow keys move the notes; otherwise they select tracks or nudge clips (step 1.3).
