- **Text fields let the keys go (2026-11-07, the owner: "clicking outside of the textbox does not un-highlight/unselect the text box"):** a press anywhere outside a text field gives the keys back to the shortcuts, so that Space plays again, and the field shows no selection once the keys have gone, as text fields do in other apps. JUCE left them in the field when the press landed on what doesn't take keys (a panel's background, a button, a knob), and drew the field's selection at half strength once they had gone elsewhere. A press in a menu over the field (its own Cut, Copy and Paste) is the field's, and one outside a dialog does nothing to the dialog's fields. Enter and Escape in the inspector's names let the keys go too, after the name changes. Showing a view never puts the cursor in a field: the browser's icon gives the browser the keys, not its search, which Ctrl+F (Search the Browser) or a click puts it in, and the Source Control icon gives the view the keys, not the commit's message (`src/ui/input/TextFieldRelease.h`).
- **The computer keyboard plays notes**, on by default so a new project makes sound right away (step 0.7):
  - the layout of Ableton Live and Logic Pro: the middle row of letters plays the white keys from middle C, the row above the black keys, Z and X change the octave and C and V the velocity in steps of 20 ([B40](#appendix-b-sources));
  - it takes only keys pressed without Shift, Ctrl, Alt or Cmd, and never keys typed into a text field;
  - a top-bar button and M in Phazite's keymap (as in Ableton Live) turn it off. In the FL Studio keymap Ctrl+T does, and F10 opens the MIDI settings, as in FL Studio ([B41](#appendix-b-sources)). FL's manual doesn't document which keys play which notes, so that keymap uses the same layout;
  - **single-key shortcuts (decided, open question 9):** as in Ableton Live 12 ([B40](#appendix-b-sources)), while the computer keyboard plays notes, Shift and a letter run that letter's shortcut (FL Studio's tools, for example), unless Shift and that letter have a shortcut of their own. With the computer keyboard off, the letters alone run them. So a keymap shouldn't give Shift and a letter a different command than the letter alone.
- **How "most intuitive" gets settled:**
  - watch three to five people, both FL Studio users and complete beginners, do the same tasks with a prototype: place a kick on every beat, trim, fade, duplicate a section, write a chord;
  - count hesitations and errors, and adjust the defaults from that.

**Keymaps as built (step 3.7, 2026-10-29, decided by Claude for the owner to review, from FL Studio's manual ([B41](#appendix-b-sources)) and Ableton Live 12's ([B60](#appendix-b-sources))):**
- Every keymap gives a key to the core actions (a test checks it): play and stop, record, the metronome, undo and redo, cut, copy, paste, delete, duplicate, select all, split, quantize, rename, open, save, save as, export, the command search, zoom, the mixer and the piano roll, the Smart and Draw tools, moving notes, and the computer keyboard; and every command a keymap names exists (a test checks that too).
- **FL Studio's** gains what its manual gives the commands Phazite has: Ctrl+X, C and V; Insert splits; Ctrl+Insert and Ctrl+Delete insert and remove time; 9 reverses audio clips; Shift+Q quantizes; Alt+Up and Down move tracks; Ctrl+Alt+C bounces in place, as its consolidating does.
- **Ableton Live's** (new): Live's keys where Phazite has the command, its mouse being Phazite's own (right-click opens menus): F9 records, Shift+Space plays on, Ctrl+L loops the selected time, 0 mutes the selected clips and R reverses them, S solos and C arms the selected tracks, U collapses them, A shows automation, Ctrl+E splits (and chops notes), Ctrl+B bounces in place, Ctrl+Alt+Shift+F freezes, Ctrl+Alt+M shows the mixer, Ctrl+Alt+3 the piano roll and Ctrl+Alt+4 the instrument, W zooms to fit, Shift+? turns hover help on and off (Live's Info View), Ctrl+Up and Down step through takes (Live's comping keys), and Ctrl+1 to 5 set the grid, as Phazite's keymap already took from Live. Phazite's tools have no keys in Live, so B is the Draw tool (Live's Draw Mode) and E, held, the Slice tool (Live splits notes by holding E), with Esc and the digits 1 to 9 as in Phazite's keymap. Capture has no key, as in Live, whose Ctrl+Shift+C copies time. Tab stays for moving the keyboard focus, so the mixer has Ctrl+Alt+M, not Live's Tab.
- The welcome window's "Coming from...?" offers Ableton Live as a choice of its own.

**Cut, Copy and Paste (step 3.7, 2026-10-29, decided by Claude for the owner to review):** Ctrl+X, C and V in every keymap, in the Edit menu, and in the clips', notes' and tracks' right-click menus (`src/model/arrangement/internal/Clipboard.h`).
- Copy takes what Delete would delete, in its order: the selected notes; or the selected time range, its clips cut at its edges; or the selected clips; with the automation under the clips or in the range, as it is when copied. Cut copies and then deletes as Delete does.
- Paste puts clips at the playhead, from the selected track down (else the first selected clip's track, else where they came from), each in place of what lies under it, as a duplicated range is, so that nothing plays twice; as copies, not aliases, as Duplicate makes them. The automation comes with them when they go back onto their own tracks, along which automation goes with clips ([section 10](#10-automation-and-modulation)). Notes go into the clip the piano roll shows at the playhead, or, with none of them there, into its first clip as far in as they were in theirs, so that a part copies from one pattern to another as in FL Studio. What Paste puts in is selected (a range's becomes the range), as Duplicate selects its copies, as one undo step ("Paste 2 Clips").
- Most DAWs paste at the playhead or the edit cursor (Ableton Live's insert marker, Logic Pro's playhead, REAPER's edit cursor); Phazite's playhead is its play start while stopped, set by a click in a ruler.
- The clipboard belongs to the open project and empties when another opens, since clips name their files by paths relative to their project. **Not yet:** copying between projects, and copying automation points, sections or tracks (Duplicate does those).

**Macros:** chain commands into one, like REAPER's custom actions, and bind the result to a key.

**Named undo:**
- every command is one named undo step ("Split clip", "Apply outline"), and an undo history panel lists them;
- Every change is a named step in the song's own undo history (`Song::getUndoManager()`): the command registry begins one for each command, and a model function that is a change of its own begins one for each edit the UI asks the model for. From architecture phase A2, the UI never starts one itself, and a drag is one step, opened when it starts and kept or rolled back when it ends ([section 24](#24-code-structure)).

**Help:**
- the hint bar is always there;
- hover descriptions are on by default for new users and can be turned off with one click;
- later, a manual page for the hovered control.

**Help as built (step 3.7, 2026-10-29, decided by Claude for the owner to review):**
- **Every command has help text:** a name, a category and a sentence that says what it does, which the menus' hints, the command search and the shortcuts editor show (a test checks every command the window registers).
- **Every control explains itself** in the hint bar, with a tooltip or a description (a test walks every visible control of the window, the mixer, the piano roll and the Instrument and Effects pages). A device's knob, choice or switch says what it ranges over and where it starts, from the device's description, and how to work it: drag it, double-click it for where it started, or right-click it to automate or modulate it.
- **Lessons** (Help > Lessons, `src/ui/shell/Lessons.h`): five short lessons for a project that starts empty, Make a Beat (five steps), Record Audio, Arrange a Song, Add Effects and Write Chords (four each), with the keys of the keymap in use (or how to find a command without a key in the command search). **Each shows in a card over the bottom right corner of the window's centre** (since 2026-11-01, as the owner asked: "lessons should be a more in-your-face ux, not just the tiny tooltip on the bottom of the screen"; until then the hint bar showed them; `src/ui/shell/LessonCard.h`): the lesson's name, a dot for each step, the step in large text, Back, Next (Skip while the step isn't done, Done at its end) and a button that stops it, with a ring in the accent colour around the control the step is about, which pulses a few times as the step starts and then stays still. A step goes on when it is done, in any way: its command runs, however it is run, or the song shows it (an instrument on a track, a track armed); only a step that nothing can see done waits for Next. The card covers nothing but its corner and everything goes on working while it shows: help shown when it is needed, beside the task the user does, works better than a tutorial that takes over, and a coach mark should point at the real control, one at a time ([B120](#appendix-b-sources)). Ableton Live teaches with lessons in a view of its own, the Learn View ([B63](#appendix-b-sources)). While a lesson runs, the guide's tips wait; a finished lesson is marked "(done)" in Help > Lessons from then on. Stop Lesson, or the card's close button, ends one.

## 13. Getting started and song outlines

**First launch** needs no account and no sign-in:
1. "Coming from…?" chooses a keymap.
2. Audio setup: pick an output, or an ASIO® driver when an audio interface is present.
3. An empty project opens (since 2026-11-01: until then a starter project with a beat and an arpeggio), and the welcome window's last page offers the example songs and the lessons; the hint bar suggests a next step ("Drag an instrument from the browser").

**The welcome window (2026-10-13)** is where first launch asks these things, a page at a time (`src/ui/shell/Welcome.h`). It opens once, over the first project, the first time Phazite starts, and **Help > Welcome** opens it again. Every page is optional. Its third page offers Phazite Sounds ([section 11](#11-the-browser)): **Download**, which shows its progress, or **Install from a File...**, and **Not Now**; its last, **Where to start** (2026-11-01), the example songs, each with what it shows, and the lessons, each a button that opens it and closes the window.

**First launch as built (step 1.9, 2026-10-15, proposed):**
- The welcome window has three pages, with **Back**, **Next** and **Not Now**: "Coming from another music app?" (FL Studio's keymap for FL Studio, Ableton Live's for Live since step 3.7; Phazite's own for Logic Pro, another app or none), the sound page, and Phazite Sounds. A choice of app takes effect at once.
- The sound page has JUCE's device chooser, with outputs only and the sample rate and buffer size behind a button, and **Play a Test Sound**, a one-second beep through the output chosen; on Windows it explains ASIO. Ableton Live opens its Learn View on first launch and points newcomers to its "Setting up Audio I/O" lesson ([B63](#appendix-b-sources)), and newcomers' "no sound" threads in DAW forums often come down to the wrong output, or an ASIO driver another app holds.
- An example song selects its lead as it opens, so the computer keyboard plays it at once (the drum sampler's pads are two octaves below the keyboard's first octave). Until 2026-11-01 the first start opened a starter project, which selected its Synth track.
- **The guide:** while the pointer rests on nothing with a tip of its own, the hint bar suggests the next step: put an instrument or a sound into the song (from the browser or the plug-in picker) or open an example song, play notes on the computer keyboard, play the song, record (or capture) what is played, and export the song (in that order since 2026-11-01, as a new project starts empty), with the keys of the keymap in use (`src/ui/shell/Guide.h`). A step goes once it is done, in any way, and stays done in later sessions; recording counts as playing notes. **Help > Getting Started Tips** turns the guide off and on.

**New project (step 1.9, 2026-10-15, proposed; the name moved to the first save on 2026-10-16):** a window with the tempo, time signature, key (none, or a root with major or minor) and an optional song outline, whose sections it lists, as GarageBand's project chooser offers tempo, key and time signature before a project starts ([B62](#appendix-b-sources)). The project is untitled until its first save asks what to call it and where it goes ([section 18](#18-projects-saving-and-export)). **A new project starts with no tracks (2026-11-01, as the owner asked: "when opening a fresh project though, it should be blank with no tracks")**, the key going on the tracks added later; until then it started with a Drums track and a Synth track unless a box was unticked, and the first start opened a starter project with a beat and an arpeggio. Starting Phazite still opens the project open last. What a new project starts with (its tempo, time signature, key and outline) is part of its first save, not undo steps (`src/model/project/NewProject.h`).

**Example songs (2026-11-01, as the owner asked: "a section in the help tab for example songs, and the songs should show usage of most features of Phazite"; `src/model/project/ExampleSongs.h`):** Help > Example Songs, and the welcome window's last page, open one as a new untitled project, so its first save asks where it goes and nothing can overwrite the example, as Ableton's templates open as "Untitled" and Reason has File > Open Demo Song ([B118](#appendix-b-sources)); the computer keyboard plays its lead.
- **Exodus (2026-11-08, the owner: "Our demo songs suck musically (lo-fi is the only semi-good one) ... it would be best however if we could use an already made song that someone made open", then "for now lets just replace the trap beat with XCALIBUR – Exodus"),** a synthwave song by XCALIBUR, from The Best of LMMS vol. 5, which its author shared with its LMMS project on the LMMS Sharing Platform under CC BY: its notes alone (`resources/songs/Exodus.mid`, written from the project by `tools/lmms-to-midi.ps1`), played on Phazite's instruments in F major as its recording plays them, 112 bars: Intro, Theme, Breakdown, Build-up, Theme 2, Breakdown 2, Build-up 2, Theme 3 and Outro. An arpeggio of seventh chords in sixteenths on three layers, as the original's: the synth's Bright Pluck and the wavetable's Harmonic Pluck an octave up, both through a tape's wobble, and FM's Glass Pluck two octaves up, which carries the intro and the breakdowns through the high-pass; a bass pulsing in sixteenths and a sub on the chords' roots (IV, ii and iii), which the chord lane follows; 80s drums on the Synthwave Kit with a fill every eighth bar; everything else pumping under the drums through sidechains; a plate on a return; the master's EQ, warm and dark as the original's, its high-pass and low-pass sweeping in automation lanes as the original's do, thinning the intro, the breakdowns and the build-ups, and a loud limiter. Each part's clips repeat as aliases, as patterns do. Its credit is in its description, its project's notes (so that it goes with what is made from it) and the About box. Its tempo is the original's, in the tempo lane: a tape speeding up from 50 BPM to 111 over the intro, 114 through the first two themes, and spinning up to 260 BPM over each breakdown before it drops to 50 and ramps back up over the build-up. Then the owner found its bass "less complex and flat feeling" than the original's and its drums "not very similar" (2026-11-08): the bass now plays on the FM synth as the original's did, a saw that a sine a fourth up and one at its own pitch move (LMMS's phase modulation), falling to nothing in 192 ms, with the original's EQ lifting its lows; the sub is three saws a few cents apart through a 12 dB low-pass that each note opens, an ensemble chorus, a little drive and the original's EQ; and each of the drums' five samples, measured, plays on the factory sound nearest it (a tight kick cut at 120 ms, a big snare with a tom tuned under it for the sample's long tone, the default clap, a woodblock tick and a short open hat). Then the owner shared the original's stems and asked for it to sound as close to the original as Phazite can, with the presets that takes (2026-11-08). Measured against them, it now follows the recording, which differs from the project XCALIBUR shared: its first theme is 24 bars, without the project's bars 24 to 32; it plays a fourth higher, in F major (the project's pitch is 0 and nothing moves it, so the recording was transposed whole: the owner heard the arpeggio's notes off while only the bass and the sub were moved); and its first two themes are at 114 BPM. Its intro follows the recording too, dark and full where the project's filters made it thin and bright: a kick on every beat, which the drum stem holds on each beat of the ramp; the master's high-pass taking the sub's fundamental and the kick's low end away at first and opening from 250 Hz until the lows are whole by bar 6, then sweeping up to 1.5 kHz into the theme; its low-pass opening from about 400 Hz to 2 kHz by bar 6 and fully into the theme; and the sub brighter until the theme, its harmonics the intro's strongest notes, as the recording's. Each was fitted to the recording's spectrum every 2 seconds, about a tenth to a twentieth of the project's values. Its drums play the Synthwave Kit, made after the original's samples as measured; its arpeggio's second layer the Harmonic Pluck, the original's pad plucked as its envelope plucks it; and everything but the drums, the plate included, pumps through the Sidechain Pump, about 30 dB down at the beat and back in 200 ms, as the original's peak controller pumps it. Its parts' balance is within about 1 dB of the stems', its mix's lows too, with its mids and highs 2 to 4 dB brighter (`docs/presets.md` says how its sounds were made); no sound of the original is in Phazite.
- **Lo-fi Beat,** 82 BPM in F major, the lo-fi outline's 48 bars, improved on 2026-11-04: ninth chords on the chord lane (ii9, V9, Imaj9, vi9 in the A parts), spread out on a soft e-piano that wobbles as a tape does (the synth's own LFO on its pitch, slowly and slightly), through a container (a saturator and a filter) with two macros, Muffle and Drive, and an LFO that pans it; a lazy boom-bap beat, swung, the snare a little late with ghost notes before it, its velocities a little uneven, a fill of snare and toms at each part's end and the A and B parts with kicks of their own, through a bus that crunches it (a downsampling filter) and makes it punch; a shaker loop, a record's crackle and rain as samples; a walking bass (each chord's root, fifth and octave and a step from a semitone under the next root); a melody through a dub delay in the B parts and its answer in the second A part; the keys' filter closing in the break; the whole song coming in through a filter on the master that opens over the intro and closes at the end (a lane) as the drums fade out.
- **House Groove,** 124 BPM in A minor, 64 bars, improved on 2026-11-04: Intro, Build-up, Drop, Breakdown, Drop 2 and Outro, and a chord lane; drums in a bus with a compressor, a beat on the drum sampler's house kit with house's open hats on the offbeats, quiet closed sixteenths, a rim that pushes and, in the second drop, a ride; samples placed in the playlist as FL Studio users place them (a shaker loop that follows the tempo, crashes, and the crash reversed and faded in before each drop); house's offbeat bass and chord stabs from the chord lane, both ducking under the kick through sidechains, the pump; the stabs' filter opening through the build-up, closing in the breakdown and opening again (an automation lane), an LFO on it; a warm pad in the breakdown; a lead, an octave up in the second drop; noise risers whose volume rises (a lane); a reverb and a delay on returns, with sends; and a multiband on the master, its Gentle Master preset.
- First Beat, four bars of drums and an arpeggio (the starter project's song until 2026-11-01), went on 2026-11-04, as the owner asked; Trap Beat (140 BPM in C minor, made in code on 2026-11-04) went on 2026-11-08 for Exodus.

Each is made in code through the model's functions by ID, as the UI makes a song, so that it keeps working however the model holds the song (demo songs saved as files would have to follow every change of the project format while Phazite is pre-alpha), with Phazite's own devices and presets (a device stays at its defaults where a preset is missing) and samples it synthesizes as it is made, as Phazite ships no sound files. A song someone else made ships as its notes alone, a MIDI file, which is no project format, under CC0 or CC BY only: people take an example song apart and save it as their own, and those licenses ask nothing more of what they make than credit. The DAWs' demo songs are mostly finished tracks by artists, as Exodus is; Phazite's own are short and show its features, since its lessons and the hint bar teach the rest.

**Song outlines (decided)** are templates that lay out a song's sections, so newcomers see how songs are usually shaped. Applying one:
- creates colored sections with typical lengths;
- gives each section an **energy**, how intense it is, which the inspector shows (the time bar drew it as a faint curve behind the sections' names until 2026-11-06, when the owner asked for the band without lines);
- gives each section a one-line tip, such as for a build-up: "Raise the energy: risers, a snare roll, open a filter".

Sections stay fully editable, and later features use them; chord suggestions, for example, can tell a chorus from a verse.

v1 outlines (decided), with typical lengths in bars at the project tempo, all editable:

| Style | Sections (bars) |
|---|---|
| Pop | Intro 4, Verse 8, Pre-chorus 4, Chorus 8, Verse 8, Pre-chorus 4, Chorus 8, Bridge 8, Chorus 16, Outro 4 |
| Rock | Intro 8, Verse 16, Chorus 8, Verse 16, Chorus 8, Solo 8, Bridge 8, Chorus 16, Outro 8 |
| Electronic (house, EDM) | Intro 16, Build-up 8, Drop 16, Breakdown 16, Build-up 8, Drop 16, Outro 16 |
| Hip-hop and trap | Intro 4, Hook 8, Verse 16, Hook 8, Verse 16, Hook 8, Outro 4 |
| Lo-fi beat (2026-10-13) | Intro 4, Loop A 8, Loop B 8, Loop A 8, Break 4, Loop B 8, Outro 8 |
| R&B (2026-10-13) | Intro 4, Verse 16, Pre-chorus 4, Chorus 8, Verse 16, Pre-chorus 4, Chorus 8, Bridge 8, Chorus 8, Outro 4 |
| Drum and bass (2026-10-13) | Intro 32, Build-up 8, Drop 32, Breakdown 24, Build-up 8, Drop 32, Outro 24 |
| Techno (2026-10-13) | Intro 32, Groove 32, Build-up 16, Breakdown 16, Peak 32, Outro 32 |

- **Later:** dubstep, trance and future bass, DJ-friendly variants with 32-bar intros and outros, and outlines saved from a song's own sections.
- **Outlines are data files**, so adding a style needs no code changes.
- Each outline can also suggest a chord progression per section, linking to [section 14](#14-chords).

## 14. Chords

**The chord lane** is a global lane under the sections that shows the song's chords, a guide to what is written over them, there while the song has chords. It drives:
- piano-roll highlighting of chord tones and the scale;
- chord suggestions ([section 16](#16-ai-and-generative-features)).

Parts generated from it ("make a chord part, bass line or arpeggio from the chord lane") were built in step 2.7 and taken out on 2026-11-07 (below).

**Chord progression outlines (decided)** are a library of common progressions to try. You can:
- browse them by mood and genre; each entry shows its numerals and a name;
- audition one in the project's key and tempo with the selected instrument;
- apply it to a section, a selection or the whole song. Change the key and the progression follows.

Starting set (numerals relative to the key; lowercase means a minor chord):

| Progression | Feel, and where it's common |
|---|---|
| I–V–vi–IV | Bright pop anthem |
| vi–IV–I–V | The same chords, more wistful; pop and EDM |
| I–vi–IV–V | 1950s doo-wop and retro pop |
| ii–V–I (with sevenths) | Jazz, house, lo-fi |
| i–VI–III–VII | Epic minor; trance, EDM, film |
| i–VII–VI–VII | Driving minor; rock and EDM |
| i–iv–v (or i–iv–V) | Dark minor; hip-hop and trap |
| I–IV–V | Rock, blues, country |
| I–♭VII–IV | Mixolydian rock |
| IV–V–iii–vi | "Royal road"; J-pop and anime |
| i–VII–VI–V | Andalusian cadence; flamenco and rock |
| 12-bar blues | Blues and rock and roll |

v1 should have about 40, tagged by mood and genre, in a data file that is easy to extend.

**Engine note:** Phazite keeps progressions in its own format: degree, accidental, quality, extensions, inversion and length, so that borrowed chords such as ♭VII and secondary dominants such as V/V can be written, which numerals for the seven diatonic chords alone (a degree with 6, 7 or 9, its quality taken from the scale) couldn't. The song keeps the chords once, in its chord lane (`CHORDLANE`), and Phazite makes its parts from them itself (below; decided at step 2.7).

**As built (step 2.7, 2026-10-18; decided by Claude for the owner to review):**
- **Where it is (since 2026-11-07, [B144](#appendix-b-sources)).** The chord lane is a row 18 pixels tall, as the sections' band is, under the playlist's bar numbers and above the tracks, there while the song has chords: every DAW with a chord track keeps it out of the way until it is used (Cubase's isn't there until it is added, Logic Pro's and Studio Pro's show only among the global tracks the user shows), and an empty lane took a row of the playlist for nothing. A song's first chord comes from the Chord Progressions window or from **Add Chord Here** in the time bar's right-click, beside Add Section Here, which shows the lane; its last deleted, or **Delete All Chords** in its header's **...** menu (one undo step, which takes the key changes after the song's start too), the lane goes. Its header, over the track headers, has its name and that menu, with the progressions. Until 2026-11-07 the lane was always there, 24 pixels tall, an empty one saying what to do with it, and its header showed the song's key.
- **The sections stay in a band of their own** over the bar numbers, rather than in the bar numbers' row: DAWs whose markers are points put them in the ruler (FL Studio, Live's locators, Bitwig's cue markers), but those whose sections span time and move what is in them give them a lane (Logic Pro's arrangement markers, Cubase's and Studio Pro's arranger tracks, REAPER's regions), and the bar numbers already take three drags (where playback starts, the playhead, the loop), which a section's move and resize would share.
- **Chords.** A click on empty space offers the key's seven chords, then those from outside it (borrowed from the parallel key, and the dominants of the scale's chords), and adds the one chosen there, a bar long, up to the next chord, as new chords in Studio Pro default to a bar ([B82](#appendix-b-sources)). A click on a chord offers the same chords in its place, its kind (major, minor, sevenths, sus, sixths, ninths, power), its inversion, and Delete; Studio Pro's right-click changes a chord's root, type and bass the same way. Dragging a chord's right edge makes it longer or shorter, on the grid, over the chord after it. Each change is one undo step. Names spell each note by its step of the scale (the leading note of D harmonic minor is C♯, not D♭).
- **Keys.** Chords are saved as numerals in the key where they are, so a change of key moves them all: the inspector's **Key**, on the song's page that shows with nothing selected (the lane's header until 2026-11-07), changes the song's key, and **Change Key from Here** in the lane's menu adds a key change at a chord or a bar. Until a key is chosen, the lane is in the new project's key, or C major.
- **Progressions.** The Chord Progressions window (**Add > Chord Progressions**, the lane's menus, the command palette) lists 40, narrowed by mood and genre, each with its name, its chords in the song's key, its numerals and its feel. **Play** hears the selected one in the song's key and tempo, held chords over their roots on Phazite's synth, looping in time with the song. **Put on the Chord Lane** lays it over the selected sections, the selected time range, or the whole song (once from the start in an empty song), repeated to fill it, as one undo step. A progression written for minor plays, in a major key, in the minor key with the song's notes (A minor's i–VI–III–VII in C major is Am–F–C–G), and one for major in a minor key in its relative major; in a mode, they play in the major or minor key with its notes.
- **Parts made from the chords, taken out (2026-11-07, the owner: "does any other daw actually have a feature where it automatically populates a pattern for you based on your chords? it seems pretty weak"; [B144](#appendix-b-sources)).** Until then **Make a Part from the Chords** (the lane's menus, **Add > Make Chord Part**, **Make Bass Line** and **Make Arpeggio**) added a track with Phazite's synth and a clip of held triads or sevenths, spread pads, chords on the beats or offbeat stabs, roots, roots and fifths or octaves, or arpeggios, which followed the chords until their notes were edited (the inspector's **Follows the chords**), as Logic Pro's Session Players follow its chord track ([B83](#appendix-b-sources)). Only Logic makes parts from a chord track among the DAWs checked, with players its reviews praise for sounding played; FL Studio's chord progression tool writes a progression into the piano roll once; Cubase and Studio Pro make nothing, but move notes the user wrote onto the chords ([B82](#appendix-b-sources), [B84](#appendix-b-sources)); and the generators that make patterns by rules, as Phazite's did, are reviewed as static, samey and arbitrary. The piano roll's chord stamp and its Arpeggiate transform write what the parts did, so the generator was a second way to a weak result. The chord lane is now a guide that plays nothing, so it can go away without anything playing from somewhere unseen. The progressions' preview (held triads over their roots) and the example songs' parts (held triads, spread chords and offbeat stabs) still write chords as notes, voiced to move little (`makePartNotes()`, `src/model/chords/ChordValues.h`). Making parts could come back as FL Studio's kind, a piano-roll tool that writes notes once, or as players worth hearing ([section 16](#16-ai-and-generative-features)).
- **Time.** Chords go with the song's time as sections do: inserting or removing time, and moving or copying a section or a range, move them.
- **The piano roll** tints the rows of each chord's notes while the chord lasts, in any key.
- **Not yet:** chords detected in MIDI or audio (Studio Pro's Extract to Chord Track), notes played by hand following the chords (Cubase's and Studio Pro's follow modes), outlines that suggest progressions for their sections, auditioning with the selected track's instrument, and progressions saved as presets.

## 15. Sound generator

**Decided:** generate new kicks, snares and other drum sounds as a set, then keep or favorite the ones you like each time.

**How it works for the user:**
- **Open it** from the browser's Generate tab, from a drum pad, or by right-clicking a drum track.
- **Pick a type:** kick, 808, snare, clap, closed hat, open hat, tom, rim or percussion. Optionally add character words: punchy, boomy, tight, dirty, dark, bright, short, long, lo-fi.
- **Generate** fills a grid with 12 sounds. Click one to hear it. **Audition in context** swaps it into the playing loop temporarily.
- **For each sound you can:**
  - **keep** it, which adds it to the project;
  - **favorite** it, which adds it to your library;
  - **lock** it, so it stays while the rest regenerate;
  - ask for **more like this**: the next batch varies around it, and a similarity slider sets how far;
  - drag it onto a track or a pad.
- **It uses the project:**
  - kicks, 808s and toms are tuned to the project's key;
  - decay lengths fit the tempo;
  - loudness matches the rest of the kit;
  - later, it steers away from frequencies the existing drums already fill.
- **History** steps back and forth through earlier batches.
- **Every sound keeps its recipe** (engine version, seed and parameters), so it can be reopened and tweaked in the drum synth or re-rendered at any sample rate.

**The first engine (after v1, since 2026-11-04): a drum synthesizer plus evolution.**
- **Each drum type is a synthesis patch.** A kick, for example, is a pitched oscillator with a pitch envelope, a click transient and saturation. A snare is a tuned body plus filtered noise. Hats are metallic oscillators or FM through filters.
- **Generate** samples the patch's parameters from musically tuned ranges for each character word. It then measures the results (loudness, length, brightness) and discards weak ones.
- **More like this** mutates the chosen sound's parameters. Later, "breed" combines two favorites, as Synplant does.
- **The drum synth is also a built-in instrument,** so a generated kick can stay live and editable on a track.
- **Why not a neural model first:** no suitably licensed model exists (see the licence table in [section 16](#16-ai-and-generative-features)). Synthesized drums are a staple of electronic music anyway. Realistic acoustic drums are where a trained model would add the most, later.

**Prior art** ([B14](#appendix-b-sources), [B15](#appendix-b-sources), [B16](#appendix-b-sources)):
- **Audialab Emergent Drums 2:** generates drums from scratch, and variations of a sample with a similarity slider.
- **Steinberg Backbone with Sony's DrumGAN:** smart parameters such as "kick-ness", and a variation slider.
- **Sonic Charge Synplant 2:** a seed with branches; plant a branch to evolve the sound further.

## 16. AI and generative features

**Principles:**
1. **Useful because it's in a project.** Each feature reads the project's context: key, tempo, chords, melody, sections and outline style, the sounds already used, and your past choices.
2. **Assistive.** You get suggestions you audition and accept. You own the result and can edit it.
3. **Local-first and opt-in.** Everything runs on your machine. Nothing leaves it unless you later add a cloud model yourself.
4. **Licence-clean.** Only models and data whose licences fit the AGPL.
5. **Transparent.**
   - Suggestions say why they were made.
   - The project records which generative features produced what (generator, version, seed). That makes results reproducible and lets users label their work accurately, for example with the music industry's "AI-Assisted" labels ([B17](#appendix-b-sources)).
6. **Out of scope:**
   - text-to-song generation; 82% of producers oppose it in Tracklib's 2025 survey ([B18](#appendix-b-sources));
   - stem separation, because many tools already do it (decided).

**The first features (decided):** chord suggestions and sound generation ([section 15](#15-sound-generator)), first planned for v1 and since 2026-11-04 among the first steps after it ([roadmap](roadmap.md#after-v1)). The assistant comes later.

**Their engines (decided):** algorithmic. The chord engine is built on music theory, and the sound generator on synthesis and evolution. Trained models can come later, behind the same interface.

### Chord suggestions

**What it suggests:**
- **Next chord:** after any chord on the chord lane, four to six suggestions grouped as safe, colorful or surprising.
- **Fill a section:** three to five complete progressions for the selected section, suited to its role (verse, chorus, drop) and the outline style.
- **Replace:** alternatives for a selected chord: substitutions, borrowed chords, and inversions for a smoother bass line.
- **Fit my melody:** chords that contain the melody's notes on strong beats, for a selected MIDI clip.

**How it presents them:**
- A "safe ↔ adventurous" slider sets how far suggestions stray.
- Every suggestion says why it was made: "contains your melody's C and E on beats 1 and 3", "borrowed from the parallel minor".
- You audition a suggestion in place, apply it with one click, and can undo it.

**Engine, v1:** a music-theory engine that scores candidate chords by:
- how well they fit the melody;
- smooth voice leading;
- harmonic function and cadences;
- the section's role;
- style weights for each outline (pop, rock, electronic, hip-hop).

It is instant, explains itself, works offline and needs no training data. It also learns your taste locally from the suggestions you accept.

**Engine, later:** a small trained model, if training data with a suitable licence exists. Today:
- Chordonomicon, the largest chord dataset with section and genre labels (about 680,000 songs), is non-commercial ([B19](#appendix-b-sources)).
- Hookpad's data is proprietary.
- The McGill Billboard annotations are public domain (CC0) and include verse and chorus labels, but cover only 890 chart slots ([B20](#appendix-b-sources)). That is enough to calibrate pop and rock weights, not electronic music.

**Prior art** ([B21](#appendix-b-sources), [B22](#appendix-b-sources), [B23](#appendix-b-sources)):
- **Cubase's Chord Assistant:** suggests the next chord by cadence, shared notes or the circle of fifths, and fills gaps between two chords.
- **Hookpad:** next-chord suggestions drawn from 75,000+ songs, chords that contain a given melody note, and a paid AI add-on.
- **Fender Studio Pro 8:** chord suggestions and progression presets.
- **FL Studio 2024:** a chord progression tool.

### Model licence check (October 2026)

| Candidate | Licence | Fit for Phazite |
|---|---|---|
| Stable Audio Open Small (Stability AI): 0.5B parameters, 44.1 kHz stereo, up to 11 s, trained on Creative Commons audio from Freesound and the Free Music Archive ([B24](#appendix-b-sources)) | Stability AI Community License: free below US$1M annual revenue; revocable; not sublicensable; requires "Powered by Stability AI"; outputs may not be used to train other foundation models ([B25](#appendix-b-sources)) | Can't be bundled with an AGPL app. At most an optional download that each user accepts separately |
| Meta AudioCraft (MusicGen, AudioGen) | Code MIT; weights CC-BY-NC 4.0 ([B26](#appendix-b-sources)) | No: non-commercial |
| RAVE (IRCAM) | CC BY-NC 4.0 ([B27](#appendix-b-sources)) | No: non-commercial |
| stable-audio-tools (training code) | MIT ([B28](#appendix-b-sources)) | Yes. It could train our own drum model on public-domain audio later, which needs GPU time and careful data curation |
| Chordonomicon (chord dataset) | CC-BY-NC 4.0 | No: non-commercial |
| McGill Billboard chord annotations | CC0 | Yes |

No permissively licensed drum-generation model was found. This table summarizes licence texts and is not legal advice.

**After v1:**
- **The assistant.** It acts through the command system: every action is shown before it runs and can be undone. It runs on a local model or your own API key, as Waveform 14's and Ardour 9.5's do ([B9](#appendix-b-sources), [B29](#appendix-b-sources)).
- Audio-to-MIDI.
- Automatic tagging and "find similar" for your samples.
- Neural sound generation with our own model.
- Analysis of a reference track's structure, shown against your song outline.

## 17. Recording and Capture

**v1:**
- **Live MIDI:** every MIDI keyboard, and the computer keyboard, plays one track, without arming it by hand: the selected track with an instrument (step 1.1), or the project's first track with one, whose arm switch is lit (2026-11-08, below).
- **MIDI recording** from controllers and the computer keyboard onto that track, with overdub or replace, and optional input quantize (step 1.7). It starts from where playback starts, after the count-in, and each take is one undo step. The song's own loop doesn't loop a recording, which runs on past the song's end; a range looped on purpose does.
- **Capture:** Phazite keeps the last 30 seconds of MIDI input (its engine's own since 2026-11-02), and one key turns it into a clip (step 1.7). This is Live's Capture MIDI and Logic's Flashback Capture ([B30](#appendix-b-sources)). While the song plays, the notes stay where they were played; while it is stopped, the clip goes at the play start's bar, or after the track's clips. If the project is empty, Capture also detects the tempo.
- **Audio recording:** record-arm, input monitoring, count-in, punch-in over the loop range, and basic takes.

**Audio recording as built (step 3.4, 2026-10-29, decided by Claude for the owner to review, from research into Ableton Live 12, FL Studio, Logic Pro, Studio Pro, Bitwig and REAPER):**
- **Arm:** a round arm button beside a track's M and S, in its playlist header and its mixer strip: one switch shown in both places, as FL Studio shows its arm on the mixer insert and the playlist track. Only a track without an instrument (not a folder or a return) has one: a track with an instrument plays what is played on MIDI, unarmed, as before. A click arms or disarms that track alone, as in FL Studio, Logic Pro, Studio Pro and REAPER; right-click offers the inputs, when the input is heard, and Arm Only This Track. Arming is neither saved (a project opens with nothing armed, so that a monitored microphone can't feed back as it opens) nor an undo step. **Arm Track** arms the selected tracks.
- **Input:** a track's first click on its arm button opens its input list: each of the audio device's input channels alone ("In 1"), then each pair ("In 1+2"), the one chosen last first, and No input; choosing one arms the track, as choosing an input in FL Studio's mixer does. New tracks start with no input, as Bitwig's do. While armed, the header says what it records ("In 1") under the track's name, and the strip says it where it says what feeds the channel. The inputs are those of the audio device the audio settings choose: Phazite opens an input's channels while a track needs them and closes them again after, leaving those the audio settings opened. A track's input is saved, its change an undo step ("Change Input").
- **Hearing the input (monitoring):** while the track is armed, at first, as most of the six do; or always, or never (for an audio interface that plays its input to its player itself), saved, each change an undo step. The input is heard through the track's devices and fader.
- **Record:** with no audio track armed, it records what is played on MIDI, as before; with tracks armed, their inputs too, all as one undo step ("Record"). The count-in applies to both.
- **Punch In** (Record's menu, the command transport.punch): Record keeps only what is played in the looped range, in place of what was there, as Ableton Live's and Studio Pro's punch use the loop's start and end; the take doesn't loop (it plays straight through, the loop's switch staying on). Its edges cross-fade with what was there over 10 ms, as Studio Pro's auto punch does. While the loop follows the whole song, there is nothing to punch into, and Punch In is off. While it is on, the loop shows red in the rulers, as Logic Pro's autopunch range is red. Saved with the project, as the loop is, and not an unsaved change.
- **Takes:** each time round a looped range (looped on purpose, of any length) becomes a take of one clip over the range, the last whole one playing, as in Studio Pro and Bitwig and Live's main lane; a take cut short by Stop is kept, but the last whole one plays. The clip shows "Take 2/3"; its menu lists the takes, and choosing one is one undo step ("Choose Take"); **Next Take** and **Previous Take** step through them, with no default key (T is the Mute tool). Take lanes and comping come after v1.
- **Files:** WAV files, 24-bit at the device's rate, in the project's Audio folder (the untitled project's before its first save), named "*track* Take *n*.wav".
- **In time:** each recording is placed where in the song it was played, known to the sample: Phazite's engine knows the song's sample its take started on (since 2026-11-02), and what came in is moved earlier by the delays the device's driver reports for its input and output and by the song's delay compensation; a note played live, by the output's delay and the delay compensation, as it was heard; the audio settings' **Recording correction** moves it further, for a driver that reports its delays wrongly (0 at first, as Ableton advises leaving its Driver Error Compensation).
- **How (docs/realtime-audit.md, finding 13; since 2026-11-02, `src/model/transport/internal/Take.h`):** Phazite's engine takes the audio device's inputs in its own callback, plays them through the tracks that hear them, and copies each armed track's input, from the first sample the song plays after Record (after the count-in), into a buffer of its own that no lock or allocation touches, which a thread of Phazite's writes into the file; the notes played live come out of the engine with the moment they came in.
- **Not yet:** an input meter in the input list and a clip mark that stays lit after the input reaches 0 dBFS; hearing the input only inside the punch range (Logic's auto input monitoring); and take lanes and comping (after v1).
- **Metronome** with count-in (step 1.7): Phazite's own click (since 2026-11-02), louder on each bar's first beat, and a bar of clicks before recording starts from a stop, on the song's beats before where it starts, while the song waits.

**Where a recording goes, and what shows as it records (2026-11-08, the owner: "recording is a little nebulous in phazite on where it is recording ... phazite also doesn't show the recording populating the playlist live like ableton does"; decided by Claude for the owner to review, from the [recording audit](recording-audit.md); roadmap step 4.9):**
- **Every track that records has an arm switch,** as in Ableton Live. A track with an instrument's is lit on the track live MIDI plays, as Live's auto-arm lights the selected MIDI track. A click on another's sends MIDI there, armed. A click on the lit one stops that track recording MIDI (it still plays), until it is clicked again or MIDI moves on. That isn't saved.
- **What each records shows under its name,** "In 1", "In 1+2" or "MIDI", and while Record records onto it, "Recording In 1" in the danger colour; the mixer strip says "Armed: MIDI".
- **Record's tooltip names what it records onto:** "Record onto Lead (MIDI) and Vocals (In 1)".
- **With nothing to record onto, Record isn't greyed out.** A click opens its menu, headed "Nothing records yet". Its **Record an Input on a New Track** lists the inputs. Choosing one adds a track called "Audio" after the selected one, recording that input, armed and selected, as one undo step, "Add Audio Track". FL Studio's record button asks what to record the same way, and no DAW checked makes a track by itself.
- **A clip grows on each track as it records,** in the track's colour with its edge in the danger colour, tagged "Recording" ("Recording take 2" round a loop). It runs from where recording started (round a loop, where this time round began; punched in, the punch's start) to the playhead's line. An input's waveform is drawn as it comes in. What is played on MIDI shows as notes from the first one played. Once recording stops, the take's clips take its place.
- **Recordings follow the song's tempo** from the tempo they were played at, as Live warps a recording, so that they keep to their beats when the tempo changes. One recorded where the tempo changes keeps its speed.

**After v1:** take lanes and comping, and audio Capture.

## 18. Projects, saving and export

**A project is a folder** containing:

| Item | Contents |
|---|---|
| Project file | The project itself |
| `Audio/` | Recordings, and imported audio copied in on import |
| `Generated/` | Generated sounds, with their recipes |
| `Renders/` | Exports: the song and its stems |
| `Backups/` | Earlier versions of the project file |
| `Cache/` | What Phazite can make again: frozen tracks' sound, and audio clips' stretched renders (step 2.11) |

**Saving:**
- **A new project is untitled until its first save (the owner, 2026-10-16),** as in Ableton Live and FL Studio: the first save (Save, Save As, or Save in the question when closing) asks, with the system's save dialog, what to call it and where it goes, starting in `Documents/Phazite/Projects`, and Phazite makes the project's folder of that name there. Later saves don't ask. Until then the project lives in a folder of its own in the user's application data (`%LOCALAPPDATA%\Phazite\Unsaved Projects` on Windows), so that recordings, imported audio and the autosave have a place from the start; the first save copies its folders across and goes on there with its undo history, and the untitled folder is deleted when the project closes, saved or not. An untitled project isn't in Open Recent, and Export Song offers `Documents/Phazite/Exports` for it. If Phazite didn't close properly while an untitled project had changes saved automatically, the next start offers to restore it before anything else (**Restore** or **Discard them**); untitled folders with nothing to restore are deleted then. A lock keeps a second Phazite running at the same time away from the folders the first one uses (`src/model/project/internal/UnsavedProjects.h`).
- Saves use the engine's safe save, which writes a temporary file and then renames it, and keep a rolling set of backups.
- **Autosave** writes a separate file in the background, 15 seconds after the first change, then once changes have waited two minutes, and at once when a recording ends (never during one). If Phazite crashed, the next launch offers to restore it. The status bar says how long ago the changes were autosaved ("Unsaved changes · autosaved 2 min ago", 2026-11-04, the owner: "i want to make sure there is autosaving on the project"), so that it shows; a save deletes the autosave, and the status bar says Saved.
- **Unsaved changes** (the `*` in the title bar, the question when closing, and what autosave writes) are what the project holds that it didn't hold when it was opened or last saved. Notifications that change nothing don't count, so opening or making a project never shows a `*`, and what a new project starts with (the starter song, an outline's sections) is part of its first save, not an undo step (2026-10-14).
- Project files contain no personal data, and media paths are relative to the project folder ([section 22](#22-privacy-licences-and-trademarks)).
- The project file is the song alone, Phazite's own document: its tracks and clips with their notes, its devices, automation and modulation, its sections and outline style, track keys, the chord lane, and later the window layout. One schema file lists all of it, and Phazite saves its own values, names or its own enums, never a library's enum numbers (2026-10-14, [section 24](#24-code-structure)).
- **No backward compatibility in pre-alpha (the owner, 2026-10-18):** a project, preset or settings file saved by an earlier build of Phazite needn't open, or keep what it held, so nothing in Phazite reads an old format: projects carry no format version, nothing converts them as they open, and Phazite keeps no code, tests or comments for what earlier builds saved or played (the migrations of format versions 0 to 11, a frozen 0.1.0 project in the golden tests, and the support for the built-in devices the first projects held, from before Phazite had devices of its own, went then). Names and IDs that projects save may change when that makes the code clearer. A project file that doesn't open leaves Open Recent with an error that says so, as any unreadable file does. The owner will say when compatibility starts; format versions, upgrades of older projects and the handling of a newer Phazite's projects ([question 17](#25-open-questions)) come back then.
- **View state** (track heights, collapsed and pinned tracks, drum mode and drum rows, the grids' settings, and since step 2.12 where plug-in windows were) is saved with the project, so it reopens as it was left. Changing it isn't an undo step and doesn't count as an unsaved change: no `*` in the title bar and no question when closing, as with the playhead's position (2026-10-14).
- **How the song plays (2026-10-16, [question 18](#25-open-questions)):** whether the loop is on, whether it loops the song or a range, and the metronome's switch are saved with the project too, and, like where the loop is and where playback starts, switching them isn't an unsaved change: they are about playing the song, not the song.

**Export:**
- WAV and FLAC; no MP3 (the owner, 2026-10-16, [question 5](#25-open-questions)).
- Choice of sample rate and bit depth.
- Normalize to a loudness target (LUFS) with a true-peak ceiling.
- Effect tails, and export of a chosen range or sections.
- Stems: one file per track or bus.

**Export Song (step 1.8, 2026-10-15, proposed):** File > Export Song renders the song from its start to its end (the end of the bar its last clip ends in), then its tails until they fall silent, 10 seconds at most; muted tracks stay silent. It writes WAV or FLAC at 44.1, 48, 88.2 or 96 kHz, in 16 bits (dithered), 24 bits, or 32-bit float for WAV. **Normalize** turns the whole song up or down to an integrated loudness (EBU R 128), never taking its true peak over a ceiling; it doesn't limit, so a mix with high peaks can end up under the target, and the report then says by how much and suggests the limiter. The file goes into the project's `Renders` folder, named after the project; it is written next to that name and replaces a file there only once it is complete, so a cancelled or failed export leaves the old one. The export runs in the background with a progress bar and Cancel; playback stops for it. A range and sections come later; stems came in step 2.11.

**Stems (step 2.11, 2026-10-18, decided by Claude for the owner to review):** the Export window's first choice is what it exports: the song, or its stems. Stems are a file for each channel that reaches the master (each track outside a bus, each bus with the tracks in it, and each return), so that together they add up to the mix without the master's devices, as FL Studio's "Split mixer tracks" and Studio One's channel stems give. Each goes through its devices, fader and pan; its sends are left to the returns' own stems, and the tracks that feed it through a sidechain or a send play silently while it renders, so it sounds as it does in the mix. A channel that is muted, or that a solo leaves silent, has no stem. They all start at the song's start, so they line up wherever they are imported, and run to its end with their own tails, as the song does. Their format, sample rate and bit depth are the song's; normalizing doesn't apply, so they keep their levels. They go into a folder named after the project, "<project> Stems", in the Renders folder, each file named after its channel and numbered in the mixer's order ("01 Kick.wav"); exporting them again replaces that folder, after asking, and the old one goes to the recycle bin.

**Bounce in Place (step 2.11, decided by Claude for the owner to review):** a track's menu bounces it: its notes or audio through its instrument and devices, before its fader, as Ableton Live and Logic Pro bounce, from the song's start to the end of its last clip and its tail, into a file in the project's Audio folder. The file plays on a new audio track right under the original, named after it with "(Bounce)", in its color, with its fader, pan, sends and output. The original is muted and collapsed, as FL Studio's consolidating leaves the original clips muted in a collapsed track: nothing is lost, and nothing hidden plays. One undo step. A device that listens to a sidechain hears its source while bouncing. Folders, buses and returns aren't bounced (their sound is their tracks': stems export it).

**Freeze (step 2.11, decided by Claude for the owner to review):** **Freeze Track** in a track's menu renders the track's notes or audio through its instrument and devices, up to its fader, and plays that file instead, so a heavy plug-in instrument takes no CPU, as Logic Pro's freeze does in its default (pre-fader) mode. The fader, pan, mute, solo, the sends and the devices after the frozen part keep working: a send, before the fader or after it, takes the sound after the devices, the frozen file's included. The frozen part stops before a device that listens to a sidechain, so that it keeps hearing the other track live.
- **A frozen track shows it:** a snowflake in its header (a click unfreezes it), frosted clips, and its frozen devices greyed, their controls off and their plug-in windows closed ("Unfreeze it to change them").
- **What plays is what is shown (the owner's rule):** changing what a frozen track froze (its clips or notes, or its frozen devices, also by undo or redo) unfreezes it, so its sound follows the change.
- **Freezing and unfreezing aren't undo steps,** as freezing in Logic Pro is a switch on the track and Phazite's mute and solo aren't: a track keeps one frozen file, so undo could otherwise bring back a frozen state whose file a later freeze had replaced. The snowflake unfreezes.
- **It runs in the background,** with a progress window and Cancel, as exporting does.
- **The frozen sound is kept in the project's Cache folder,** so a frozen track opens frozen the next time, as Ableton Live and Logic Pro keep theirs in the project. Without its file (if the Cache folder was deleted), the track opens unfrozen. Closing a project deletes what nothing in it uses any more.
- Returns, buses and folders aren't frozen, as in Ableton Live.

**After v1:** archives that include the media. DAWproject import and export were built ahead of v1, with the versions ([versions.md](versions.md), D1 and D2, 2026-11-04), but for a DAWproject's plug-ins and sends imported into a new project.

## 19. Plug-in hosting and stability

**Formats:**
- v1: VST3 on Windows and Linux.
- Later: Audio Units on macOS.
- After v1: CLAP. JUCE doesn't host CLAP, so the helper program needs a plug-in format of its own for it, built on the MIT-licensed CLAP SDK.
- Later: LV2 on Linux.

**Scanning:**
- Plug-ins are scanned in the helper program, a file at a time, so a plug-in that crashes or hangs as it is scanned takes only the helper with it (`model/engine/internal/PluginScanner.h`).
- Each file has a time limit, so a hanging plug-in can't stall the scan: one that takes longer is left out, and later scans skip it.
- Plug-ins that fail to scan are listed.

**v1 crash protection:**
- autosave and crash recovery ([section 18](#18-projects-saving-and-export));
- a plug-in that crashed while loading is disabled on the next start and reported.

**Before v1 (the owner, 2026-10-16, [question 7](#25-open-questions)): sandboxed hosting, on by default,** unless measurements show that it costs a lot of performance; then it is a choice per plug-in.
- Plug-ins run in helper processes. If one crashes, or hangs and is ended, its slot shows its name with "Crashed" instead of Phazite closing, and a click on it (or Reload in its menu) loads it again from the state it last had; one that crashes as it loads stays off, at the next start too, until it is reloaded (done 2026-11-02, T3).
- Grouping options trade memory for isolation, as in Bitwig: all together, by vendor, or one per plug-in ([B31](#appendix-b-sources)). **Decided 2026-11-02 from T3 step 0's measurement ([performance-audit.md](performance-audit.md), section 10):** one helper for each plug-in by default, every instance of it in that helper with an audio thread of its own, as Bitwig's default; a round trip to a helper costs about 28 microseconds a block (1 % of a 128-sample block for each plug-in in a chain), and nothing measurable when the engine plays tracks side by side, so sandboxing is on and the only way Phazite hosts plug-ins.
- *Why on by default:* Waveform's sandboxing is off by default, and a MusicTech reviewer lost the whole app to one plug-in ([B9](#appendix-b-sources)).

**Stable parameter identity:** the song names every plug-in parameter it refers to (its automation, what modulates it, its value set by hand, its automation lane) by the plug-in's own ID for it (a VST3's), never by its index, so that a plug-in update that reorders its parameters can't move saved automation onto another (since 2026-10-28; `model/devices/internal/Parameters.h`). The helper that hosts the plug-in finds each parameter by that ID (`src/sandbox/core/PluginInfo.h`).

## 20. Performance

**Targets** (to be measured; see the [roadmap](roadmap.md)):

| Area | Target |
|---|---|
| Audio | No dropouts at a 128-sample buffer (about 3 ms) with a typical electronic project: 20–40 tracks and a dozen third-party synths, on a mid-range laptop |
| UI | 60 fps scrolling and zooming in the playlist and piano roll with 100+ tracks. Near-zero CPU when idle: only what changed gets repainted |
| Startup | Under 3 seconds to a playable starter project on an SSD, with plug-in scanning in the background |
| Memory | Long audio streams from disk rather than loading into memory |

**Real-time rules:** the audio-thread rules in `CLAUDE.md` apply to all Phazite code.
- **Checked, not assumed:** Phazite's engine, its devices and the channel to the plug-ins' helpers keep to them, and the [real-time audit](realtime-audit.md) checks what the audio thread does: it counts allocations there in guarded scenarios, which fail if steady playback allocates, and lists what is known of the code Phazite builds on (JUCE's device callbacks). The sanitizer builds (AddressSanitizer, ThreadSanitizer) run the tests in CI.

**Heavy tracks:** freeze and bounce in place relieve them, and a per-track CPU meter shows where the time goes. After v1, tracks that aren't being edited can be pre-rendered in the background, like Digital Performer's PreGen.

**Latency** (the owner's decisions, 2026-10-21, from the [performance audit](performance-audit.md)):
- **First run:** Phazite picks "Windows Audio (Low Latency Mode)" with a buffer of 128 to 256 samples where the output offers one, and Windows' shared mode with the device's own buffer otherwise. It doesn't pick ASIO by itself: an ASIO driver takes its device from every other app, and which driver belongs to the output in use is only a guess from names. The audio settings say that an audio interface's own ASIO driver gives the lowest delay.
- **Low latency switch:** a "Low latency" switch in the transport bar, as Cubase's Constrain Delay Compensation and Studio One's Low Latency Monitoring do, off at first: while it is on, devices that look ahead (the Limiter and the Multiband, and plug-ins that report latency) are bypassed and the buffer shrinks, so that what is played live has no delay compensation.
- **Bluetooth outputs:** the audio settings and the welcome window say when the output is Bluetooth, and what it costs (about 150 to 250 ms between a key and its sound), and suggest a wired output for playing and recording.
- **Shown:** the audio settings show the buffer and the output's latency in milliseconds, and the song's delay compensation.

## 21. Accessibility

- **Keyboard:** every action can be reached from the keyboard: the command palette, moving focus between panels, and arrow-key editing of notes and automation points.
  - **A view its key shows gets the keys (roadmap step 3.6, 2026-11-04):** F5, F6, Shift+F6, F7 and F9 move the keyboard focus into the view they show (the playlist, the instrument, the effects, the piano roll or the mixer), so that a screen reader says its name and Tab goes on into its controls. The mixer and the Devices view take the focus themselves, as the playlist does, so that the arrow keys still run commands until Tab reaches a control (a test in `tests/AppShellTests.cpp`).
- **Screen readers:**
  - Every custom control gets a JUCE accessibility handler with a name, role and value.
  - **Checked across the window (step 3.6):** every visible control, and every component that takes the keyboard, has a name and a role for screen readers. That includes the scrolling areas, each named for what it scrolls ("Channels", "Properties"); a tree takes the keys itself, without a stop of its own for its scrolling (a test in `tests/AppShellTests.cpp`, with the playlist, the mixer, the piano roll and the Devices view showing).
  - JUCE supports Windows UI Automation and macOS; it has no Linux back end yet ([B33](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **Vision:**
  - themes meet WCAG 2.2 contrast: 4.5:1 for text and 3:1 for controls ([B34](#appendix-b-sources));
  - color is never the only signal: aliases, for example, show an icon as well as a tint;
  - UI scaling and HiDPI apply everywhere, including plug-in windows.
- **Benchmarks:** REAPER with OSARA, and Live 12.

## 22. Privacy, licences and trademarks

**Privacy:**
- **No account and no telemetry by default.** Any future crash reporting is opt-in.
- **No personal data in project files.** A project file is the song alone, without the user's name, and the files it plays are kept by their paths within the project's folder.
- **API keys** for optional cloud AI (later) are stored in the operating system's credential store, never in projects or settings files.
- **Downloads (2026-10-13):** Phazite connects to the internet only when the user asks it to download something, so far only Phazite Sounds (from GitHub, over HTTPS). It checks each download against the SHA-256 it was built with.
- **Updates (step 4.1, 2026-11-07):** Phazite asks for a newer version only when the user asks (Help > Check for Updates...), or each time it opens once the user has turned that on (Check for Updates When Phazite Opens, off until then, in the Updates window and the command search). A check reads one small file from Phazite's latest GitHub release and sends nothing but the request.

**Licences:**
- **Compatibility:** only AGPL-compatible code, models and data; the list is in `CLAUDE.md`.
- **Notices:** third-party notices ship with the app, for example Rubber Band Library (GPL-2.0-or-later, `licenses/RubberBand.txt` next to the app), the Inter font (SIL OFL 1.1) and VS Code's, whose themes' colours and layout Phazite's look takes (MIT, `licenses/VSCode.txt`).
  - **One list (step 4.2, 2026-11-07):** `resources/licenses/notices.json` names everything from others compiled into the app and its helper, JUCE's bundled libraries included (from JUCE's bill of materials, [B150](#appendix-b-sources)), with its version, licence, copyright and use. CMake copies each one's text into `licenses/` next to the app, with Phazite's licence (`Phazite.txt`) and the GPL, version 3, that the ASIO SDK is used under, and writes `NOTICES.txt` from the list; the installer shows `NOTICES.txt` on its first page, and installs them all.
  - **The About box (Help > About Phazite):** the logo, the version (Copy Version copies it with the system's name, for a report), "Copyright (C) 2026 the Phazite contributors" (the owner's choice: no personal name), the notice the AGPL asks every interactive program under it to show (that it comes with no warranty, that it may be shared and changed under the licence, and where the licence is: [B149](#appendix-b-sources)), a link to the source code, then every licence in a list, Phazite's first, the text of the one chosen beside it, and the trademarks' sentences under them.
- **Fonts:** fonts may use the SIL Open Font License 1.1. They ship as separate files with their licence text.
- **Sounds:** Phazite Sounds are CC0 1.0 and a separate download from their own repository; the program never includes sounds (its metronome's click and its startup sound are synthesized as they play).
- **No proprietary code:** nothing under a proprietary licence alone is compiled in; the ASIO SDK headers bundled with JUCE are used under Steinberg's GPLv3 option.

**Trademarks:**
- VST is a registered trademark of Steinberg Media Technologies GmbH.
- ASIO is a registered trademark of Steinberg Media Technologies GmbH.
- Visual Studio Code is a trademark of Microsoft Corporation. Phazite isn't affiliated with or endorsed by Microsoft, and its themes don't carry VS Code's name.
- Other product names in this document are trademarks of their respective owners. They are used only to describe and compare.
- **Presets after classic instruments (2026-11-08, the owner asked to name a preset "TR-808" only if allowed; [presets.md](presets.md)):** they are named by their sound, not a trademark. "808" and "909" are the words for sounds they have become (Ableton ships a "Kit-Core 808" and a "909 Core Kit"; no registration of either alone was found, and dictionaries define "808" as the sound); "TR-808", "TR-909", "TB-303" and "Roland" are never used, as Roland registered them for plug-ins and sound recordings in 2020 and 2021 and enforces its marks. Every other classic is named by its sound (Acid Bass, Tine E-Piano, String Machine), as Ableton, Logic and Bitwig name theirs, and near-variants of a mark aren't used either. The About box's notice covers them: other names of products are their owners' trademarks, named only to say what Phazite works with or what a sound is like, and Phazite isn't affiliated with their owners. Before a release, a trademark lawyer should look at the list, especially for the EU, where presenting a product as an imitation of a named one is on weaker ground than in the US.
- **No ASIO or VST logos (the owner, 2026-11-07):** Phazite uses the ASIO SDK under the GPL, version 3, and the VST 3 SDK under the MIT licence, under which using the names or the logos is optional, and if used, Steinberg's usage guidelines apply (the ASIO SDK's README, the VST 3 developer portal: [B146](#appendix-b-sources), [B147](#appendix-b-sources)). Their "Compatible" logos would go in the About box and in every dialog that configures ASIO, but they aren't under a free licence, so shipping them would break the licence-clean rule. Phazite names both in plain text, as it must to say which driver or plug-in format to choose, with ® at the first mention and the attribution the guidelines' section 15 asks for, "ASIO is a registered trademark of Steinberg Media Technologies GmbH." and "VST is a registered trademark of Steinberg Media Technologies GmbH.", in the About box, `NOTICES.txt` and the documentation. If Steinberg asks for the logo where ASIO is configured, it can be added there.

**Installing and updating (step 4.1, 2026-11-07, decided by Claude as the owner asked):**
- **The installer is Inno Setup's,** as most open-source audio apps on Windows use (Surge XT's, Vital's, Audacity's): free (its licence is zlib's, with a request that companies buy a licence, which Phazite isn't), already on GitHub's Windows runners ([B148](#appendix-b-sources)), and with an uninstaller, file associations and a per-user install built in. `packaging/windows/Phazite.iss`, through CMake's `installer` target.
- **For the user who installs it, without administrator rights,** in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\Phazite`, as VS Code's user installer does, so that an update needs no UAC prompt; `/ALLUSERS` installs for every user, in Program Files. Its first page shows `NOTICES.txt`, with Next, not "I accept": the GPL family's licences don't ask to be accepted to use the program.
- **File associations:** `.phazite` opens in Phazite, with its icon; `.dawproject` gets Phazite in its Open With menu, which imports it, without taking the file type from the DAW the user has. A file opened while Phazite runs goes to that Phazite (one runs at a time), which asks to save the open project first.
- **The uninstaller** takes the app, its shortcuts and its associations away, and leaves the user's projects, presets, Phazite Sounds and settings, as uninstallers do.
- **Updates come from Phazite's GitHub releases:** each release carries `phazite-update.json` (its version, its page, and on Windows its installer's address, size and SHA-256), which the installer target writes. Phazite takes an installer only from its own releases, over HTTPS only, following redirects only to HTTPS, never more bytes than the update file says, checks its SHA-256 once it has it and again just before it runs it, and runs it only when the user clicks Install and Restart: the project is saved first if the user wants, Phazite closes, and the installer, which waits for it to close, updates it silently and opens it again. Once Phazite is signed (step 4.3), it also checks that the installer is signed by the same publisher.
- **A newer release shows** as a badge on the settings' icon at the activity bar's foot, as VS Code marks its Manage icon, and in the update command's name ("Update to Phazite 0.2.0..."), never as a window that opens by itself.

## 23. Phazite's engine

Phazite plays, records and renders its songs with code of its own, on JUCE for the audio devices, the plug-in formats and the windows ([section 24](#24-code-structure) has the layers).

**The song is Phazite's own document** (`src/model/base/Song.h`, every name in it defined once in `model/base/internal/Schema.h`): its tempo map, tracks and folders, clips with their notes, devices, containers, sends, automation, modulators and macros, sections and the chord lane, each item by an ID the song gives (`Song::makeID()`), never given twice nor taken back by an undo. A project file is the song alone, saved as XML (`model/project/internal/Project.h`), and the song keeps its own undo history, where every change is one named step ([section 24](#24-code-structure)); undo puts back what plays, the values set by hand included (`phazite::undo()`). Positions are beats, so clips, notes, automation and sections stay on their beats when the tempo changes.

**The playback engine** (`src/engine/`, engine-neutral: JUCE's core, audio and DSP modules and Phazite's devices only):
- The model compiles the song into a plan (`model/playback/internal/SongPlayer.h`, `engine/core/Plan.h`): each track's notes, audio clips, devices, automation and fader, its sends, and the buses and the master they reach. The engine is handed each plan whole and frees nothing on the audio thread (`engine/core/Handover.h`); what plays from one plan to the next (each strip and channel, each device, each modulator) is kept by the song's IDs, so a sound rings on through an edit, and a value set by hand reaches what plays at once, without a new plan.
- Automation and modulation move what they move on the song's control points, one every 32 samples from its start (`engine/automation/Curve.h`), so a song plays the same at any buffer size ([section 10](#10-automation-and-modulation)).
- Delay compensation lines up every path to the master, the sends' and the sidechains' too; the Low Latency switch leaves it off for what is played live ([section 20](#20-performance)).
- Worker threads help play each block, a strip a task, at the audio thread's priority (`engine/core/Workers.h`); audio files are read ahead of where they play by a reading thread, so the audio thread never waits for the disk (`engine/audio/FileReader.h`).
- Recording is the engine's too: it knows the sample a take started on, and copies the armed inputs into buffers that a thread of Phazite's writes into files ([section 17](#17-recording-and-capture)). The metronome's click is synthesized (`engine/transport/Click.h`), and the master's loudness measured by EBU R128 ([section 8](#8-the-mixer)).
- Each open project has a playback session (`model/playback/internal/PlaybackSession.h`), which gives the engine the song again as it changes, holds its plug-ins and records its takes. The engine context owns the audio device (`model/engine/internal/AudioIO.h`), the plug-in host and the engine on the device, with a second engine beside it for the browser's previews (`model/playback/internal/PlaybackHost.h`).
- Exports, stems, freezes and bounces play the song offline on a thread of their own, through the same engine, the same to the last bit however many threads play it (`model/playback/internal/OfflineRender.h`).

**Plug-ins run in helper processes** ([section 19](#19-plug-in-hosting-and-stability)): `PhaziteHost`, one for each plug-in file, each block a round trip through shared memory with a deadline (`src/sandbox/`, `src/host/`, `model/plugins/internal/`), so a plug-in that crashes or hangs takes only its own helper with it. Scanning runs in a helper too, a file at a time with a time limit.

**Phazite's own devices** ([section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets)) are engine-neutral DSP (`src/devices/`), which the engine plays through `engine::DeviceRunner`: it glides their parameters, applies an effect's mix with the sound coming in delayed to match, plays a mono channel in stereo and lets a device sleep on silence.

**Stretching and pitching audio clips** (step 2.18, 2026-10-28): a clip that follows the song's tempo, is pitched or is reversed plays a render that Phazite makes in the background with Rubber Band Library's R3 engine (GPL-2.0-or-later, `src/devices/stretch/`, `model/arrangement/internal/ClipRenders.h`) into the project's Cache folder; the browser's previews stretch the same way, rendered into memory as a loop starts and played as soon as a quarter of a second is ready, the render keeping ahead, so that a preview sounds as its clip will (`model/engine/internal/PreviewLoop.h`). Tests check that a stretched drum loop plays every hit once, on its beat, and that a held chord stays clean between its notes (`tests/StretchTests.cpp`). Why R3:
- **Signalsmith Stretch against SoundTouch (measured 2026-10-17):** Signalsmith sounds cleaner (pitch-shifted a voice and a chord, it leaves 9 and 15 dB less energy outside the notes than SoundTouch), but it takes about three times as long to render (3 to 4 seconds for a 3-minute recording, against 1.3), delays its sound by 100 to 134 ms, and stretching as it plays, as the browser's preview does, it costs ten times as much and spends 45% of an audio block catching up with its delay each time a preview loops. Measured by where the energy of clicks on each beat lands, rendering offline as export does.
- **Stretched drum hits played twice (measured 2026-10-28, from the owner's report that stretched samples stutter on snares, sometimes):** SoundTouch stretches by cutting the audio into 60 ms pieces and overlapping each where it best matches the last, within 25 ms. Slowing a loop down, it plays a few milliseconds twice here and there, and a drum hit there plays twice, 5 to 20 ms apart: a flam, which on a snare sounds like a stutter, and a render plays the same hits twice every time. Measured with a temporary test on loops of hits at 120 BPM, each hit made of its own noise so that every copy of it can be found in the stretched audio, and on a held chord (how much of its energy lands between its notes; the chord itself has -61 dB):
  - **SoundTouch** (its Better setting; Normal does as badly): slowed to 115, 110, 100 and 90 BPM, 13, 20, 30 and 38% of 16th-note hits play twice (22 to 50% of a snare-like loop's eighths), the copy up to as loud as the hit; sped up, some attacks come out weaker; hits land 8 to 28 ms early. The chord: -6 to -10 dB. About 500 times real time to render one channel.
  - **Signalsmith Stretch,** its delay compensated: no hit twice, each within about 2 ms of its beat and as loud, with a soft smear in the 25 ms before it (+3 to +12 dB there). The chord: -10 to -23 dB. 80 to 125 times real time.
  - **Rubber Band's R3 engine,** offline: no hit twice, each within about a millisecond of its beat, attacks within 1 dB, a smaller smear (+1 to +7 dB). The chord: -16 to -29 dB, the cleanest. 18 to 25 times real time: a 4-bar stereo loop in under a second, a 3-minute stereo recording in about 20 seconds.
