  - **Rubber Band's R2 engine,** as it plays, with its percussive or melodic options: drums clean and on time within a millisecond, but the chord -3 to -4 dB, muddier than SoundTouch.
  - **Slices at each hit,** each played at its own speed from its beat, as Ableton Live's Beats mode does: every hit an exact copy, exactly on time, but silent gaps when slowing down, and no way to stretch a held note.

  R3 was the one stretcher that did well on everything, so Phazite stretches with it alone, offline, behind an interface of its own (`src/devices/stretch/Stretch.h`). Rubber Band Library is GPL-2.0-or-later, which Phazite's AGPL-3.0-or-later allows.

**Points to keep in mind:**
- **Unsaved changes:** JUCE notifies a value written again as another type, which changes nothing, so `phazite::Project` keeps a copy of the song as opened or last saved, and counts an unsaved change only when what counts differs from it (2026-10-14). What a new project starts with goes into its first save (`Project::create()`), so it starts without undo steps or unsaved changes.
- **What isn't an undo step:** mute and solo, freezing, the loop, and view state ([section 24](#24-code-structure)).
- **Devices' own states:** what a plug-in holds of its own (its settings, program and channel layout) is stored into the song outside the undo history, before the song is saved or copied (`Song::storeDeviceStates()`); a change made in its window counts as an unsaved change.
- **Aliases:** each clip of an alias group holds the group's notes in its own element, so an edit changes every clip of the group in the same undo step.
- **Sections and time edits:** Phazite's time edits (`src/model/arrangement/internal/Arrangement.h`) make a section longer or shorter rather than cutting it in two (step 1.2).
- **Plug-in parameters:** the song names them by the plug-ins' own IDs, which updates keep ([section 19](#19-plug-in-hosting-and-stability)).
- **Real-time rules:** [section 20](#20-performance).

**How Phazite came to its own engine:**
- **An engine of Phazite's own was a hedge, not a goal (decided 2026-10-14).** *Why:* a rewrite leaves features behind for a long time (Audacity 4.0 shipped without MIDI tracks or the mixer, [B49](#appendix-b-sources)), and "Why you shouldn't write a DAW" explains what writing an engine costs ([B55](#appendix-b-sources)). So the code was structured for a part to be replaced (after architecture phase A3, only the model reached the engine, behind Phazite's own functions, IDs and values: [section 24](#24-code-structure)), and a part was replaced only where Phazite gained something, one capability at a time, each after a decision dated in the [decision log](#26-decision-log): an interface in front of that part, the replacement built behind it, and the old code deleted after the switch ([B54](#appendix-b-sources)), with the golden tests, which compare saved projects and short renders with committed files, as the safety net. Phazite's own devices were engine-neutral from step 1.6.
- **The first parts (2026-10-28 and 2026-10-30):** stretching (step 2.18), then presets in Phazite's own format, the selection held by IDs, waveforms and sections of Phazite's own, and the modulation of Phazite's devices ([roadmap](roadmap.md#architecture-track), A5).
- **The whole engine (planned 2026-10-30, at the owner's request; done 2026-11-04):** the song model first, then the playback engine and recording, plug-ins in helper processes, the audio and MIDI devices and the devices, renders and previews, and a build on JUCE alone ([roadmap](roadmap.md#architecture-track), A6, phases T0 to T6), each phase begun on the owner's go-ahead, dated in the decision log.

## 24. Code structure

*Decided 2026-10-14*, after a review of Phazite's code and of how other open-source DAWs keep their layers apart ([appendix A](#appendix-a-research-summary)). The structure aims for three things:
- the UI can be redesigned without touching the rules of editing;
- every edit can be tested without a window, and later run by scripts and the assistant, through commands;
- a part of the engine could be replaced without rewriting the UI ([section 23](#23-phazites-engine)).

The roadmap's [architecture track](roadmap.md#architecture-track) got there in phases A0 to A6. After each step the app builds, behaves the same and passes its tests.

**Layers.** Each row uses only the rows below it:

| Folder | What it holds | Built into |
|---|---|---|
| `src/app/` | Creates everything and connects it: startup, the main window with its menus and dialogs, and the open project. It owns the services and hands them to what needs them | `phazite_app`, a library the tests link, and `Main.cpp`, the program |
| `src/ui/` | What the user sees and touches: `theme/` (design tokens, look and feel, icons), `components/` (the base controls), `input/` (keys, keymaps, the computer keyboard, keys over plug-in windows, drag and drop), `devices/` (device menus and windows), and a folder for each view: the time line, the playlist, the piano roll, the mixer, the browser and the window's shell | `phazite_ui` |
| `src/services/` | What the app keeps outside any project: settings (every key in one list), recent projects, the Phazite Sounds download and the browser's library index | `phazite_core` |
| `src/model/` | The song model: a folder for each area (`arrangement/` for tracks, clips, sections and the grid, `notes/`, `chords/`, `mixer/`, `automation/`, `modulation/`, `presets/`, `transport/`, `project/`, `engine/`), each with the functions the UI calls and, in its `internal/` folder, its insides for the model, the app and the tests; `playback/`, which compiles the song into what Phazite's engine plays; `plugins/`, the plug-ins hosted in helper processes; `commands/`, the command registry without UI code, with undo; and `base/`, which every area builds on: the song, its IDs and beats, and the schema of what a project saves | `phazite_core` |
| `src/host/` | The helper program that hosts plug-ins for Phazite (`PhaziteHost`, next to the app), which nothing uses | `phazite_host`, and the program |
| `src/engine/` | Phazite's playback engine: it plays what the model compiles from the song, live and in renders ([section 23](#23-phazites-engine)) | `phazite_core` |
| `src/sandbox/` | What Phazite shares with the helpers: shared memory, the channel a plug-in's blocks go through, the control channel's messages | `phazite_core` |
| `src/devices/` | From step 1.6: Phazite's own instruments and effects, the modulators and the stretcher, as engine-neutral DSP that the layers above may all use | `phazite_core` |
| JUCE | Windows, audio and MIDI devices, audio files, DSP, and the plug-in formats the helpers host | `phazite_core` |

**Rules.** From phase A0, `tools/check-architecture.sh` counts what breaks rules 1 to 4 and 7 in CI and before every commit. Where code still breaks a rule, the count has a limit that can only go down, so nothing new breaks it while the old cases are fixed; a commit that raises a limit on purpose says why in its message, and CI checks that it does.

1. **Dependencies point down only:** the app uses the UI, the UI uses the services and the model, the services may use the model, and the model the engine and the sandbox; the helper program uses the sandbox; every layer may use the devices, which use none of them; never the other way round.
2. **No window code in the model:** the model and the services include none of JUCE's window modules (`juce_gui_basics`, `juce_gui_extra`) and name no window class; key and mouse handling, pixel sizes and how values are shown belong in the UI. For now the model may still use `juce_graphics`, for color values. *None has since phase A1: the model's hook for devices' windows moved to the app, and the architecture check counts no window module or window class in the model or the services.*
3. **The UI decides nothing about the project:** it never changes the project or starts an undo step; it runs a command or calls a model function. *None since phase A2 (2026-10-15): the UI began 46 undo steps and changed the project directly in about 20 places when A2 began, and the architecture check counts no undo step begun in the UI.*
4. **Phazite's own types across boundaries:** objects pass between layers as IDs and plain values, never as pointers to the song's or the engine's insides. The UI includes none of the model's `internal/` headers, where each area keeps them. *The UI named the engine's types in 546 places and included its headers in 7 files at the end of phase A1, and none after phase A3 (2026-10-16), whose UI library compiled without the engine's headers; the architecture check counts the model's `internal/` headers included in the UI (none).*
5. **Tokens and strings:** every color, size, corner radius and font in the UI comes from a design token, and every text the user reads from a strings table (phase A4).
6. **Fast data is polled:** meters, the playhead and other values that change many times a second are read from small read-only sources on a timer, never stored in the project or sent as change messages (phase A3).
7. **One named undo step per change:** every change to the project happens inside one named undo step, owned by a command or by the model function the UI called; from phase A2, a drag is a gesture session that opens one step and keeps or rolls it back when the drag ends. Changes that are deliberately not undo steps (mute and solo, the loop, view state) go through model functions too.

**What stays the same, on purpose:**
- The song is the single store of project data, as Audacity 4 kept its old storage ([B48](#appendix-b-sources)).
- One library, `phazite_core`, compiles JUCE once for every program.
- Commands stay the public API: menus, shortcuts, the palette, macros and named undo use them, and later scripts and the assistant will ([section 12](#12-commands-keys-and-help)).
- The app passes each object the services it needs; at Phazite's size that is simpler than a dependency-injection framework.
- The busiest views (the playlist, the piano roll, the meters) stay painted in C++; a designer works through the tokens and the gallery app ([design language](#design-language)).
- The real-time rules in `CLAUDE.md` don't change.

**Project data (decided 2026-10-14):**
- The song is the project store: a `.phazite` file is the song, its `PHAZITE` element, saved as XML, which Phazite writes and reads itself (the song alone since 2026-10-30, roadmap A6, T1).
- One schema file names every property Phazite saves and marks which are view state ([section 18](#18-projects-saving-and-export)). It carried a format version, with migrations that converted older projects, until the owner decided on 2026-10-18 that pre-alpha keeps no backward compatibility ([section 18](#18-projects-saving-and-export)); versions and migrations come back when the owner says compatibility starts.
- Phazite saves its own values, never a library's enum numbers, so that a library's update can't change what a saved project means.
- Notes have stable IDs saved in the project (phase A3, 2026-10-16), so that selections, undo and scripts can refer to one note reliably: each note has one that no other note of its clip has, and a copy of a clip, an alias too, keeps them (`schema::phaziteNoteID`).

## 25. Open questions

1. ~~AI engines in v1~~ **Decided:** algorithmic in v1, trained models later ([section 16](#16-ai-and-generative-features)).
2. ~~Outline styles first~~ **Decided:** pop, rock, electronic, and hip-hop/trap ([section 13](#13-getting-started-and-song-outlines)).
3. ~~UI font~~ **Decided:** Inter, bundled, under the SIL Open Font License; `CLAUDE.md` now allows the OFL for fonts ([design language](#design-language)).
4. ~~Engine fixes~~ **Decided (2026-10-12):** simple workarounds first, a fork if they got awkward, and problems reported upstream either way ([roadmap](roadmap.md), step 0.3); replacing a part of the engine outright was a separate choice, made one capability at a time after a dated decision (2026-10-14). The engine is Phazite's own now ([section 23](#23-phazites-engine)), so its problems are fixed in Phazite's own code.
5. ~~MP3 export~~ **Decided (2026-10-16, the owner):** no MP3 export. Phazite exports WAV and FLAC.
6. ~~Airwindows~~ **Decided (2026-10-16, the owner):** plug-ins that aren't Phazite's own, such as Airwindows' effects and Surge XT, are never built into Phazite. Phazite may offer them as optional downloads, as it offers Phazite Sounds, and its own devices keep one design language ([section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets)).
7. ~~Plug-in sandboxing~~ **Decided (2026-10-16, the owner):** before v1, on by default, unless it costs a lot of performance: other people's plug-ins mustn't be able to crash Phazite ([section 19](#19-plug-in-hosting-and-stability)).
8. ~~Ableton Live keymap~~ **Decided (2026-10-16, the owner):** in v1.
9. ~~Single-key tools and the computer keyboard~~ **Decided:** Ableton Live's way. While the computer keyboard plays notes, Shift and a letter run that letter's single-key shortcut ([section 12](#12-commands-keys-and-help)).
10. ~~The computer keyboard at startup~~ **Decided (2026-10-16):** it starts off, so that the tools' letters (P, S, T, D, E, C, Z) work; M or the transport bar's button turns it on, and Phazite remembers the choice. The digits 1 to 9 pick the tools either way.

Raised by the mixer research (2026-10-13), each with what the research recommends:

11. ~~Folders as buses~~ **Decided (2026-10-16):** every new folder is a bus, as group tracks are in Ableton Live and Bitwig and folders in REAPER; the Bus switch can still make it plain ([section 8](#8-the-mixer)).
12. ~~Choosing where a track's sound goes~~ **Decided (2026-10-16):** the Out line becomes a menu: Master, the folder buses, and a new bus from the selected tracks. A folder is the bus of the tracks in it, so choosing a bus moves the track into that folder, and Master moves it out of its buses, as REAPER's folders and Bitwig's groups route what is in them: the playlist and the mixer then never disagree about where a sound goes. Sending a track's output straight to another track, as FL Studio's "route to this track only" does, can come later. Built in step 2.9.
13. ~~Sends~~ **Decided (2026-10-16):** a send knob for every return on every strip, off until first touched, as in Ableton Live and Bitwig: one sentence explains it to beginners, and nothing has to be added first. "+ Send" stays, to make a new return. Built in step 2.9.
14. ~~New tracks~~ **Decided (2026-10-16):** "+ Track" makes an empty track, as in Ableton Live, FL Studio, REAPER and Bitwig; the browser, the plug-in picker and **Choose instrument** give it an instrument.
15. ~~Starter project~~ **Decided (2026-10-16):** no reverb return at first. Most DAWs' new projects have none (Ableton Live's default set is the exception), it would use CPU while nothing is sent to it, and "+ Send" makes a return with a reverb in one step.

Raised by the third round of feedback (2026-10-13):

16. ~~Keys over plug-in windows~~ **Decided (2026-10-16):** as proposed, a "Send All Keys to the Plug-in" switch in each plug-in window's menu, as REAPER and FL Studio have, for plug-ins that draw their own text fields without JUCE, where Phazite can't tell that a name is being typed. It is off at first, and remembered for that plug-in in every project, since such a plug-in needs it everywhere ([section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets)). Built in step 2.12.

Raised by the architecture review (2026-10-14):

17. ~~Projects from a newer Phazite~~ **Decided (2026-10-16), set aside (2026-10-18):** Phazite didn't open a project a newer version saved, whose data it might misread or drop when saving; it said that a newer Phazite saved it, kept it in Open Recent, and at startup opened the next recent project instead. (The alternatives were opening it read-only, or after a warning.) With no format versions while Phazite is pre-alpha ([section 18](#18-projects-saving-and-export)), there is nothing to tell a newer project by, so this waits until the owner says compatibility starts.
18. ~~Choosing what loops~~ **Decided (2026-10-16):** turning looping on or off, and switching between looping the song and a range, are treated like the loop's position: saved, but not unsaved changes. So is the metronome's switch, which step 1.7 had counted as the loop's switch was ([section 18](#18-projects-saving-and-export)).

Raised by the golden tests (2026-10-14):

19. ~~What a duplicated time range does to what is after it~~ **Decided (2026-10-16):** each piece of the copy replaces what it lands on, as REAPER's razor edits do by default and as pasting in Ableton Live overwrites, so nothing plays twice. (The alternatives were clearing the whole stretch after the range on its tracks, which also deletes clips on tracks where the range was empty, or laying the copy over what is there, as FL Studio's playlist does.)

Raised by the comb filters (2026-10-18), the audit the owner asked for:

20. ~~More filter types~~ **Decided (2026-10-18, the owner):** all the recommended ones, built in step 2.15 ([section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets)): the synth's notch and 24 dB, the vowel, sample and hold and the phaser. The audit: after step 2.14 the Filter device and the wavetable synth offer low-pass and high-pass at 12 or 24 dB, band-pass, notch, Comb + and Comb -; the synth offers low-pass, band-pass and high-pass at 12 dB and the combs. Common in the synths and filters the research looked at ([B97](#appendix-b-sources)–[B104](#appendix-b-sources), [B90](#appendix-b-sources), [B91](#appendix-b-sources)), and what each would take:
    - **A notch and 24 dB slopes for the synth:** Surge XT, Ableton's Auto Filter and FL Studio's Love Philter have both, and Phazite's other two have them already. Easy: its filter has the notch's output, and the wavetable synth's second section shows how.
    - **Vowel (formant):** Ableton's Auto Filter (Vowel), Bitwig's Filter+ (Vowels), Vital, Pigments and Phase Plant (Formant), Massive X (its parallel SVFs make formants). It makes a sound talk, as the Vowels table does for a wavetable. Moderate: a few band-passes at a vowel's formants, gliding from vowel to vowel; Ableton's sets the vowel with a knob of its own, so it needs one more control, or the cutoff as the vowel.
    - **Sample and hold (resampling):** Surge XT and Ableton's Auto Filter; it holds the sound at a rate the cutoff sets, aliasing on purpose, for lo-fi sounds. Easy.
    - **Phaser or all-pass:** Serum's phase filters, Pigments' Phaser, Surge XT's and FL Studio's all-pass. A few all-pass sections fed back, whose notches move with the cutoff. Easy to moderate.
    - **Morph and DJ:** Ableton's Auto Filter morphs from low-pass through band-pass to high-pass on one knob, and its DJ filter is a low-pass and a high-pass on one bipolar knob. Each needs a control of its own: moderate, and less needed while the types are a list.
    - **Peak:** FL Studio's PK, a boost around the cutoff. Easy, but an EQ's bell does it already.
    - **Analog models:** ladders and other circuits (Surge XT's, Bitwig's, Massive X's, Pigments', Ableton's four circuits): most of them have some, for the character of driven resonance; worth doing only well, so the hardest.
    - Recommended, by value for the effort: the synth's notch and 24 dB first, then vowel and sample and hold, then the phaser; the others later.

Raised by the owner's feedback of 2026-11-01:

21. ~~Keep the routing view, or route only on the strips?~~ **Decided (2026-11-01, the owner): the routing view is removed; routing is set on the strips.** The owner asked for an audit of whether DAWs put routing in line with the mixer or in a view apart ([B115](#appendix-b-sources)). All eleven checked (Ableton Live, Bitwig, FL Studio, Logic Pro, Cubase, Studio One/Studio Pro, Pro Tools, REAPER, Reason, Ardour, Waveform) put everyday routing on the strip: an output chooser, a send for each return, and a sidechain chooser in the device; those that can hide these sections switch them from what the mixer has already (Live's corner, Bitwig's footer). A view apart is a side tool where it exists: a set-up patchbay (Pro Tools' I/O Setup, Cubase's Audio Connections, Ardour's matrix), a power user's extra (REAPER's Routing Matrix, whose own guide calls its wiring diagram bewildering in a large project), a legacy (Logic's Environment, which Apple says needn't be opened) or a plug-in container (Waveform's racks, which users call harder to understand); and Reason, the one DAW built on cables, made routing from menus "sorted by tracks" its default in Reason 14. What DAWs show instead is what the selection links (Logic's Single view, Cubase's "Show Channels that are Connected", Pro Tools' bus interrogation, FL Studio's cables for the selected track), which Phazite's outline does, sidechains included since 2026-11-01. Everything the routing view does is on the strips already (the Out menu, the send knobs, a device's Sidechain button), so it is a second way to do each thing (design principle: one way per task). Its switch went from the tab bar with the view's code, and step 2.9's "done when" changed: a sidechain set up from the picker shows in the mixer's outline.

Raised by the owner on 2026-11-05 ("what users would most appreciate being in the left icon bar ... separating out the search from the browser ... or separate out types of browser content (samples vs plugins etc) into their own browsing icons"):

22. **What the activity bar holds.** **The Project view decided and built (2026-11-06, the owner: "add the Project view to the left wing with it's own icon where you suggested"; [section 4](#4-the-window)).** The rest is open, for the owner. The research ([B135](#appendix-b-sources)–[B138](#appendix-b-sources)) recommends three views, in this order, from what is outside the song, to what is in it, to its history:
    - **Browser**, as now, with **one search** across everything at its top. Every DAW checked keeps library search inside its browser; VS Code's Search is a view of its own only because it searches inside every file and replaces across them, which a DAW's library doesn't need, and requests to search inside a song are rare (one, on Ableton's forum, from 2005). Under the search, **a row of chips with words** (All, Sounds, Instruments, Effects, Presets) filters it, each with its own filters (a sound's type, loop or one-shot, tempo and key, as section 11 plans; a plug-in's maker and format, with Group by Maker and Hide), rather than an icon each: five of the six DAWs checked keep every kind of content in one browser (Logic Pro alone splits them, and no praise for that was found), users ask for instant switching and for instruments apart from effects, which chips give, and one browser lets a search for "808" find the sample, the kit and the synth. Bitwig 5's row of bare icons drew complaints, hence words.
    - **Project** (new): what the song uses, as Bitwig's Project panel shows it: notes kept in the song; its audio files, the missing ones to find and the unused ones (Clean Up Unused Audio would move here, staying one way to do it); and its plug-ins, the missing ones and those kept off after a crash. Users of Ableton Live asked for a list of the plug-ins a set uses from 2013 to 2019, and for notes in the project since 2005.
    - **Source Control**, as now, with a folded **Undo History** section: the steps since the last commit, a click going back to one. Ableton users asked for an undo history from 2015 until Live 12.1 shipped one; FL Studio keeps its own in its browser. Here, going back stays in one place, as VS Code's Timeline lists local saves beside commits.
    - **Not an icon of its own:** commands (the command search), jumping to a track, clip or section by name (later, in the command search, as VS Code's Ctrl+P), favorites and recent items (places in the browser), tags, similar sounds and previews in key (the browser, later), cloud sample stores (a browser place, if ever: they need a commercial deal and an account), templates (the new-project window), lessons (Help and the hint bar), and a list of sections (the sections strip has them).
    - **At the foot:** Settings as now; the research suggests hiding Account until accounts exist, as a placeholder (the owner asked for it on 2026-11-05).
23. ~~The browser after its filters and Swap~~ **Decided (2026-11-07, Claude, as the owner asked: "use your best judgement and do whichever ones you think would be good to do"; [section 11](#11-the-browser)):** Sounds by Type went, the Type filter listing a type's sounds over every place; a drum pad's Load... went, Swap and a drop from the browser or Explorer putting sounds on pads; the Presets menu stayed as it was, Swap its first item, the one button for every device's presets, a plug-in's included.

Raised by the FM synth (2026-11-07):

24. ~~The FM synth's operators, one at a time or side by side~~ **Decided (2026-11-08, Claude, from the owner's request for research into the FM synths people use most and a clearer editor; [section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets), [B156](#appendix-b-sources)):** side by side, as the editors praised show theirs (Operator's shell, the opsix's operator mixer and opsix native's columns, PX7's rows, DX7 V's overview, the reface DX's pages) and the ones complained of don't (Operator's "gazillion modal panels"): the four in columns with their levels, ratios and envelopes always in sight, the rest of their controls a group at a time under tabs, as opsix native's tabs switch its six columns; strips that drag as knobs do, filled where they are amounts, made it fit the default dock.

## 26. Decision log

| Date | Decision |
|---|---|
| 2026-10-10 | Licence AGPL-3.0-or-later; built on an existing open-source DAW engine and JUCE; VST3 first; Windows first; ASIO on Windows |
| 2026-10-12 | The structure in this document is accepted in outline |
| 2026-10-12 | Each track is its own mixer channel; no channel rack |
| 2026-10-12 | The clip launcher waits until after v1 |
| 2026-10-12 | Keys and mouse: whatever users find most intuitive. FL Studio-style keys and a Pro Tools-style Smart Tool, plus a first-launch keymap picker, validated with users |
| 2026-10-12 | AI in v1: chord suggestions and sound generation, useful through project context. No stem separation; the assistant comes later |
| 2026-10-12 | Song outlines and chord progression outlines for newcomers |
| 2026-10-12 | The v1 AI engines are algorithmic (music theory, synthesis and evolution); trained models later |
| 2026-10-12 | First outline styles: pop, rock, electronic, and hip-hop/trap |
| 2026-10-12 | Typography: bundle Inter (SIL OFL 1.1) as separate files, as part of a design language with color, spacing, icon and motion tokens |
| 2026-10-12 | Engine problems: simple workarounds first; a fork of the engine if workarounds get awkward or upstream issues block us |
| 2026-10-12 | Step 0.7 defaults: the computer keyboard plays notes, laid out as in Ableton Live and Logic Pro; live MIDI plays the first track with an instrument until tracks can be selected |
| 2026-10-12 | Icons: Lucide (ISC), step 0.6 |
| 2026-10-12 | Single-key shortcuts and the computer keyboard: Ableton Live's way, Shift and a letter for the shortcut while the keyboard plays notes |
| 2026-10-12 | Step 1.1 defaults: the playlist's time axis is in beats, as in FL Studio and Ableton Live, so tempo changes don't move the grid; the grid adapts to the zoom unless a fixed size is picked; live MIDI follows the selected track (like FL Studio's selected channel); Delete deletes the selection and selects the next track, as in Ableton Live; track heights are saved in the project but aren't undo steps |
| 2026-10-12 | Smart Tool defaults until the milestone 1 usability test settles them: Ctrl+drag copies a clip and Ctrl+Shift+drag makes an alias, in both keymaps (FL Studio's Shift+drag clone isn't mapped yet); Alt places freely; clips show their track's color unless given their own; Duplicate puts copies of clips right after them, as FL Studio's Ctrl+B does |
| 2026-10-12 | Step 1.1 tools: FL Studio's letters in both keymaps, Esc for the Smart Tool; Draw places an alias of the last MIDI clip used (FL Studio's pattern reuse) or a copy of the last audio clip; with FL Studio's keymap right-click deletes clips, otherwise it opens a menu (a keymap setting, `rightClickDeletes`). Phazite's keymap takes Ableton Live's editing keys (Ctrl+E split, Ctrl+I insert time, Ctrl+Shift+Delete remove time, arrows nudge, 0 mutes), FL Studio's keymap its own (Shift+arrows nudge, Alt+M mutes) |
| 2026-10-12 | Step 1.1 folders and files: a new folder is plain (its volume works as a VCA) and becomes a bus when it holds an effect; Ableton Live's keys group (Ctrl+G), ungroup (Ctrl+Shift+G) and collapse (U); collapsed and pinned states are saved but aren't undo steps. Dropped files are copied into the project's `Audio` folder (paths relative to the project); the first goes on the track it is dropped on if that suits it, and the others on new tracks named after them and colored by the words in their names |
| 2026-10-12 | Step 1.2 defaults: a dragged section goes in at the section boundary nearest the pointer, and the sections between make room, as with Studio One's arranger track; Ctrl copies it and Ctrl+Shift copies it as aliases. Dragging a section's right edge inserts or removes time at its end, so the rest of the song moves along; Shift resizes the section alone. Clips are split at a section's edges so that what is inside goes along, automation moves with them, and tempo and time-signature changes stay where they are when a section moves. Each outline section has an energy level (drawn as the curve) and a tip (shown in the hint bar), in the outline's data file. Section colors follow their role, from the track palette |
| 2026-10-12 | Step 1.3 defaults: the piano roll follows the MIDI clips selected in the playlist and edits several at once. As in FL Studio, new notes take the length and velocity of the note clicked last, a drag that follows the click moves the new note, Ctrl+drag selects in a rectangle and Shift+drag copies. Ghost notes show every other track's notes until they can be chosen per track, and a double-right-click on one edits its clip (as in FL Studio: a click adds a note). With notes selected, the note keys come before the playlist's: Ableton Live's in Phazite's keymap (the arrows transpose and move, Shift+Up and Shift+Down by octaves, Shift+Left and Shift+Right change the length), FL Studio's in its keymap. Drum mode is on for tracks whose instrument is a sampler, and the rows' order and hidden rows are kept in the track without undo steps. Quantize's swing makes every other grid line up to a third of a step late (a triplet feel at 100 %), and each transform keeps the settings used last |
| 2026-10-12 | Step 1.4 defaults: devices go before a channel's sends and fader, instruments first, and instruments only on tracks. A folder becomes a bus when it gets a device, its fader starting where the plain folder's volume was, and turns plain again when its last device goes. A new send starts at −6 dB, after the fader. The loudness meter measures the master's devices, before the master fader. Until step 1.6 the device list offers only the engine's own reverb and synth besides scanned plug-ins, because its other devices break the real-time rules. Device windows stay above the main window while Phazite is in front |
| 2026-10-13 | After trying steps 1.1 to 1.4: a track's color runs through its whole row; dragging past an edge scrolls; sections become a band in the time bar and ghosts behind the tracks, with no row or Song menu of their own, and four more outlines (lo-fi, R&B, drum and bass, techno); the piano roll counts bars from the clip's start and opens there, shows a selected track's clip at or nearest the playhead, and on a track without clips the first note starts one; a key is a track's, off until chosen, and the top bar shows none; drum mode has no button; the menu bar is File, Edit, Add, View, Options and Help; "+ Track" sits over the track headers, with a row after the last track that adds one; the Smart Tool's fades are for audio clips, and short clips move from anywhere but their edges |
| 2026-10-13 | Keys: a held key repeats only the commands that take a step; tool keys spring back after a hold with the mouse, as in Bitwig and Ableton Live; in Phazite's keymap the digits 1 to 9 pick the tools and M turns the computer keyboard on and off |
| 2026-10-13 | Second round: playback loops the whole song and the loop grows with it, as in FL Studio's song mode, unless a selection or sections are looped on purpose; a Loop button and L turn looping on or off. A track moves only by the handle in its header. Tracks collapse to a thin row (the arrow in the header, U, or dragging the bottom edge past the smallest height). Track colors are stronger in headers and rows, with black or white names, whichever reads better. With FL Studio's keymap a click on empty space places the last clip used |
| 2026-10-13 | Mixer redesign, after research into the mixers people praise: a strip reads top to bottom as the sound goes, with the instrument in a slot of its own above the effects, and "+ Effect" and "Choose instrument" menus that list only what they add; a track with notes but no instrument asks for one, in the mixer, the inspector and the piano roll. "+ Send" can make a return with an effect in one step, named after it; returns are solo-safe and sit together next to the master; folders get a Bus switch; selecting a channel outlines what it feeds and what feeds it. Mute and solo stay separate buttons, and a solo never changes the mute switches (FL Studio's destructive solo is a long-standing complaint); right-click M solos a track alone, as FL users expect. Ctrl+L loops the selection, or the song with none; with FL Studio's keymap a selected time range loops while it is selected; playback that doesn't loop stops at the song's end |
| 2026-10-13 | Third round, after the owner tried the mixer: Space stops back at the play start, which a click in a time ruler sets and which is the song's start in a project just opened, as in FL Studio, Ableton Live, REAPER and Waveform; Shift+Space pauses (Ctrl+Space in FL Studio's keymap). Play, stop and the computer keyboard work while a plug-in window has the keyboard focus, through a message hook installed before any plug-in's. Each effect has a Mix knob, as FL Studio's mixer slots have, with its limits documented (no latency compensation or ramping yet). The inspector's channel strip fills its height |
| 2026-10-13 | Step 1.5 defaults, after research into the browsers people praise: one tree of places, as in FL Studio, where one click opens a folder; clicking a sound or moving to it with the arrow keys previews it (on by default), Esc stops it, and Shift+Enter or Right plays it again; loops preview at the song's tempo and start on its bar while it plays, and keep their own pitch until Phazite has a song key to match; Enter or a double-click puts the item on the selected track at the play start; a dropped loop follows the song's tempo, as it previewed; F8 is the plug-in picker in both keymaps, Phazite's keymap moves the browser to Ctrl+Alt+B and adds Ctrl+F for its search; favorites by a star; Recent keeps the last 50 items put into the song; "Sounds by Type" lists every library's sounds by tag. Phazite's own sounds are CC0 only: CC0 libraries plus Phazite's own synthesis, each recorded with its source |
| 2026-10-13 | Phazite Sounds become an optional download, kept out of Phazite's repository and installer: the releases of their own public repository (phazite-sounds), pinned in Phazite by address, size and SHA-256. Phazite asks once, in a welcome window that will grow into first-run setup with more pages; the browser offers them until they are installed, and a zip downloaded by hand installs too |
| 2026-10-14 | Code structure, after a review of Phazite's code and of how Ardour, MuseScore, Audacity, Zrythm, ossia score and LMMS keep their layers apart: the layers, folders and seven rules of [section 24](#24-code-structure). The app uses the UI, the UI the services and the model; the model has no window code; the UI runs commands and calls model functions rather than changing the project or starting undo steps, and in time sees only Phazite's own types. `tools/check-architecture.sh` checks the rules in CI and before every commit, with limits that can only go down. The roadmap's architecture track (phases A0 to A5) does the work |
| 2026-10-14 | An engine of Phazite's own is a hedge, not a goal: Phazite stays on the engine it is built on and replaces a part only where it gains something, one capability at a time, each after a decision dated in this log, behind an interface and with the golden tests as the safety net. Phazite's own devices are engine-neutral from step 1.6 either way. Engine problems are still fixed with workarounds first and a fork if those get awkward (2026-10-12) |
| 2026-10-14 | Projects: the engine's project document stays the project store. Phazite adds a schema version, keeps the list of what it saves and the migrations of older projects in one place, and saves its own values rather than the engine's enum numbers; the two saved that way so far (the grid's fixed size and the scale of a key) are converted when an older project opens |
| 2026-10-14 | Notes get stable IDs saved in the project, so that selections, undo and scripts can refer to a note reliably (architecture phase A3) |
| 2026-10-14 | View state (track heights, collapsed and pinned tracks, drum mode and drum rows, grid settings) stays saved in the project, but changing it no longer counts as an unsaved change, as with the playhead's position |
| 2026-10-14 | Architecture phases A0 and A1 (golden tests and the architecture check, then untangling the model without changing what the app does) come now, before step 1.6, so that the new devices start on the new structure |
| 2026-10-14 | Designer workflow: the design tokens move into a file in the DTCG format, which a designer edits or exports from Figma, Penpot or Tokens Studio, and a gallery app shows every control and view in all three themes (architecture phase A4). A declarative layout tool (JIVE or foleys_gui_magic) only if dialogs become a bottleneck |
| 2026-10-14 | Go to End goes to the song's end, where the loop ends: the end of the bar its last clip ends in, as FL Studio ends a song for export. Sections don't count, even in a song without clips |
| 2026-10-14 | A note edit makes its clip longer only into the empty space after it, never over the next clip on its track, as Bitwig extends clips and as Studio One can keep events from overlapping; a note that doesn't fit stays in the clip without playing. A looped clip doesn't grow |
| 2026-10-14 | Duplicate works on a selected time range, as in REAPER and Ableton Live and as FL Studio's Ctrl+B repeats its time selection: what lies in it on its tracks goes again right after it, each piece replacing what it lands on (open question 19), and the range moves to the copy so that Duplicate again repeats it, and back on Undo, as REAPER's undo puts razor edit areas back; the clips in the range aren't cut, and Duplicate as Alias stays for clips and sections |
| 2026-10-15 | Step 1.6 begins while the owner is away, with these as proposals until the owner decides: Phazite's devices have plain names (EQ, Compressor, Limiter, Reverb, Synth, Drum Sampler); each of Phazite's effects has a Mix of its own, delayed to match the effect and ramped; the EQ has eight switchable bands with the defaults in [section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets), its editor's dots move a band's frequency and gain as one undo step, and its slot's key controls are the shelves' gains; projects that hold Phazite's devices are format version 2, which an older Phazite won't open |
| 2026-10-15 | Proposals for the compressor, until the owner decides: the defaults in [section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets), stereo-linked peak detection with attack and release as time constants, a low cut on what it listens to, and a sidechain that can listen to any track that wouldn't make a loop, muted or not, with "Off" meaning its own sound |
| 2026-10-15 | Proposals for the limiter, until the owner decides: the defaults in [section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets), a fixed 2 ms look-ahead (no knob), true-peak detection always on, and a mix like every other effect's, though parallel limiting lets peaks through |
| 2026-10-15 | Proposals for the reverb, until the owner decides: the controls and defaults in [section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets), with the decay measured on the bass and a 25 % mix on an insert, and it replaces the engine's reverb in the device list (projects that hold that one still open and play) |
| 2026-10-15 | Proposals for the synth, until the owner decides: the controls in [section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets) with their defaults (two detuned saws through a 3 kHz low-pass that its envelope opens two octaves, a 2 ms attack, 80 % sustain and a 200 ms release, at -6 dB), 16 voices, the mod wheel on the LFO's depth, and no unison, chorus or wavetables in this first version |
| 2026-10-15 | Proposals for the drum sampler, until the owner decides: 16 pads in General MIDI's layout from note 36, one-shots only (no gated or looping pads yet), a synthesized default kit, the hats in one choke group, linear velocity, files copied into the project's Audio folder, sounds up to 30 s, and a length knob whose longest setting plays the whole sound |
| 2026-10-15 | Step 1.7 begins while the owner is away, with these as proposals until the owner decides. The metronome clicks on every beat, louder on each bar's first; the project keeps whether it is on and counts switching it as a change to save, as it does the loop's switch, but it isn't an undo step. The count-in is a bar of clicks before recording starts from a stop, heard with the metronome off; it is the app's setting for every project, off at first. Keys: O in Phazite's keymap (Ableton Live's), and Ctrl+M and Ctrl+P (FL Studio's metronome and precount) in FL Studio's. A right-click on the metronome button offers the count-in |
| 2026-10-15 | Proposals for the tempo and time signature, until the owner decides: the top bar's tempo is a field to drag up or down (a beat per minute every 2 pixels), scroll, step with the arrow keys, or click and type (to a hundredth), as in Ableton Live; each drag, typed tempo, notch or key press is one undo step, "Change Tempo", and clips keep their beats. A click on the time signature offers 2/4, 3/4, 4/4, 5/4, 6/8, 7/8, 9/8 and 12/8. A song has one tempo and one time signature, from its start, until a tempo lane comes |
| 2026-10-15 | Proposals for MIDI recording, until the owner decides: Record (R, in both keymaps: FL Studio's key, in its manual; Logic Pro's in Apple's guide for the iPad and in third-party lists, though Apple's guides for the Mac that were found don't confirm it) records the computer keyboard and MIDI keyboards onto the track live MIDI plays (the selected track with an instrument), with nothing to arm, from where playback starts after the count-in, or from where the song is if it plays already. Overdub is on at first: the notes join the clips already there; off, they take those clips' place. Quantize Recordings (off at first) moves notes to the nearest sixteenth as they go in. Both are the app's settings. The song's own loop doesn't loop a recording, which runs on past the song's end, and the loop is back around the song afterwards; a range looped on purpose loops the recording, if it is at least 2 seconds long (the engine's limit then). Each take is one undo step, "Record", and stopping goes back to where recording started. Live MIDI's track stays armed, invisibly, so that Record starts at once |

Capture takes the last 30 seconds the engine keeps of what was played, recording or not. While the song plays, the notes stay where they were played; while it is stopped, they go at the bar the play start is in, or after the track's clips, the first note on the bar's first beat. In a song without clips, Capture first sets the tempo from the notes: the whole BPM between 80 and 160 at which they fall best on sixteenths, with more of them on beats, nearer 120 when tempos fit as well, and none when no tempo fits. The clip runs whole bars, its notes quantized when recordings are. It is one undo step, "Capture", the new tempo with it |

Built by Claude for the owner to review: feedback on every operator, toward a saw one way and a square the other, as the reface DX's (operator 4's alone before), Timbre turning it down with the modulators; Spread, each note twice, detuned apart, left and right, as Operator's (seven of the eight DAWs' FM synths have spread or unison); a DC blocker; the editor rebuilt: the algorithm played drawn large, numbered, carriers and modulators in two colours, all eight in a pop-up with what each is good for, the harmonics the operators make under it, and the four operators side by side, each saying what it does ("Heard", "→ 1"), with a group of their controls at a time under tabs ([question 24](#25-open-questions) answered); Chip Square, and Spread on the pads. Left out, with reasons: six operators and DX7 patches, a matrix and algorithms of the user's own, DX-style envelopes and breakpoints, an LFO amount for each operator, effects and a randomizer. |
| 2026-11-07 | The owner saw clips' tags repeat their tracks' names but for a tempo ("the track header says "Shaker" but the audio clips in the track say "Shaker 85bmp""; [section 6](#6-the-playlist)): a clip's name like its track's shows no tag either, the same words but for their capitals, what is between them, and a tempo or a key that only one of the two names gives, read as the browser reads them; a word more, another tempo or another key still shows the tag. |
| 2026-11-08 | The owner asked for a pass on every instrument's presets against the industry's ("I don't want our presets (or our in-house plugins for that matter) to be missing anything, or for people to turn away from our presets because they don't sound correct for what they expect it to sound like"), and for many classic synths as presets, the TR-808 named so if that is allowed ([section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets), [section 22](#22-privacy-licences-and-trademarks); [presets.md](presets.md)). Decided by Claude for the owner to review: the classics named by their sound ("808" and "909" as the words for sounds they have become; never "TR-808", "TR-909", "TB-303" or "Roland"); what 27 classic sounds want added to the synth (Sync 2, Env 2, the sub's shapes and octaves, Drift, Filter Drive and Velocity, the LFO's Pulse Width and Fade In, a pitch envelope and Legato glides), bit for bit as before at their defaults; the 808, 909, Trap, Rhythm Box and Percussion kits, each voice after its circuit or what producers expect, in place of Deep 808 and Techno; 49 synth, 29 wavetable, 36 FM and 8 sampler presets, leveled alike, which the tests check. |
| 2026-11-08 | Then the owner asked whether any of what the pass left should be built now. Claude recommended the chorus, the effect every DAW ships, which ten of the 27 classic sounds want and the Flanger only came near, and not the rest: track presets would be a second way to the same sounds, and an arpeggiator, SFZ multisamples and a drum synth are each a step of their own. Built ([section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets)): Classic and Ensemble, the Juno-60's modes and the string machines' ensembles among its presets, the Flanger's chorus presets moved to it. |
| 2026-11-08 | The owner found the example songs weak musically and asked for songs others made and shared openly, their notes re-played on Phazite's instruments, in any style. Claude searched the LMMS Sharing Platform, the Best of LMMS albums' judged tracks and other open sources; the owner listened and chose. Exodus by XCALIBUR (CC BY) replaces Trap Beat, its notes in a MIDI file, credited in its description, its project's notes and the About box; songs from others may be CC0 or CC BY only, as the songs people make from an example carry its license ([section 13](#13-getting-started-and-song-outlines)). The search for another goes on; songs whose licenses don't allow this (Treadbygrooves by MK Rexx, CC BY-NC-SA; Humid Downpours by Etorna, all rights reserved) need their authors' permission. |
| 2026-11-08 | The owner asked how much work smooth tempo changes would be, then what the popular DAWs do around them, then for "all of the reccomended system for the tempo work" ([section 4](#4-the-window); roadmap step 4.8): as most DAWs do, a tempo lane at the top of the playlist while the tempo changes, its points holding and jumping or ramping, ramps bent as automation's lines; the top bar's tempo the one at the playhead, with no override mode; samples that follow the tempo stretched along its changes; tempo maps from MIDI files and both ways in DAWprojects. Tempo detection and tap tempo wait until after v1. Exodus took the original's tempo back, a spin-up from 50 BPM and breakdowns up to 260. The owner also found its bass "less complex and flat feeling" and its drums "not very similar" to the original's: its bass moved to the FM synth as the original's phase modulation, its sub took the original's three detuned saws, filter envelope, chorus and EQ, its drums the factory sounds nearest the original's samples as measured, and its master the original's warm, dark EQ ([section 13](#13-getting-started-and-song-outlines)). Rubber Band's key frames drifted a few milliseconds from a time map along a steep ramp; the owner then asked for the fix the same day: R3 in real-time mode, its stretch set at each hop to land on the map. |
| 2026-11-08 | The owner shared Exodus's stems and asked for it to sound as close to the original as Phazite can, with the presets that takes. Measured against them, Exodus now follows the recording, which differs from the project XCALIBUR shared: its first theme is 24 bars, without the project's bars 24 to 32; everything plays a fourth higher, in F major (the bass stem's lowest series is on B flat and G where the project's sub plays F and D, and the arpeggio's on B flat major sevenths where the project's are on F; the project's pitch is 0 and nothing moves it, so the recording was transposed whole); and its first two themes are at 114 BPM. Its intro follows the recording, fitted to its spectrum every 2 seconds: a kick on every beat (the drum stem's hits fall on each beat of the ramp), and the master's high-pass and low-pass at about a tenth to a twentieth of the project's values, high-pass opening from 250 Hz by bar 6 and sweeping to 1.5 kHz into the theme, low-pass opening from 400 Hz, with the sub brighter until the theme. Its drums play a new Synthwave Kit, synthesized after the original's samples as measured; its arpeggio's second layer plays a new Harmonic Pluck, the original pad's envelope; and everything but the drums, the plate included, pumps through a new Sidechain Pump, about 30 dB down at the beat and back in 200 ms, as the original's peak controller pumps it. Nothing of the original's audio is in the repository ([section 13](#13-getting-started-and-song-outlines); `docs/presets.md`). The same day, as the owner asked, a sample stretched along tempo changes renders through Rubber Band's R3 in real-time mode, its stretch set at each hop to land on the time map, which ended that drift; and an idle project no longer asks to save when its plug-ins only said their settings changed (JUCE's VST3 host reports every restartComponent() as a program change). |
| 2026-11-08 | The owner asked whether Phazite had a bounce in place or consolidate, for Ableton Live's recording, warping and quantizing of audio, for it to be clear where a recording goes and what it records, and for recordings to show in the playlist as they come in, after an audit of what Live gets right ([recording-audit.md](recording-audit.md); [B157](#appendix-b-sources)). Built by Claude for the owner to review ([section 17](#17-recording-and-capture), [section 7](#the-audio-clip-editor), [section 6](#6-the-playlist); roadmap step 4.9): an arm switch on every track that records, lit on the instrument track live MIDI plays as Live's auto-arm; what each records under its name; Record's tooltip naming what it records onto, and with nothing to record onto its menu offering Record an Input on a New Track; a clip growing on each track as it records, an input's waveform and the notes drawn as they come in; recordings following the tempo; Consolidate (Ctrl+J), MIDI joined at once and audio rendered before the track's devices; warp markers in the audio clip editor, the hits in its audio found in the background; and Quantize Audio. Kept: Bounce in Place, as Logic Pro and Cubase name it (Live 12.2's Bounce to New Track for a whole track); arming per track, not exclusive. Left for later: take lanes and comping, an input meter, warp modes, tempo-detecting warp commands. |
| 2026-11-08 | The owner asked how best to bring piano and guitar into Phazite, how other DAWs make such sounds, whether one instrument that plays many is their way, and what high quality would take, and wanted other real instruments too; then for it in the roadmap and here, with an issue ([section 9](#9-devices-containers-and-presets); [B158](#appendix-b-sources); roadmap step 4.10). Planned by Claude for the owner to review: the **Multisampler**, a device of its own beside the Sampler, as every DAW plays its real instruments on one engine, playing instruments of many recordings from SFZ files through Phazite's own reader (sfizz is archived, and liquidsfz's MPL 2.0 isn't among Phazite's licences), streamed from disk, with a piano's pedal, release noises and resonance; its instruments its presets, each a pack of Phazite Sounds, CC0 only, downloaded when first picked (a grand and an upright piano, the VSCO 2 orchestra, an electric bass and a double bass), edited and measured as the presets are. Step 4.10 sits in v1, as a sampled piano is as common among DAWs as an FM synth; articulations in the piano roll, guitars (no well-sampled acoustic guitar is CC0 yet) and a synthesized organ, electric piano and plucked strings wait until after v1. |

## Appendix A. Research summary

In October 2026, research agents reviewed about 20 DAWs.
- **Sources of opinion:** reviews (Sound On Sound, MusicTech, Attack, Tape Op, MusicRadar), KVR and vendor forums.
- **Limits:** Reddit and Gearspace couldn't be fetched, so treat opinions as a sample, not a census.

| DAW | Loved | Borrowed for Phazite | Avoided |
|---|---|---|---|
| FL Studio 2026 | The piano roll ("like a paint program"); fast pattern painting; lifetime free updates | The piano-roll mouse model and transforms, single-key tools, playlist colors and folders, F-keys | The channel rack / pattern / playlist / mixer split; invisible routing; floating windows |
| Ableton Live 12 | Session View for sketching; MIDI Tools; the tag browser; Capture MIDI; Bounce in Place; Info View | Capture, transforms with preview, hover help, a per-track CPU meter | Shortcuts that can't be remapped; clips that silently override the arrangement |
| Bitwig Studio 6 | Modulators on anything; clip automation and aliases; tools on number keys; a footer showing mouse actions; plug-in sandboxing | Drag-to-modulate, aliases, the hint bar, sandboxing | Depth before basics; odd default shortcuts; a messy browser |
| Fender Studio Pro 8 (Studio One) | Drag-and-drop everything; Arranger track; chord track; Channel Overview | Drop to create tracks, track presets, sections, progression presets | An assistant that can only talk; setup before the keyboard plays |
| Reason 14 | Players (chord/arp generators); the Combinator; auto-cabling | Containers with macros (its routing view went on 2026-11-01) | A sequencer that lags behind the instruments |
| Logic Pro 12 | Live Loops tied to tracks; track stacks (folder or bus); Smart Controls; Quick Help; Flashback Capture | Folders that become buses, key controls on channel strips, hover help | The old beginner/pro switch (removed in Logic 12); CPU-heavy presets |
| Cubase 15 | Modulators; last-touched automation; the docked lower zone; Chord Assistant | Last-touched automation, the editor dock, chord suggestions | "Not a beginner DAW"; inconsistent UI; half-finished features |
| GarageBand | Hover help on by default; templates that set key and tempo first | First-run help, the new-project dialog | Hidden mixer and buses |
| Pro Tools 2026 | Editing speed; the Smart Tool; single-key command mode; Track Pin | The Smart Tool, track pinning | Tiered track limits; iLok; "fiddly" MIDI |
| REAPER 7 | Price and size; "everything is just a track"; a searchable action list; scripting; crash protection | The command palette, macros, range edits across tracks | "Nothing there when you first open it" |
| Ardour 9 | Open source, pay what you want; Region FX; note brushing | Clip effects, paint tool | "If in doubt, right-click somewhere"; track-snapped scrolling |
| Waveform 14 | One-screen layout; one track type; racks and drag-on modifiers | Its one track type and its modulators | An "eccentric" first screen; sandboxing off by default |
| Digital Performer, Sonar, LUNA, Renoise, MPC, BandLab | Several arrangements per project; workspace presets; automatic track naming and coloring; keyboard-first editing; perform then commit; sound in seconds | Keymap import, auto-naming, Capture | Private vocabulary; account and activation hurdles |

**Mixers (researched 2026-10-13, for the [mixer redesign](#8-the-mixer)):** Logic Pro, Pro Tools, Live, Bitwig, Studio One, Cubase, FL Studio, REAPER, Reason, Ardour, Mixbus and Waveform, from their manuals, reviews and forums.
- **The instrument has a place of its own** above the effects in the DAWs that don't trip people up (Logic, Reason's Track Panel, Live's enforced chain order). Where instruments share the effect slots (Pro Tools, REAPER, Waveform), order mistakes cause silence.
- **Sends:** a send for every return on every track (Live, Bitwig, Mixbus) is explained to beginners in one sentence, and Studio One's "drop an effect on the sends to make a return" is the most praised setup. Logic's hidden auxes and FL Studio's unnamed mixer inserts confuse newcomers. The words aux, bus, send and return mean different things from one DAW to the next.
- **Folders that are buses** are where the field converges (Live, Pro Tools routing folders, Cubase 15, Logic's summing stacks).
- **Routing diagrams** get mixed reviews; showing routing on the selected strip (Logic, REAPER's route button) works without clutter.
- **Solo:** every other DAW keeps solo apart from the mute switches; FL Studio's solo changes them, a complaint on its forum since 2007.

**Panels around the timeline (researched 2026-10-17, for [section 4](#4-the-window)):** what seven DAWs put at the bottom of the window and at its side, from their manuals and Sound On Sound ([B64](#appendix-b-sources) to [B75](#appendix-b-sources)).

| DAW | Bottom of the window | The selected track's devices in line | Side panel |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ableton Live 12 | Clip View or Device View, or both stacked: the selection only | The Device View; a plug-in shows the parameters chosen with Configure, and a button opens its window | None; the mixer is in Session View, and on each track's row in Arrangement View |
| Bitwig Studio 6 | One at a time: Detail Editor, Automation Editor, Device panel or Mixer; the Mix view has the mixer in the centre and the Device panel below | The Device panel; a plug-in shows a list of its parameters, and a button opens its window | Inspector (left): properties of the selection |
| Fender Studio Pro 8 | One at a time: Editor, Console or Channel Overview (new in 8) | The Channel Overview: the channel's controls, then compact panels (Studio Pro's own effects with their graphs and knobs, a plug-in's first eight parameters or those chosen) | Inspector (left) |
| Logic Pro | Editors, Mixer or Smart Controls | Smart Controls: a few knobs for the track's sound; plug-ins open windows | Inspector (left): region and track settings, and two channel strips (the track's and where it goes) |
| Cubase 15 | Lower Zone tabs: MixConsole, Editor, Sampler Control, Chord Pads, Modulators | None; plug-ins open windows | Inspector (left): sections, a copy of the channel's fader among them |
| Reason 14 | The Track Panel (new in 2026): the selected track's devices, signal chain, sends and pan, in the sequencer | Its rack devices | None |
| FL Studio 2025 | No dock: floating windows (F7 piano roll, F9 mixer) | None: the mixer's side panel lists the selected track's effect slots, and each opens a window | The mixer's side panel |

**Audio clip editors (researched 2026-10-17, for [the audio clip editor](#the-audio-clip-editor)):** what five DAWs show of an audio clip, from their manuals ([B76](#appendix-b-sources) to [B81](#appendix-b-sources)). Logic Pro's is from its guide for the iPad; the cells marked "not checked" weren't in the pages read.

| DAW | Where | Gain | Pitch | Reverse | Tempo |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ableton Live 12 | The Clip View, under the session or arrangement: its sample with start and end markers | A slider, in dB | Semitones, and cents in a second field | Makes a reversed copy of the sample | Warp, from the sample's tempo |
| Logic Pro | The region inspector | In dB | Transpose (semitones) and Fine Tune (cents) | Yes, though not while Flex & Follow is on | Follow Tempo (Apple Loops), or Flex & Follow |
| Fender Studio Pro 8 | The inspector, and the Edit view (F2), which opens the Audio Editor for audio events | Not checked | Not checked | Not checked | The event's File Tempo; each track Timestretches, Follows or Doesn't Follow |
| Bitwig Studio | The inspector, and the Detail Editor | A gain expression, in dB | A pitch expression, in semitones | Not in the inspector | A stretch mode, and the event's original tempo |
| FL Studio | The sampler's channel settings | Not checked | Pitch, keeping the length | Reverse | Time (to the project's tempo), and a stretch mode |

**Code structure (researched 2026-10-13, for [section 24](#24-code-structure)):** Ardour, MuseScore and its shared framework, Audacity 3 and 4, LMMS, Zrythm 2, ossia score and the JUCE hosts Element and Helio, from their developer documentation and source.
- **Boundaries hold only where a build rule or a check enforces them.** Ardour's core library builds without its UI toolkit ([B43](#appendix-b-sources)), and MuseScore makes each module a CMake project of its own ([B44](#appendix-b-sources)). LMMS, with folders only, let its core grow UI code: its core track class declares how to make its own view ([B52](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **In the newer designs, the UI never changes the project directly.** It sends a command or calls an operation that owns the undo step: MuseScore's view models send commands to controllers ([B45](#appendix-b-sources)), Zrythm writes down "zero direct model mutation" ([B50](#appendix-b-sources)), and ossia's presenters submit commands, a drag sending updates and then one commit or rollback ([B51](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **Commands are becoming the public API.** MuseScore's 2026 decision records turn them into typed calls with parameters and results, and say which ones shortcuts, scripts or an assistant may use ([B46](#appendix-b-sources), [B47](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **Objects cross boundaries as stable IDs, never pointers:** Ardour's `PBD::ID`, ossia's paths and Zrythm's UUIDs, each item looked up by its ID in the one store of project data.
- **Engines were wrapped only when something was really being replaced, one piece at a time.** Audacity 4 rebuilt its UI over its old engine through adapters ([B48](#appendix-b-sources)) and shipped 4.0 without MIDI tracks or the mixer ([B49](#appendix-b-sources)). Replacing one capability behind an interface ("branch by abstraction", [B54](#appendix-b-sources)) avoids a rewrite, whose cost "Why you shouldn't write a DAW" describes ([B55](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **Designers work through named theme values, and the busiest views stay painted in code.** Design tokens have a shared format, DTCG 2025.10 ([B56](#appendix-b-sources)); the Melatonin Inspector shows a running JUCE app's sizes, colors and fonts ([B57](#appendix-b-sources)).
- **Fast-changing data stays out of the project:** meters and the playhead are read from small sources, not stored in the project or sent as change messages.

**Where the field is converging:**
- Each track is a row, a chain and a channel.
- Two automation layers: lanes under each track, and modulators. Where a DAW also has clip-like automation, it lives inside an instrument's clips or a parameter's own lane (research of 2026-10-17, [section 10](#10-automation-and-modulation)).
- Linked clips with "make unique".
- Capture instead of record.
- One window with layouts.
- Every action is a command.
- No beginner/pro switch.
- Help in place.
- Sandboxing on by default.
- AI that assists inside the work.
- Project portability through DAWproject.

The stock instruments of eight DAWs, from their manuals and edition pages: Ableton Live 12: <https://www.ableton.com/en/live/compare-editions/>, <https://www.ableton.com/en/live-manual/12/live-instrument-reference/>; FL Studio 2026: <https://www.image-line.com/fl-studio/compare-editions>, <https://www.image-line.com/fl-studio-learning-content/fl-studio-online-manual/html/generator_plugins.htm>; Logic Pro 13: <https://support.apple.com/guide/logicpro/lgsi6c2728cc/mac>; Bitwig Studio 6.1: <https://www.bitwig.com/userguide/latest/device_descriptions/>; Cubase 16: <https://www.steinberg.net/cubase/compare-editions/>; Fender Studio Pro 8: <https://fenderstudiopromanual.fender.com/en/Content/Built-In_Instruments_Topics/Chapter-Built_in_Virtual_Instruments.htm>; Reason 14: <https://www.reasonstudios.com/shop/product/reason>; REAPER 7.85: <https://www.reaper.fm/about.php> (a general sampler in all eight; a drum synth in seven; granular, FM, bass and a sampled piano in six). What they use: Image-Line's forum poll of the native generators most used (2024, 2,125 votes: FLEX 230, Sytrus 227, Harmor 192, Channel Sampler 163; the samplers together 370): <https://forum.image-line.com/viewtopic.php?p=1976494>; Ableton's forum poll of the instruments left unused (2012, 259 votes: Electric, Tension and Collision 55 %, Simpler 4 %, Operator 6 %): <https://forum.ableton.com/viewtopic.php?p=1381136>; KVR members' favourite instruments (Kontakt 7th, Dexed 9th, FM8 16th): <https://www.kvraudio.com/plugins/instruments/most-popular>; Splice's downloads of 2024 (hip-hop 48.7 million, trap 35.0 million, the top two genres): <https://www.musicbusinessworldwide.com/splice-which-hit-nearly-350m-downloads-in-2024-reveals-the-fastest-growing-genres-on-its-platform>; Splice INSTRUMENT, a free player of sampled presets, the new home of Spitfire's LABS (2025): <https://splice.com/blog/introducing-splice-instrument/>; MidiCaps, piano, drums and guitars predominant in 168,407 MIDI files: <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2406.02255>
- **B153** Samplers' controls and what their users say, the research of 2026-11-07: Ableton Live 12's Simpler and Sampler: <https://www.ableton.com/en/manual/live-instrument-reference/>, <https://www.ableton.com/en/live-manual/12/converting-audio-to-midi/>, Sound On Sound's "Keep It Simple": <https://www.soundonsound.com/techniques/keep-it-simple>, and its users' complaints of clicks and slicing: <https://forum.ableton.com/viewtopic.php?p=1803365>, <https://forum.ableton.com/viewtopic.php?p=1802851>, <https://forum.ableton.com/viewtopic.php?p=1824177>; Logic Pro's Quick Sampler, its modes, waveform display and root key settings: <https://support.apple.com/guide/logicpro/choose-a-mode-lgcpabc8c044/mac>, <https://support.apple.com/guide/logicpro/slice-mode-lgcp18515788/mac>, <https://support.apple.com/guide/logicpro/quick-sampler-waveform-display-lgcp4492eed9/mac>, <https://support.apple.com/guide/logicpro/sampler-settings-lgcpe384fe15/mac>; FL Studio's Sampler, Slicex and Fruity Slicer 2: <https://www.image-line.com/fl-studio-learning/fl-studio-online-manual/html/chansettings_sampler.htm>, <https://www.image-line.com/fl-studio-learning/fl-studio-online-manual/html/plugins/Slicex.htm>, <https://www.image-line.com/fl-studio-learning/fl-studio-online-manual/html/plugins/Fruity%20Slicer%202.htm>, and its users' requests: <https://forum.image-line.com/viewtopic.php?p=2052268>, <https://forum.image-line.com/viewtopic.php?p=2070813>; Bitwig Studio 6.1's Sampler: <https://downloads.bitwig.com/6.1/Release-Notes-6.1.html>, <https://www.soundonsound.com/news/bitwig-studio-61-now-available>; Cubase's Sampler Track: <https://www.soundonsound.com/techniques/cubase-13-new-sampler-track-features>, and its glide that restarts the sound: <https://forums.steinberg.net/t/glide-knob-doesnt-work-as-it-shoud-be-sampler-track/133980>
- **B154** FM synths' controls and what their users say, the research of 2026-11-07: Ableton's Operator: <https://www.ableton.com/en/manual/live-instrument-reference/>, <https://roberthenke.com/technology/operator.html>, and its users' "way too much unnecessary mousing and clicking": <https://forum.ableton.com/viewtopic.php?p=1548545>; Yamaha's DX7 manual: <https://homepages.abdn.ac.uk/d.j.benson/pages/dx7/manuals/dx7-man.pdf>, its chips taken apart: <https://www.righto.com/2021/12/yamaha-dx7-chip-reverse-engineering.html>, Google's Music Synthesizer for Android: <https://github.com/google/music-synthesizer-for-android>, and Dexed: <https://asb2m10.github.io/dexed/>; Native Instruments' FM8 manual: <https://storage.googleapis.com/pdf-guides/FM8-book_EN_ebook.pdf>; Elektron's Digitone and Sound On Sound's and MusicRadar's reviews: <https://www.soundonsound.com/reviews/elektron-digitone>, <https://www.musicradar.com/reviews/elektron-digitone>; Korg's opsix manual and Sound On Sound's review: <https://www.korg.com/us/support/download/manual/0/874/5046/>, <https://www.soundonsound.com/reviews/korg-opsix>; Image-Line's Sytrus and Sound On Sound's review: <https://www.image-line.com/fl-studio-learning/fl-studio-online-manual/html/plugins/Sytrus.htm>, <https://www.soundonsound.com/reviews/image-line-sytrus>; Chowning's paper (1973): <https://yamahasynth.com/wp-content/uploads/images/fm_synthesispaper-2.pdf>; the DX7's factory voices: <https://yamahablackboxes.com/patches/dx7/factory/rom1a.syx>; Yamaha's 4-operator algorithms: <https://csound.com/icsc2019/proceedings/3.pdf>; feedback's averaging against hunting: <https://patents.google.com/patent/US5619002>
- **B155** Sampled instruments under CC0 or in the public domain, in SFZ, for the sampler's multisamples later: the SFZ Instruments catalogue's pianos (Scarypiano CC0, Splendid Grand Piano public domain, VCSL Keys CC0; Salamander Grand Piano CC-BY): <https://sfzinstruments.github.io/pianos>; Versilian's VCSL Keys (three grands, two uprights, five harpsichords, CC0): <https://versilian-studios.com/vcsl-keys/>; and VSCO 2 Community Edition (a chamber orchestra, CC0): <https://versilian-studios.com/vsco-community/>
- **B156** FM synths, the most used and best rated, what they all have and how their editors are laid out, the research of 2026-11-08. How much they are used and liked: KVR's product pages (Dexed the most favourited of the FM synths, 478; FM8 4.39 of 5 from 33 reviews, the best rated): <https://www.kvraudio.com/product/dexed-by-digital-suburban>, <https://www.kvraudio.com/product/fm8-by-native-instruments>; Reverb's best-selling synths of 2025 (the Digitone second): <https://musictech.com/news/gear/these-are-the-best-selling-synths-drum-machines-and-controllers-at-reverb-in-2025>; Thomann's ratings (Digitone II 4.9 of 5 from 69, reface DX 4.6 of 5 from 93): <https://www.thomannmusic.com/elektron_digitone_ii_reviews.htm>, <https://www.thomannmusic.com/yamaha_reface_dx.htm>; MusicTech's best free synths (Dexed for FM, 2026): <https://musictech.com/guides/buyers-guide/best-free-synth-plugins/>; what users name and want (a resizable, readable editor, custom algorithms, DX7 patches, low CPU): <https://www.elektronauts.com/t/fm-synth-vsts/198892>, <https://www.kvraudio.com/forum/viewtopic.php?p=8836410>, <https://forum.ableton.com/viewtopic.php?p=1811892>. How they are laid out: Ableton Live 12's Operator (a shell of every oscillator's Coarse, Fine, Fixed and Level beside one display, its 11 algorithms on its global page, feedback on any unmodulated oscillator, Spread): <https://www.ableton.com/en/live-manual/12/live-instrument-reference/>, and "a tiny UI with a gazillion modal panels": <https://forum.ableton.com/viewtopic.php?p=1829123>; Reason's PX7 (every operator a row, one algorithm picture down their side; "no paging or menus"): <https://www.manualslib.com/manual/596161/Propellerhead-Px7.html>, <https://www.kvraudio.com/forum/viewtopic.php?p=5169693>; Bitwig's Phase-4 (four coloured strips, a scope of each and of the output): <https://www.bitwig.com/phase-4/>, <https://www.musicradar.com/reviews/bitwig-studio-23>; Korg's opsix (the operator mixer, carriers red and modulators blue) and opsix native (six columns that tabs switch all at once, the algorithm drawn with arrows and all 40 in a pop-up, a scope of each operator): <https://www.manualslib.com/manual/2053292/Korg-Opsix.html?page=23>, <https://www.soundonsound.com/reviews/korg-opsix>, <https://musictech.com/reviews/plug-ins/korg-opsix-native-review-a-hardware-fm-monster-repackaged-as-a-plug-in/>; Yamaha's reface DX (its FREQ, LEVEL and FB pages each one value of all four operators under the algorithm, feedback on each, toward a saw one way and a square the other): <https://usa.yamaha.com/files/download/other_assets/7/438827/reface_en_rm_a0.pdf>, <https://www.synthtopia.com/content/2015/09/22/yamaha-reface-dx-synthesizer-review-user-friendly-fm-programming-in-a-hardware-keyboard/>; Elektron's Digitone (the algorithm drawn, its operators in groups, ratios explained as familiar waves): <https://elektron.se/wp-content/uploads/2024/09/Digitone_Keys_User_Manual_ENG_OS1.41_231108.pdf>, <https://www.soundonsound.com/reviews/elektron-digitone>, <https://www.soundonsound.com/reviews/elektron-digitone-ii>; Arturia's DX7 V (each operator lit by its output, every ratio and level in a column, an output scope): <https://www.arturia.com/products/software-instruments/dx7-v/overview>, <https://musictech.com/reviews/arturia-v-collection-6-review/>; Plogue's chipsynth OPS7 (the algorithm as the editor) and Dexed: <https://www.plogue.com/products/chipsynth-ops7.html>, <https://s3.amazonaws.com/chipsynth/OPS7_manual.pdf>, <https://github.com/asb2m10/dexed>; Native Instruments' FM8 (the matrix, the Easy page, a spectrum): <https://storage.googleapis.com/pdf-guides/FM8-book_EN_ebook.pdf>, <https://www.musicradar.com/reviews/tech/native-instruments-fm8-22776>; Image-Line's Sytrus and Toxic Biohazard: <https://www.image-line.com/fl-studio-learning/fl-studio-online-manual/html/plugins/Sytrus.htm>, <https://www.soundonsound.com/reviews/image-line-sytrus>, <https://www.musicradar.com/reviews/tech/image-line-toxic-biohazard-178217>; Logic Pro's EFM1 and Retro Synth: <https://support.apple.com/guide/logicpro/efm1-overview-lgsifb887970/mac>, <https://www.soundonsound.com/techniques/totally-retro>. How FM is taught: its sidebands, as many pairs as the index and one more: <https://cmtext.com/synthesis/chapter4_fm5.php>; users of a simplified FM synth still looking for what each control does to the sound (NIME 2021): <https://nime.org/proc/nime2021_83/>.
- **B157** What Ableton Live 12.4 gets right in recording, comping, consolidating, warping and quantizing audio, and how FL Studio, Logic Pro, Studio One, Bitwig and REAPER show where a recording goes, the research of 2026-11-08: every source is in [recording-audit.md](recording-audit.md), section 7, among them Live 12's manual, Recording New Clips: <https://www.ableton.com/en/live-manual/12/recording-new-clips/>, Comping: <https://www.ableton.com/en/live-manual/12/comping/>, Audio Clips, Tempo, and Warping: <https://www.ableton.com/en/live-manual/12/audio-clips-tempo-and-warping/>, Bounce to Audio: <https://www.ableton.com/en/live-manual/12/bounce-to-audio/>, and its release notes: <https://www.ableton.com/en/release-notes/live-12/>
- **B158** Real instruments in DAWs and the free recordings of them, the research of 2026-11-08, for the Multisampler: what Logic Pro's Session Players play (drums, bass and keys, and synths since Logic Pro 12): <https://www.soundonsound.com/techniques/logic-pro-synth-players>; Fender Studio Pro 8's instruments, Presence XT and its amp and pedal models: <https://www.macworld.com/article/3037910/fender-studio-pro-daw-review-powerful-updated-rebranded.html>, <https://en.audiofanzine.com/general-sequencer/fender/studio-pro-8/editorial/reviews/test-fender-studio-pro-8.html>; the SFZ format's reference: <https://sfzformat.com/>; SFZ players' code: sfizz (BSD-2-Clause, archived on GitHub, its last change on 2025-03-17): <https://github.com/sfztools/sfizz>, and liquidsfz (MPL 2.0): <https://github.com/swesterfeld/liquidsfz>; the SFZ Instruments catalogue, with each instrument's licence (pianos: Scarypiano CC0, Splendid Grand Piano public domain, Salamander Grand Piano CC-BY-3.0; guitars: Karoryfer's Emilyguitar and Shinyguitar CC0, the guitars with many articulations under licences of their own or sold): <https://sfzinstruments.github.io/pianos>, <https://sfzinstruments.github.io/guitars>, <https://github.com/sfzinstruments/sfzinstruments.github.io/blob/HEAD/data/sfz/instruments.yml>, and its repositories, Karoryfer's under CC0 (basses, a cello, saxophones, guitars, drums, and Osiris Piano with Versilian Studios): <https://github.com/sfzinstruments>; the Versilian Community Sample Library (CC0; its grand and upright pianos and harpsichords among its Chordophones): <https://github.com/sgossner/VCSL>; and VSCO 2 Community Edition (CC0; strings, brass, woodwinds, keys and percussion): <https://github.com/sgossner/VSCO-2-CE>
